<meta>This page contains the front cover of the manuscript. As there is no written text on the cover itself, the following description translates the visual elements and heraldry into a descriptive narrative for the reader.</meta>

# Front Cover of the Manuscript

-> [An illustration of the front cover] <-

This volume is bound in high-quality **red morocco** <term>morocco leather</term> <note>A fine-grained, soft leather made from goatskin, highly prized by 17th-century book collectors for its durability and its ability to take vibrant dyes.</note>. The exterior is decorated with a **gold-tooled** triple-border of thin lines, known as fillets, with small floral stamps at each of the four corners.

At the very center of the cover is an ornate, gold-stamped **armorial centerpiece** <note>A heraldic design indicating ownership of the book.</note>. This emblem consists of:

*   An oval wreath made of **laurel branches**.
*   A central shield featuring a **vertical serpent**. This is a punning reference—often called <term>canting arms</term>—to the owner’s name, as the Latin word for serpent is *coluber* <note>original: "coluber"</note>.
*   A **marquis’s coronet** resting atop the shield, indicating the owner's noble rank.

These are the arms of **Jean-Baptiste Colbert** <note>Colbert (1619–1683) was the powerful Minister of Finances under King Louis XIV of France and one of the most famous bibliophiles in history; his library was one of the greatest private collections of the 17th century.</note>. 

The spine of the book, visible at the left, features **raised bands** with gilded compartments. Each section between the bands is decorated with small, gold-stamped floral ornaments.

<summary>This page shows the 17th-century red morocco leather binding of the manuscript, featuring the gold-stamped coat of arms of the famous French statesman and collector Jean-Baptiste Colbert.</summary>

<keywords>Jean-Baptiste Colbert, red morocco, heraldry, bookbinding, 17th century, coat of arms, serpent, gold-tooling</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the epigraph or dedication page following the title page of Ole Borch's 1668 work on the history of chemistry. The "ghosting" or "bleed-through" mentioned in the transcription is a common phenomenon in 17th-century books, where the heavy ink from the title page has left a mirrored impression on this leaf over the centuries.</meta>

->Seneca, Moral Letters, 80.<-

->*It is the mark of a lazy mind to be content with what*<-
->*has already been discovered by others.*<- <note>original: "Pigri est ingenii contentum esse his, quæ ab aliis inventa sunt." Borch chooses this quote from the Roman Stoic philosopher Seneca the Younger to justify his scholarly project. It suggests that a true scientist or historian should not merely accept existing knowledge but should actively investigate the origins and advancements of their field.</note>

<summary>This page features a motivational epigraph from the Roman philosopher Seneca, framing the author's historical investigation into chemistry as an essential pursuit for an active and inquisitive mind.</summary>
<keywords>Seneca, Epistulae Morales, philosophy of science, discovery, intellectual curiosity, Ole Borch</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>Continuing from the epigraph of Seneca on the previous leaf, this page begins the formal dedication of Ole Borch's work to his royal patron, King Frederick III. The author adopts the highly formal, humble tone expected in 17th-century courtly addresses, presenting his research as an act of duty.</meta>

(†) 2
<meta>catchword: aucto- (The text breaks here to be continued on the following page; this likely begins the word "auctoritatem," meaning authority.)</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal typographical ornament composed of two rows of repeating fleur-de-lis or leaf-patterned scrollwork elements.</image-desc>

### AUGUST, PIOUS,
### FORTUNATE. <note>original: "Auguste, Pie, Felix." These were standard honorific titles for a Christian monarch, emphasizing his majesty, religious devotion, and the prosperity of his reign.</note>

# FREDERICK
# THE THIRD,
# MONARCH OF DENMARK AND NORWAY,

## MOST GRACIOUS LORD.

IN obedience to the commands of YOUR Sacred MAJESTY, this <term>Dissertation</term> <note>In this context, a "dissertation" refers to a formal, systematic discourse or treatise on a specific subject—here, the history and origins of chemistry.</note> of mine bows at your AUGUST knees. It is, to be sure, a slight work, and not decorated with the colors of eloquence, but I hope it will prove its worth through its own strength and sinew. <note>Borch uses the Latin anatomical metaphors "nervis" (sinews/nerves) and "lacertis" (muscles/upper arms) to suggest that while his writing may not be "flowery" or stylish, it is built on firm, robust, and rigorous evidence.</note> Nothing so much short of...

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of the treatise to King Frederick III of Denmark and Norway, framing the work as a direct response to a royal command. The author, Ole Borch, humbly presents his research, emphasizing intellectual substance over rhetorical style.</summary>
<keywords>Frederick III, Denmark, Norway, royal dedication, Ole Borch, patronage, eloquence, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's dedication to King Frederick III, where Ole Borch described his work as a modest but rigorous effort. The text continues from the previous page's final sentence: "Nothing so much short of..."</meta>

<header>DEDICATION.</header>

(†)

<meta>catchword: claims,</meta>

ancient authority, or asserted by experiments, and therefore nothing short of the dust <note>Borch uses "dust" (original: *pulverem*) as a metaphor for the "dust of the arena"—the hard work and struggle of scholarly research and laboratory practice.</note>; because the Egyptians, Greeks, and their followers—Masters of that most noble Art <note>The "most noble Art" refers to Chemistry or Alchemy, which in Borch's time were often considered parts of the same discipline.</note>—out of a certain habitual jealousy, mostly described the matter in ambiguous senses, revealing their meaning only by concealing it. For that reason, I had to venture into the crypts of ancient writers and surround their hiding places with a kind of hunter's net <note>original: "indagine." This is a vivid metaphor comparing the search for hidden truth in obscure texts to a hunt where nets are used to encircle and capture wild animals.</note>, lest the wild beasts hidden there might escape me unawares. Hence, if in this work [following the rigid tribunal of the most learned <term>CONRING</term> <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681) was a famous German polymath and physician who had recently published a scathing critique of the historical origins of chemistry, challenging its alleged ancient Hermetic roots.</note> erected against <term>HERMES</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary figure to whom the origins of alchemy were traditionally attributed. Borch is writing to defend Hermes's historical legacy against Conring's attacks.</note> and his School], I fail to fully satisfy expectations regarding a nearly desperate argument, the [claims] <note>The Latin word *vindiciæ* (claims/defenses) is completed by the catchword from the bottom of the page.</note> produced in haste and amidst many occupations will excuse the fault...

<summary>Ole Borch explains the difficulty of researching the history of chemistry due to the intentionally obscure and "jealous" writing style of ancient masters. He frames his work as a defense of the Hermetic tradition against the harsh intellectual "tribunal" of the scholar Hermann Conring.</summary>

<keywords>Ole Borch, alchemy history, Hermeticism, Hermann Conring, Hermes Trismegistus, ancient Greek science, Egyptian science, scholarly methodology</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's final word "vindiciæ" (claims/defenses). Borch is justifying why he has undertaken such a difficult task despite the obscurity of ancient alchemical texts and the criticism of contemporary scholars like Conring.</meta>

# DEDICATION.

(†) 3

<meta>catchword: to me,</meta>

claims, will clear away the almost inescapable
difficulties of a matter so hidden and removed
from the common people; the very sparse and
quiet speech of the most ancient writers will clear them.
But none of these things ought to have
deterred me, as the majesty of the subject
invited the effort, and the trophies of Miltiades
disturbed my slumber <note>original: *Miltiadis trophæis ſomnos interpellantibus*. This is a classical allusion to Themistocles, who famously said he could not sleep because he was so inspired by the military victories of the Greek general Miltiades. Borch uses it to describe his own restless drive for scholarly achievement.</note>.
But what Miltiades? What trophies?
The Muses also have, O **AUGUSTUS** <note>original: *AUGUSTE*. Borch addresses King Frederick III with this imperial title, comparing him to the Roman emperors to signify his supreme status.</note>,
trophies of their own kind; they also have their own
Leaders, in whose footsteps they may follow.
I set before myself the praises of **PETER SEVERINUS** <note>Peder Sørensen (1542–1602), or Petrus Severinus, was a renowned Danish physician and an influential proponent of Paracelsian medicine. His work *Idea Medicinae Philosophicae* was a cornerstone for chemical medicine in the 17th century.</note>,
my countryman, and formerly Chief Physician to
**FREDERICK THE SECOND**, the most glorious
King of the Danes, won in the field of **CHEMISTRY**;
since his life—by some admirable temperament,
I know not whether of the stars or of fate <note>During this era, it was common to believe that a person's character and destiny were shaped by the alignment of the planets (astrological temperament) or divine providence.</note>—
so accords with my own, I must strive...

<summary>Borch describes his intellectual inspiration, comparing his academic drive to the ambition of Greek heroes and identifying the famous Danish physician Peter Severinus as his primary role model.</summary>

<keywords>Petrus Severinus, Frederick II, Denmark, Chemistry, Miltiades, scholarly ambition, Paracelsian medicine</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page: Borch is comparing his own life and career to that of his predecessor, Peder Sørensen (Petrus Severinus), suggesting that he feels a duty to uphold the legacy of Danish scholarship. The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing how "the stars or fate [had tailored Sørensen's life]..."</meta>

# DEDICATION.

<meta>catchword: to me,</meta>

to me, so that if I do not enter more deeply into the secrets of Nature <note>original: *arcana Naturæ*</note>, I might at least do so in a way better suited to human understanding; I must strive so that the laurel crown of fame which he gathered across all of Europe, I do not wear away through my own fault, even if I should fail to match him due to the dullness of my own intellect. The literary training ground of Ribe <note>original: *Ripensium palæstra*. Borch refers to the Ribe Cathedral School, one of the oldest and most prestigious schools in Denmark, which both he and Sørensen attended.</note> sent him to the University of Copenhagen <note>original: *Academiam Hafniensem*. Hafnia is the Latin name for Copenhagen.</note>; it sent me to the same. The kindness of the great King **FREDERICK II** promoted him to the public Professorship of Philology <note>The study of language and classical literature.</note> before he had set foot on foreign soil; the kindness of the great King **FREDERICK III** promoted me likewise. Later, a passion for penetrating more deeply into the Nature of things summoned him to foreign Universities and the more pleasant parts of Europe, in the same years of the previous century as the same impulse has carried me away in this century. Him

<summary>Borch outlines the striking parallels between his career and that of Peder Sørensen, highlighting their shared education at Ribe and the University of Copenhagen, as well as the royal patronage they both received.</summary>
<keywords>Peder Sørensen, Frederick II, Frederick III, Ribe Cathedral School, University of Copenhagen, Philology, Secrets of Nature, academic career</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Borch is drawing a direct parallel between the career of his predecessor, Peder Sørensen (Petrus Severinus), and his own. The previous page ended by stating that fate had prepared "Him" (Sørensen)...</meta>

# DEDICATION

<meta>catchword: AUGU-</meta>

after he had formed friendships with wise men, and after he had more deeply inspected the inner secrets of Nature <note>original: *Naturæ interiora*</note>, upon his return to his homeland, **FREDERICK II AUGUSTUS** <note>Frederick II (r. 1559–1588) was the King of Denmark and Norway, famously the patron of Tycho Brahe. "Augustus" is a Roman imperial title used here to denote royal majesty.</note> cherished him, chose him to be among his own Physicians, and supported him with Royal grace and favor until the end of his life. Since, upon his ancestral throne, the great **FREDERICK III AUGUSTUS** <note>Frederick III (r. 1648–1670) was the reigning monarch at the time Borch was writing.</note> has most mercifully honored me with a very similar grace, nothing remains to be more earnestly desired, and nothing more surely hoped for, than that the endurance of that fate of **SEVERINUS** <note>original: *fati illius SEVERINIANI*. This refers to Peder Sørensen (Petrus Severinus), the influential Danish Paracelsian physician whose career path Borch is mirroring.</note> might also continue in me until the end of my life. With this prayer, and full of hope, I conclude, and [commend] **YOUR MAJESTY**,

<summary>Borch concludes his comparison by noting that just as Frederick II supported Peder Sørensen, Frederick III has shown him similar royal favor. He expresses his hope that his own career will match the longevity and success of his distinguished predecessor.</summary>

<keywords>Frederick II, Frederick III, Peder Sørensen, royal patronage, physician, Danish history, Nature's secrets</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the concluding formal sign-off of the dedication to King Frederick III. Borch completes the sentence begun on the previous page, where he expressed his hope to mirror the successful career of Peder Sørensen under the King's patronage.</meta>

### DEDICATION.

**AUGUST [KING]** <note>original: *AUGUSTE*. This is a formal address using the Roman imperial title "Augustus," which was standard for addressing monarchs in Latin to emphasize their supreme authority.</note>, together with your entire hereditary and **AUGUST** house, I heartily commend [Your Majesty] to God and to Mother Nature.

->TO YOUR SACRED MAJESTY<-

->*most intimately devoted*<-

->Citizen<-

->OLAUS BORRICHIUS.<- <note>Olaus Borrichius (Ole Borch, 1626–1690) was a renowned Danish polymath, chemist, and physician who served as a royal physician and professor at the University of Copenhagen.</note>

<vocab>Olaus Borrichius, Dedication, Augustus, Majesty, Mother Nature</vocab>

<summary>Olaus Borrichius concludes his formal dedication by commending King Frederick III and the royal family to the protection of God and Mother Nature, signing himself as a devoted citizen.</summary>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page is a laudatory poem written by Abbot Boucaud to the author, Ole Borch (Olaus Borrichius). It follows Borch’s own dedication to King Frederick III, serving as a "recommendation" or "commendatory verse" which was common in 17th-century scholarly books to establish the author’s authority and network of influential friends.</meta>

## TO the Most Illustrious Man,
# LORD OLAUS BORRICHIUS,
## delight of Apollo and Hermes,
### the closest of Friends.

While the pen of BORCH <note>original: *BORRICHII*. Olaus Borrichius (Ole Borch) was the author of the work this poem praises.</note> calls forth the cradles of chemistry from the earliest age with its accustomed skill,
We follow through the centuries and nations: now into the lofty temples of
Memphis <note>The ancient capital of Egypt, often cited by early modern scholars as the birthplace of the "hermetic" or alchemical arts.</note>, 
now to the Greeks, and the homes of the Romans;
And among the Arabs, and the houses of the Chinese <note>original: *Serum*. The *Seres* was the Latin term for the people of Northern China, famous for silk and, in alchemical lore, ancient wisdom.</note>, and the citadels of Seville <note>original: *Hispalis*. Seville was a major center of learning in Moorish and later Spanish history.</note>,
To the Seine <note>Paris</note>, and the Thames <note>London</note>, and the German soil.
We witness the secrets penetrated by the Egyptian <note>original: *Phariis*. Referring to Pharos, the island of the lighthouse of Alexandria, standing in for Egypt.</note> Monarchs,
We witness them continued by the Greek flock,
We witness the indelible glory of the genius of Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a foundational figure in Arabic alchemy.</note>,
And the skilled hands of Lull <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and polymath to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note>, and of Roger <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1219–1292), the English friar and philosopher known for his work in empirical science.</note>.
One thing remains yet to see, and to vow eternally 
for my BORCH, as he has deserved: a twin destiny. <note>The poet suggests that Borch’s fame will equal that of the great masters he writes about.</note>

<margin>The Hague</margin> <note>original: *Hagæ Com.* Short for *Hagæ Comitum*, the Latin name for The Hague, Netherlands.</note>
->**ABBOT BOUCAUD**,<-
->Almoner and Councilor to the Most<-
->Christian King.<- <note>The "Most Christian King" (Rex Christianissimus) was the traditional title for the King of France.</note>

<sig>(††)</sig>
<meta>catchword: To</meta>

<summary>A commendatory poem by Abbot Boucaud praising Ole Borch's comprehensive history of alchemy, tracing the art from ancient Egypt and China through the Middle Ages to the present.</summary>
<keywords>Olaus Borrichius, Abbot Boucaud, history of chemistry, Geber, Ramon Llull, Roger Bacon, Memphis, alchemy, 17th-century poetry</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of laudatory poems prefacing Olaus Borrichius’s work. The previous page featured a poem by Abbot Boucaud tracing the geographic history of chemistry; this page presents a shorter, punchy epigram focusing on Borrichius’s role in restoring the dignity of the "Spagyric" art against its detractors.</meta>

<header>❦(0)❦</header>

->To the<-
## MAN
### Most Experienced and Celebrated,
## LORD OLAUS BORRICHIUS,
### DOCTOR OF MEDICINE,
->Royal Physician and Public Professor at Copenhagen,<-
->commenting on the Origin and Progress of Chemistry.<-

---

> *Until now, chemical matters have been torn apart by ignorant*
> ->*tongues,*<-
> ->*as if they were vain, and as if they were the work of Muhammad’s*<-
> ->*age:* <note>The poet refers to the common 17th-century prejudice that dismissed alchemy as either a fraud or a purely "Saracen" (Islamic) invention of the Middle Ages, lacking ancient authority.</note><-
> *You, BORRICHIUS, full of the Divine, carve out the beginnings from* 
> *deeper down, and you thunder forth the age of the Deluge. <note>Borrichius argued that chemistry was a sacred art dating back to before the Great Flood.</note>*
> *You more fully untangle the benefits of the Spagyric <term>Spagyric: A term coined by Paracelsus referring to the alchemical process of separating and then recombining elements to create potent medicines.</term> study,*
> *which are known well enough and more to the choir of Machaon; <note>original: *Machaonio... choro*. Machaon was the son of Asclepius and a legendary healer in the Iliad; the "choir" refers to the community of physicians.</note>*
> *Things sweet to Mars when he rages with thundering powder, <note>A reference to the use of chemical knowledge in creating gunpowder for warfare.</note>*
> *and to be admired in the metal-bearing schools.*
> *I speak of small things: Nature herself submits to that art;*
> *She is to be seen naked in this way, or in no way at all.*

<sig>(††)</sig>
<meta>catchword: Macte</meta>

<summary>A commendatory poem praising Olaus Borrichius for defending chemistry against its critics by proving its ancient, pre-biblical origins and its vital utility in medicine, warfare, and metallurgy.</summary>
<keywords>Olaus Borrichius, Ole Borch, Spagyric art, Machaon, alchemy, history of chemistry, gunpowder, Nature, medicine</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the celebratory front matter of Olaus Borrichius’s treatise. Following the previous poem that contextualized chemistry’s ancient origins, this page features a shorter, more personal tribute from a fellow physician in Hamburg, Christian Langermann. He praises Borrichius not just as a chemist, but as a "Renaissance man" whose skills span across poetry, botany, medicine, and linguistics.</meta>

<page-num>(0)</page-num>

Success to that genius of yours, O light now devoted to the North, <note>original: *Trioni*. Literally the "plow-oxen" constellation (the Big Dipper), used here to represent the Northern regions, specifically Denmark.</note>
    a light long sought by other suitors.
It is not enough for you to illuminate the Latin world with your critical mind;
    nor enough to have earned a place in the heavens with your poems;
It is not enough to search for the Divine within the human anatomy;
    nor enough to have set foot in the kingdoms of Flora <note>original: *Floræ*. The Roman goddess of flowers, here representing the study of botany.</note>;
It is not enough to have so often restored the sick, and bodies near death's ferry <note>original: *cymbæ*. A reference to Charon’s boat which transports souls to the underworld.</note>,
    with the healing hand of Apollo <note>original: *Phœbeâ... manu*. Phoebus Apollo was the Greek and Roman god of medicine and healing.</note>;
It is not enough to bring honor to your post <note>original: *Spartam exornare*. A classical idiom meaning "you have found your Sparta, now adorn it," or to do credit to the position and country one serves.</note> in the famous Academy,
    whether it is time for public oratory or the joy of teaching;
It is not enough to daily challenge the Goddess Nature to reveal new things
    through the craftsman's interpreting flame <note>A poetic description of the chemist's furnace and the experimental process.</note>.
In addition to these, you hasten to write, and it is sweet to have rescued 
    Chemistry from the clutches of the common rabble
    with your powerful pen:
Continue to defend your Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of the "Hermetic" or chemical arts, whom Borrichius sought to prove was a historical figure.</note> with your writings, and 
    Hermes himself will finally be grateful in his own person.

->CHRISTIAN LANGERMANN<-
->Doctor of Philosophy and Medicine, Hamburg.<-

At Hamburg.

<summary>A laudatory poem by the Hamburg physician Christian Langermann celebrating Olaus Borrichius’s wide-ranging expertise in medicine, botany, and the arts before specifically praising his defense of chemistry.</summary>
<keywords>Olaus Borrichius, Christian Langermann, Hermes Trismegistus, history of medicine, botany, alchemy, Copenhagen, 17th-century scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the celebratory front matter of Olaus Borrichius’s treatise. The previous page ended mid-sentence in a poem by Christian Langermann. This page begins a new dedication poem—likely by a different contributor, though the author is not named on this specific leaf—praising Borrichius for defending the dignity and antiquity of chemistry against those who dismiss it as a mere medieval or "Saracen" invention.</meta>

<page-num>(o)</page-num>
<sig>(††)</sig>

### To
# THE MAN
## Most Experienced and Most Celebrated,
# LORD OLAUS BORRICHIUS,
## DOCTOR OF MEDICINE,
### Royal Physician and Public Professor at Copenhagen,
### commenting on the Origin and Progress of Chemistry.

*Hitherto chemical things are torn apart by ignorant*
->*tongues,*<-
*as if they were vanities, and as if the work*
->*of the age of Muhammad:*<- <note>original: *Muhametis*. A reference to the then-common scholarly debate over whether chemistry (alchemy) was a relatively recent Arabic invention or a much older, "sacred" science. Critics often used the "Muhammadan" label to dismiss the art as fraudulent or pagan.</note>
*But you, Borrichius, filled with divine inspiration, carve out its beginnings from a*
*deeper source, and proclaim the age before the Flood.* <note>Borrichius was famous for arguing that chemistry was not a medieval invention but an ancient wisdom dating back to the biblical patriarchs and the Egyptian Hermes Trismegistus.</note>
*You unravel more fully the benefits of the Spagyric art,* <term>Spagyria: A term used by Paracelsus and his followers for chemistry, derived from Greek words meaning "to separate and to combine," specifically regarding the preparation of medicines.</term>
*which are known well enough—and more than enough—to the guild of physicians;* <note>original: *Machaonio... choro*. Machaon was the son of Asclepius and the legendary physician to the Greeks at Troy; his "choir" or "chorus" refers to the community of medical doctors.</note>
*they are a delight to Mars when he rages with thunderous powder,* <note>original: *fulmineo... pulvere*. A poetic reference to gunpowder, a product of chemical knowledge used in warfare (the domain of the god Mars).</note>
*and are to be admired in the schools of metallurgy.*
*But I speak of small things: Nature herself submits to that art;*
*either by this means, or by no other way, can she be seen naked.* <note>The poet argues that chemistry is the only tool capable of stripping away the "veils" of matter to reveal the true essence of Nature.</note>

<meta>catchword: Macte</meta>

<summary>A laudatory poem dedicated to Olaus Borrichius, defending chemistry as a noble and ancient science that predates the Islamic era and serves as the essential key to understanding the secrets of Nature.</summary>
<keywords>Olaus Borrichius, Spagyric art, alchemy, Machaon, history of chemistry, metallurgy, Copenhagen, Danish science</keywords>

<meta>This page contains a celebratory poem by Christian Langermann of Hamburg. While the previous page's translation ended mid-sentence discussing the "Machaonian chorus" (the guild of physicians), this page begins a fresh poem in elegiac couplets. This suggests that the previous poem may have concluded on the bottom of the preceding page or that there is a slight gap in the transcription provided. This text continues the theme of praising Olaus Borrichius’s multi-faceted brilliance, transitioning from his medical and botanical skill to his defense of chemistry.</meta>

<page-num>(o)</page-num>
<sig>(††)</sig>

Increase in that genius of yours, O light now devoted to the North, <note>original: *Trioni*. Literally referring to the 'Septentrio' or the seven stars of the Great Bear constellation; a common poetic way to refer to the North, specifically Denmark in this context.</note>
A light long sought by other suitors.
It is not enough for you to illuminate the Latin world <note>original: *Latium*. Referring here to the Latin language and Roman literature, in which Borrichius was a renowned expert.</note> with your critical mind;
It is not enough to have earned the heavens with your poems;
It is not enough to search for the Divine within human anatomy;
It is not enough to have set foot in the realms of Flora; <note>original: *Floræ*. Flora was the Roman goddess of flowers; her "realms" here signify the study of botany.</note>
It is not enough to have restored the sick—and bodies so close to the ferryman's boat— <note>original: *cymbæ*. Literally "the skiff," referring to the boat of Charon that carries the souls of the dead across the River Styx.</note>
So many times with the hand of Apollo; <note>original: *Phœbéâ*. Referring to Phoebus Apollo, the god of healing and medicine.</note>
It is not enough to bring honor to your station in the famous Lyceum, <note>original: *Spartam exornare*. A classical idiom meaning "You have found your Sparta, now adorn it," or "Do your duty in the place where you are stationed." The "Lyceum" refers to the university or place of higher learning.</note>
Whether it is fitting to speak or pleasing to teach;
It is not enough to daily challenge the Goddess Nature to new things
Through the craftsman’s interpreting flame. <note>A reference to the alchemical or chemical furnace, where fire acts as the "interpreter" that reveals the hidden properties of matter.</note>
Now you hasten to write on these matters, and it is sweet
To have defended Chemistry from the clutches of the common rabble
With a robust pen:
Continue to defend your Hermes in your writings, and Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy (the Hermetic art). Langermann suggests that by defending the antiquity of the science, Borrichius is vindicating Hermes himself.</note>
Himself will at last be grateful in his own person.

---

<margin>At Hamburg.</margin>

->**CHRISTIAN LANGERMANN**<-
->Doctor of Philosophy and Medicine at Hamburg.<-

<summary>A laudatory poem by the Hamburg physician Christian Langermann praising Olaus Borrichius for his expertise in philology, poetry, anatomy, botany, and medicine, before specifically celebrating his defense of "Hermetic" chemistry against its detractors.</summary>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of congratulatory poems dedicated to Olaus Borrichius (Ole Borch). While the previous page praised his skill in medicine, poetry, and botany, this poem focuses specifically on his work in "Hermetic" or chemical arts, framing his scholarly contributions as a revival of ancient Egyptian wisdom for the benefit of the North.</meta>

<page-num>(6)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Regi-</meta>

Through the occult arts of Ham <note>original: *Chami*. Ham, the son of Noah, was traditionally identified by early modern scholars as the founder of Egypt (Khem) and the originator of the "chem-ical" arts.</note>, and the secrets of ancient Canopus <note>original: *Canopi*. An ancient Egyptian coastal city, here used as a metaphor for Egyptian mystical knowledge.</note>,
A new light and a new life returns.
We marvel less at the altars of Greek Osiris,
And marvel less at your mysteries, O Isis.
Those sacred things of Memphis <note>original: *Memphitica*. Referring to Memphis, the ancient capital of Egypt, legendary for its priestly wisdom.</note> which the care of priests once concealed,
We now perceive to be read more clearly in the learned world.
The shrines of Hermes <note>original: *Hermetis*. Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy and "Hermetic" philosophy.</note> lie open, as do the Lemnian temples <note>original: *Lemnia fana*. Lemnos was the island of Vulcan, the god of fire and smithing; this refers to the mastery of fire in chemistry.</note>,
And the sacred dogmas of the school of Pelusium <note>original: *Pelusiacæ*. Another reference to Egyptian scholarly tradition.</note>.
Nature could not be confined within Egyptian cages <note>original: *Phariis catastis*. Literally "Pharian scaffolds/stages." Pharos refers to the lighthouse of Alexandria; the poet suggests that natural secrets cannot be locked away in one country.</note>,
She was meant to be cherished in many lands:
Memphis by Lake Mareotis was not enough for these secrets,
Nor were one or two initiates sufficient for them.
Let **BORRICHIUS** proclaim the rest with his eloquent mouth:
Denmark <note>original: *Cimbria*. The Cimbrian Peninsula, the poetic name for Denmark and the Jutland region.</note> rejoices and exults in her own **BORRICHIUS**.

->**AURELIUS G. BENZONIUS.**<-

<summary>A celebratory poem by Aurelius G. Benzonius praising Ole Borch for revealing the "occult" secrets of Egyptian alchemy and Hermetic philosophy to the modern world, specifically bringing honor to Denmark.</summary>
<keywords>Ole Borch (Borrichius), alchemy, Hermeticism, Egyptology, Hermes Trismegistus, Denmark (Cimbria), history of science</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the formal lecture or preface by Ole Borch (Borrichius). It follows the preceding congratulatory poems, picking up from the catchword "Regi-" to introduce "Reginam Artium Chemiam" (Chemistry, the Queen of Arts). Borch is positioning chemistry not as a fringe pursuit, but as a royal science essential to the advancement of medicine and supported by the Danish Crown.</meta>

<meta>catchword: excessive</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative headpiece featuring two rows of mirrored typographic fleurons or scrollwork ornaments.</image-desc>

<image-desc>Large ornamental initial 'R' decorated with intricate vine and floral motifs within a square border.</image-desc> I am commanded, my Listeners, to touch upon <term>Chemistry</term> <note>original: *Chemiam*. In the 17th century, the terms "chemistry" and "alchemy" were often used interchangeably to describe the study of matter and its transformations.</note>, the Queen of Arts—an art long superior to the censures of the common crowd, and one that has always been held in high esteem in the minds of Kings, Princes, and every most awakened intellect. It is that most illustrious monument of Divine wisdom and power within created things, and a true shortcut to a wise and solid way of philosophizing. I am called to contribute my own small token, however thin it may be, alongside other Consultants of Nature, so that <term>Medicine</term> <note>original: *Medicina*. Here referring to the art of healing as a whole.</note>, that preserver of humanity, may be gradually advanced to a greater and more venerable height beyond the customary pillars of pharmaceutical instructions. Such is the will of the Sacred ROYAL MAJESTY <note>Refers to King Frederik III of Denmark, a patron of the sciences and Borch’s employer.</note>, lest anything that might benefit future ages, or anything suitable for procuring human health, should remain unknown for long (so far as the gloom weighing upon the human mind permits). Nor should <term>Copenhagen</term> <note>original: *Hafnia*.</note> hear that, by Royal command, <margin>Who today handles chemical matters more accurately?</margin> **Boyle** <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691), the famous Anglo-Irish natural philosopher often called the "father of modern chemistry."</note> attempts such things in **London**, **Gobelin** in **Paris**, **Talducci** in **Florence**, **Vulpiano** in **Rome**, **Ludovico de Comitibus** in **Venice**, **Beza** in **Strasbourg**, **Michaelis** in **Leipzig**, **Rolfinck** in **Jena**, **Rapp** in **Mainz**, **Fauss** in **Heidelberg**, and others elsewhere, while she herself meanwhile turns her eyes too sluggishly toward the contemplation of Nature's light. Furthermore, although this most noble of Arts, being hedged about by immense difficulties, may terrify and not infrequently cast down those who approach it, that should not be interpreted as the fault of Chemistry, but rather the result of excessive...

<summary>Ole Borch introduces chemistry as the "Queen of Arts," asserting its importance for medicine and divine wisdom while noting that the Danish King has commissioned its study so Copenhagen can keep pace with the great scientific centers of Europe.</summary>
<keywords>Chemistry, Medicine, Robert Boyle, Copenhagen, Royal Patronage, Natural Philosophy, European Universities</keywords>

To the hidden arts of Ham <note>original: *Chami*. In early modern scholarship, Ham (son of Noah) was often identified as the founder of Egypt and the first practitioner of the "Hermetic" or chemical arts.</note> and of old Canopus <note>An ancient Egyptian coastal city famous for its temple of Serapis and mystical traditions.</note>,

We marvel less at the altars of the Greek Osiris;
->**We marvel less at your mysteries, Isis.**<-
The sacred Memphis rites that the care of priests once concealed,
->**We now perceive are read more clearly in the learned world.**<-
The shrines of Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary "Thrice-Great" figure to whom the origins of alchemy and philosophy were attributed.</note> stand open, as do the Lemnian temples,
->**And the sacred dogmas of the Pelusian school.**<-
Nature could not be confined to Egyptian scaffolds;
->**She deserved to be honored in many more lands.**<-
Mareotic Memphis <note>Refers to the region near Lake Mareotis in Egypt, associated with Alexandrian scholarship.</note> was not enough for these secrets,
->**Nor were one or two initiates sufficient for them.**<-

->**Cimbria <note>A poetic name for Denmark, specifically the Jutland peninsula.</note> rejoices and triumphs in her own Borrichius.**<-

->**Aurelius G. Benzovius.**<-

<page-num>1</page-num>
<header>(0) 1</header>
<sig>A</sig>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut border consisting of stylized foliate and floral patterns spans the width of the page. Below it, a large ornamental drop cap 'R' is framed in a square woodcut featuring intricate vine and leaf motifs.</image-desc>

<margin>Who today may handle chemical matters more accurately?</margin>
**R**eginam Artium <note>original: *Reginam Artium Chemiam*—The Queen of Arts, Chemistry.</note> Chemistry, the Queen of Arts, long superior to the censures of the common crowd, and always held in high esteem in the minds of Kings, Princes, and every most awakened intellect—that most illustrious monument of Divine wisdom and power in created things—I am commanded, Listeners <note>original: *Audit.*, an abbreviation for *Auditores*, common in academic orations delivered to an audience.</note>, to touch upon. I am tasked to approach this true shortcut to wise and solid philosophizing, and to contribute my own small token, however thin, alongside other Consultants of Nature <note>A term for natural philosophers or scientists.</note>. My goal is that Medicine, that preserver of mankind, may be gradually advanced to a greater and more venerable height, far beyond the customary pillars of pharmaceutical instructions. Such is the will of his Sacred ROYAL MAJESTY, lest anything beneficial to future ages, or anything suitable for securing human health, should long remain unknown—at least as far as the darkness pressing upon the human mind permits. Copenhagen <note>original: *Hafnia*</note> must not hear that others are attempting similar great works by Royal command—Boyle in London, Gobelin in Paris, Talducci in Florence, Vulpian in Rome, Ludovico de Comitibus in Venice, Beza in Strasbourg <note>original: *Argentoratum*</note>, Michaelis in Leipzig, Rolfinck <note>Werner Rolfinck (1599–1673), a famous German physician and chemist.</note> in Jena, Rapp in Mainz <note>original: *Moguntia*</note>, Fauss in Heidelberg, and others elsewhere—while she herself meanwhile turns her eyes too sluggishly toward contemplating the light of Nature. Although the most noble of Arts, hemmed in by immense difficulties, may terrify those who approach it and not infrequently cause them to stumble, that must not be interpreted as a crime of Chemistry, but...

<summary>Ole Borch introduces chemistry as the "Queen of Arts" and a divine monument, arguing for its advancement in Denmark to keep pace with the scientific progress happening across Europe's major cities.</summary>
<keywords>Chemistry as Queen of Arts, Royal patronage, Robert Boyle, Werner Rolfinck, European scientific centers, Copenhagen, medical advancement</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory oration of Ole Borch (Borrichius). The previous page ended with "Bo...", likely referring to the author himself or a royal command. The text here opens mid-sentence, concluding a thought about the dangers of excessive boldness in the pursuit of chemical knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>ON THE ORIGIN AND</header>

<margin>Why does Chemistry not answer the prayers of all?</margin>
...too much for those who are daring, but immoderate for those who strive. Chemistry, Listeners <note>original: *Audit.*, abbreviation for *Auditores*. Borch is addressing an audience at the University of Copenhagen.</note>, is like Penelope <note>The faithful wife of Odysseus in Greek mythology, who was besieged by many unworthy suitors while her husband was away. Here, Chemistry is the "bride" and the chemists are the "suitors."</note>; her marriage is sought not only by the prudent, but often by the stupid, the ignorant, the drunkards, and the ill-advised. Into their laps Astraea <note>The virgin goddess of Justice and purity.</note>—the most beautiful of the Goddesses—would never recline with her full dowry. If Chemistry has frustrated anyone’s hope, let him know that she hates those with immoderate expectations; let him know that Eleusis <note>The site of the ancient Eleusinian Mysteries; Borch suggests Chemistry is a sacred mystery that requires initiation.</note> does not reveal its secrets to everyone; let him know it was fatal for Clodius <note>A Roman politician who famously disguised himself as a woman to sneak into the women-only festival of the *Bona Dea* (Good Goddess) in 62 BCE. He was later tried for sacrilege.</note> to have even crept stealthily into the rites of the Good Goddess. Chemistry opens a most ample and charming theater, decorated with every kind of animal, herb, tree, mineral, and metal. In that hall, she commands her lovers, whoever they may be, to exert themselves: to extract, evaluate, temper, and put to use now the richness of oils, now the subtlety of spirits, and now the sharpness of salts <note>In 17th-century chemistry, "Spirits, Oils, and Salts" were the three primary principles or substances extracted from matter via distillation.</note>. But if she has a more private inner shrine <note>original: *lararium*, a household chapel for the Roman Lares (gods).</note>, penetrating there is granted only to those born of the Gods; for the profane to have rushed in has always been, and will be, damaging. 

By your faith, Listeners: if some traveler, admitted by favor into the Hall of a great Prince, should behold here the most select of tapestries ingeniously woven with beasts, there paintings by Apelles <note>The most famous painter of ancient Greece, used here as a symbol of perfection in art.</note> competing with Nature herself, in another place a most choice collection of marbles, statues, and craftsmanship, and elsewhere whatever rare thing flies in the air, whatever unusual thing swims in the waters, and whatever admirable thing sparkles underground—if only the door of a single study <note>original: *tablini*, a room in a Roman house for keeping records and family memories.</note> were kept closed (the one which would lead to the contemplation of the Assyrian Phoenix <note>A mythical bird that regenerates from its own ashes; in alchemy, it often symbolizes the final stage of the Great Work or the Philosopher's Stone.</note>), would you judge him an ungrateful guest if he became angry for that reason and descended into insults? Yet we must consider how much more ungrateful are the many suitors of this Penelope, who are turned away by the imaginary trouble of frequent and profound meditations, and by the necessary patience of labors...

<meta>catchword: tiâ (the end of *patientiâ*, meaning patience/endurance)</meta>

<summary>Borch argues that Chemistry is a divine and selective art that rejects unworthy, "profane" practitioners. He compares the pursuit of chemical secrets to being a guest in a royal palace where one should be grateful for the wonders revealed rather than angry at the few secrets still hidden.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Penelope, Astraea, chemical principles (oils/salts/spirits), Assyrian Phoenix, chemical philosophy, initiation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory oration of Ole Borch (Borrichius) concerning the progress of chemistry. The previous page ended with "put...", and the sentence completes here with "potential" or "power," referring to those exhausted by their own efforts.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>ON THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.</header>

...wearied by their own power <note>original: *potentia*. Borch is describing failed seekers of knowledge.</note>, and hardly worthy to be esteemed even as the handmaidens of Penelope <note>A reference to the disloyal servants in Homer's *Odyssey* who consorted with the suitors; Borch suggests these failed chemists cannot even reach the lowest level of the "household" of science.</note>, they prematurely aim for a path to the Mistress herself. Nor are they content to have inspected the inner parts of herbs, trees, animals, and minerals; they are indignant that they are not immediately admitted into the innermost sanctuaries—places which are, by a fatal law, inaccessible to most mortals. When fortune frustrates their expectations, they turn their pens to the disgrace of the art, casting the blame upon Nature, the best of Mothers—a blame which they actually owe either to their own fate or to their ill-advised labor. Indeed, that most indulgent Parent [Nature] groans when she so often sees Chemistry, the firstborn of her daughters—who was bestowed upon the earth to the injury of none, but for the health of all—spoken of as a reef of patrimonies <note>original: *scopulum patrimoniorum*. A "reef" or shipwreck of inheritance; a common 17th-century complaint that alchemy caused families to go bankrupt.</note> and beaten by the insults of every most ignorant person. But she comforts herself again with the consciousness of having deserved well; she advises those striving for excellence that these delights must be "digested" <note>original: *concoqvendas*. A chemical term for slow heating, here used metaphorically for mental processing.</note>. For nothing pertains more to the glory of an excellent Art than to be despised and barked at by the crowd. Surely the Moon does not refute such clucking noises <note>original: *poppyismos*. Derived from a Greek term for clicking or smacking sounds used to mock or to call animals.</note> in any other way than by shining, nor does Jupiter cease from his benefits, even though he never pleases everyone at once. On this matter, the genius of Nature, Hippocrates <note>The famous Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine."</note>, says in *On the Art*: *There are those, he says, who think it an art to dishonor the other arts by basely attacking them; but they do not achieve what they think they do, but rather ambitiously display their own ignorance. To me, however, it seems the desire and the work of science to discover something of those things which have not yet been found, or to bring those half-finished to a conclusion.*

<margin>What is the purpose of Chemistry?</margin>
This is the purpose of Chemistry, Listeners: to advance natural things toward more certain and evident results, so that Philosophy may no longer be left hanging <note>i.e., remain purely speculative without physical proof.</note>, and so that Medicine may not always remain stuck in the same footprint, but may step forward and be handed down to posterity more muscular and robust, to be further advanced by them in turn. If anyone [expects] greater things from Chemistry...

<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: sibi (to himself)</meta>

<summary>Ole Borch defends chemistry against its critics and failed practitioners, arguing that the true purpose of the art is to provide a physical foundation for philosophy and medicine. He uses classical metaphors to describe how the "mother" Nature endures the insults of the ignorant while her true students work toward uncovering hidden truths.</summary>
<keywords>Ole Borch, philosophy of science, defense of chemistry, Hippocrates, Nature as Mother, scientific method, medical progress</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 3, which warned of those who prematurely seek the highest secrets of nature. It transitions into a defense of "moderate chemistry" and its practical medical applications.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>ON THE ORIGIN AND</header>

...let him promise it to himself, let him do so at his own risk, and whether his Gods are angry or kind, let him enter a sea that has shipwrecked many. Meanwhile, we shall stand at the threshold of the Goddess, and leaving her sublime Mastery to those more fortunate and elevated spirits, we will consider in a few words what moderate Chemistry <term>Chemia</term> promises to the attentive, the industrious, and those eager for labor. How much protection there is for Medicine in her is clear from the fact that, were Chemistry to cease, no one could produce distilled water—so necessary a vehicle for medicines—nor noble oil for balms, nor that salt of plants, the conqueror of so many fevers, nor those most vigorous spirits for strengthening the heart. "But surely," they say, "the medicines of the Chemists are poisonous, dangerous, drastic, violent, and reek with an unpleasant stench, and therefore must be utterly rejected." Yet the Chemical fire, *it is said*, easily transforms and corrects poison if any be present; it wards off danger, blunts violence, refines the <term>empyreuma</term> <note>original: *empyreuma*. The "burnt" or "smoky" taste and smell that often resulted from early distillation techniques.</note>, and adds strength, grace, flavor, and efficacy. Therefore, in the hand of a wise man, chemical medicines are free from all danger; in the hand of a fool, even Galenic <note>original: *Galenica*. Referring to the traditional herbal medicine based on the theories of the ancient Greek physician Galen, which chemists of this era often criticized as outdated.</note> remedies are so many swords in the hands of the mad. If, then, the head aches, Chemistry will help—guided by a careful method of healing—with the most effective tincture of the Moon <note>original: *Lunæ tinctura*. In alchemy, "Moon" refers to silver. Silver-based preparations were traditionally used for brain and nervous disorders.</note>; if a faintness creeps upon the heart, the drinkable liquid of the Sun <note>original: *solis liqvor*. "Sun" refers to gold. *Aurum potabile* (drinkable gold) was a legendary and expensive tonic for the heart.</note> will heal it; if stubborn clumps of flowing humors have blocked the liver, spleen, pancreas, or mesentery, nothing is more effective or less dangerous than an extract of Mars <note>original: *Martis extracto*. "Mars" refers to iron, used to treat "obstructions" or anemia.</note>; if an obstruction attacks the kidneys or bladder, it is cleared by the Spirit of Venus <note>original: *Spiritu Veneris*. "Venus" refers to copper.</note>; if the Neapolitan disease <note>original: *Neapolitana lues*. A common 17th-century name for syphilis.</note> has besieged the whole body and grown strong through delay, the patient's health lies beyond hope unless Mercury-based vapors <note>original: *fumi Mercuriales*. Mercury was the standard treatment for syphilis, often applied through fumigation.</note> provide aid; if the womb is troubled by restlessness, it will be pacified

<meta>catchword: by the juices [succo]</meta>

<summary>Ole Borch defends chemical medicine against accusations of being poisonous, arguing that the chemist's fire purifies substances and that danger lies in the physician's ignorance rather than the medicine itself. He provides a list of planetary-linked metallic remedies for specific ailments, such as silver for the head and gold for the heart.</summary>
<keywords>iatrochemistry, medical alchemy, planetary medicine, Galenic medicine, distillation, syphilis, pharmacology, Ole Borch</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the utility of Chemistry, transitioning from general medical benefits to specific chemical compounds and their applications in surgery, cosmetics, and warfare. It maintains the defense against traditional Galenic medicine established on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.</header>
<sig>A 3</sig>

<margin>Chemistry provides noble remedies.</margin>
<margin>Paracelsus's Surgical works.</margin>
...by the juice of Jupiter <note>original: *succo Jovis*. In alchemy, Jupiter represents the metal tin.</note>; if an inflammation has raised a swelling in the outer limbs, harmless help is promised by the dissolved salt of Saturn <note>original: *Saturni sale*. Saturn represents the metal lead; lead salts were commonly used in cooling ointments.</note>. Who today restrains the burning of fevers, unless the spirit of salt <note>hydrochloric acid</note>, the spirit of Vitriol <note>sulfuric acid</note>, or the spirit of sulfur is called to his aid? Who easily halts the onset of recurring illnesses without the leached salts of plants? Who suspends fainting spells without cephalic spirits <note>medicines intended for the head and nervous system</note>? Noble, pleasant, and delicate remedies are finished, unless we plead with Chemistry: who today, except those with dulled and worn-out palates, can bear those unpleasant Hippocratic potions and those loathsome Galenic purges? As for Surgery—though all others may lash out at *Paracelsus*—she confesses she owes so much to his styptics, <term>oppodeldocs</term> <note>a type of soap-based liniment or medical plaster popularized by Paracelsus</note>, and balms, that she can never fully repay the debt. I say nothing of so many cosmetics made from talc, Mercury, and bismuth: so many ointments from distilled oils and liquids: so many Turinese "Suns-dews" <note>original: *Rores Solis*. Likely referring to Rosoglio, a cordial or liqueur often used as a restorative</note>, so many balms for embryos, and so many elixirs of life, all of which trace their very births back to the Chemical fires.

<margin>The most powerful use of Chemistry.</margin>
But it would be a small thing for Chemistry to claim dominion for itself in Medicine, unless it also commanded with a fuller power in the other arts. What governs wars today, if not that little Chemical powder made from nitre and sulfur? What casts down walls? What overturns cities through subterranean tunnels? What suspends the craftsmen’s globes of fire in the air—now starring, now forming letters, now pregnant with other smaller fires—if not the ingenuity of the Chemical powder? Hannibal would not have broken the Maritime Alps with all the vinegar of Africa [regardless of what *Livy*, *Plutarch*, *Silius*, and *Juvenal* may assert, while *Polybius* is not without reason silent on the matter], for they are shattered with a modest amount of gunpowder <note>original: *pulvere pyrio*</note>. *Archimedes* would have puzzled for a long time over that crown of Hiero's, nor would he have promised himself a certain and consistent result, as has been accurately demonstrated by *Ercker* <note>Lazarus Ercker, a 16th-century German metallurgist and author of a definitive work on ores and assaying</note>—

Without Chemistry, life is lived uncomfortably in the North.

Chemistry scrutinizes the innards of nature.

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>### ON THE ORIGIN AND PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.</header>

Indeed, these are the first and indubitable foundations of Chemistry: namely, that the bodies of plants, animals, and minerals must be "killed" and released into putrefaction <note>The author refers to the chemical process of *putrefactio*, where a substance is broken down to be purified or transformed.</note> before they allow their more noble essence <note>original: "nucleum," literally "kernel" or "nut."</note> to be extracted from them. The Spirit of God teaches in *1 Peter 1* that gold and silver are **perishable** <note>original Greek: "φθαρτὰ" (phtharta), meaning corruptible or subject to decay.</note> and subject to corruption; the silversmith will vehemently deny this, but only the **Chemist** <term>Chemicus: at this time, the term referred to those practicing what we now call alchemy and early chemistry.</term> will affirm and demonstrate it. The Spirit of God teaches in *Acts 2* and *Revelation 6* that the **Sun is to be turned into darkness and the Moon into blood**; and although we know this is to be understood strictly regarding those celestial lights, we are nonetheless filled with both delight and wonder when we note that nearly similar things happen to the **Sun** and **Moon** <note>In alchemical shorthand, the "Sun" (Sol) refers to gold and the "Moon" (Luna) refers to silver. The author is describing color changes in chemical reactions.</note> of the Chemists during their labors. And why? He who is the Father of Scripture is also the Father of Nature; therefore, it would be unjust to deny credit to the Architect when He judges His own workmanship.

*Valesius* <note>Francisco Vallés (1524–1592), a Spanish physician who wrote on the intersection of philosophy and the Bible.</note> took up this argument in his book *On Sacred Philosophy* not ungracefully, but in such a way that he left a gleaning for others more fertile than his own harvest. Nor do we see *Rueus* <note>François de la Rue (1520–1585), author of a famous treatise on the gems mentioned in the Bible.</note> comment unsuccessfully *On the Gems of Sacred Scripture*. I knowingly pass over *Ader* <note>Guillaume Ader (1567–1638), who wrote a work examining the physical nature of diseases cured in the Gospels.</note> *On the diseases described in the Gospel*, and others who weigh the Natural History touched upon in the sacred writings. But we leave those matters to the venerable assembly of the Theologians; and so that we may pursue our own purpose, we have decided to speak today on some aspects of the origin, progress, and fortune throughout the world of **Egyptian** and **secret chemistry**, before we shortly—God favoring us—begin to publicly teach the elements of **medical chemistry**. <term>Chemia jatrica: also known as iatrochemistry, the branch of alchemy focused on creating medicines rather than transmuting metals.</term>

<margin>Chemistry was invented by Tubalcain, and...</margin>
The cradles of Chemistry look back into the most ancient times. The more wise conclude from the history of **Tubalcain** [who to other nations is **Vulcan** <note>In Roman mythology, Vulcan was the god of fire and metalworking; the author synchronizes biblical history with classical myth.</note>] that it was born before the Flood. To discover all of this requires nothing less than a Divine intellect, though once discovered, any common laborer can carry out the work. For it must not be denied that not only more labor, but also—what carries more weight—a more subtle genius is required to make copper obedient to the blows of the hammer than is required for gold or silver. This is because even a raw nugget of gold <note>original: "aurigleba"</note>, though wrapped in earthy mixtures, passes into pure, unadulterated refined gold <note>original: "obryzum," a Greek-derived term for gold tested and purified by fire.</note> after only one or two conflicts with the fire. In contrast, the earthy nugget of copper—as we believe from the writings of *Ercker* <note>Lazarus Ercker (c. 1530–1594), a famous Bohemian mineralogist.</note>, *Agricola* <note>Georgius Agricola (1494–1555), often called the "father of mineralogy."</note>, and the daily experiments of the metal-working "Pyracmons" <note>original: "Pyracmonum," a reference to one of the Cyclopes who forged lightning bolts for Zeus; here used to describe tireless smiths.</note>—hardly obeys the hammer properly or is approved by the merchant for its correct color or ductile tenacity before the twelfth firing. Although today metallurgists, even those poorly practiced in Chemistry, easily smelt metals from mixed ores because a Master has shown them the way, it was nevertheless necessary for the first Inventor and Master to be a Chemist. ...[powder] <note>Likely "pulverem" (powder/gunpowder), continuing from the previous page's discussion of chemical preparations.</note> which any neighborhood charcoal-burner could prepare, once the chemist **Berthold Schwarz**, following **Roger Bacon** <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292) was an English friar and philosopher; Berthold Schwarz is the legendary 14th-century German alchemist credited with inventing gunpowder.</note>, showed the way; a secret which fifty solid centuries and more before him had ignored. Nor did the use of the weather-glass <note>original: "vitri calendarii"</note> or the thermoscope <note>An early form of thermometer that shows changes in temperature without a numerical scale.</note>, or the discovery of that excellent fiery color (called **Scarlet** by the Italians) in which Belgian cloths take such pride, proceed from anyone but the chemist **Cornelius Drebbel** <note>Drebbel (1572–1633) was a Dutch engineer and alchemist famous for inventing a scarlet dye and an early submarine.</note>, although common artisans now imitate both inventions with equal success.

<margin>by which reasoning</margin> For indeed, *Hearers*, the first inventors of things consume a lifetime among infinite experiments of the most diverse kinds before they complete a solid and stable art that consistently meets expectations. Although therefore the matter itself speaks for **Tubalcain**—that is, in the judgment of **Vossius** and **Bochart** <note>Gerardus Vossius and Samuel Bochart were prominent 17th-century scholars who specialized in linguistics and biblical history.</note>, and the similarity of the names themselves being persuasive, **Vulcan**—having first proceeded by experimenting upon those masterworks of bronze and iron; it would nevertheless be helpful to complete these arguments by hearing that most truthful of writers, **Diodorus Siculus**, who upon returning from his Egyptian travels wrote thus in *Antiquities, Book V*:
<margin>this fact</margin>

<margin>persuade;</margin> Ἥφαιστον δ’ λέγουσιν εὑρετὴν γενέσθαι τῆς περὶ τὸν σίδηρον ἐργασίας ἁπάσης, καὶ τῆς περὶ τὸν χαλκὸν, καὶ χρυσὸν, καὶ ἄργυρον, καὶ τῶν ἄλλων, ὅσα τὴν ἐκ τοῦ πυρὸς ἐργασίαν ἐπιδέχεται· καὶ τὰς ἄλλας δὲ χρείας τὰς τοῦ πυρὸς ἁπάςας προσεξευρεῖν, καὶ παραδοῦναι τοῖς τε τὰς τέχνας ἐργαζομένοις, καὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις ἅπασιν ἀνθρώποις

> <note>The following is the translation of the Greek text above.</note> *That is: They say that **Vulcan** was the inventor of all that work concerning iron, and bronze, and gold, and silver, and all other things which receive the action of fire. Furthermore, he discovered all other uses of fire and communicated them both to craftsmen and to all other men.* **Homer** agrees in the *Odyssey*, book 15.

-> *I will give you a bowl of marvelous craft,* <-
-> *made entirely of solid silver, and gold encircles its rim;* <-

<note>original: "Δώσω τοι κρητῆρα... / Craterem tibi donabo...". A reference to the divine smith Hephaestus (Vulcan) crafting precious metalwork, often cited in classical literature like Homer.</note>

For indeed, that gold existed from the very beginning is shown abundantly by the land of **Havilah** <note>In Genesis 2:11, Havilah is described as a land where "the gold is good," establishing the antiquity of the metal.</note>. Moreover, that the arts of **Vulcan** were also occupied with the careful handling of gold—or something similar—was sensed long ago by the sublime genius of **Pindar** <note>An ancient Greek lyric poet (c. 518–438 BC) known for his complex odes.</note> in his *Olympian Ode 7*: *The great King of the Gods bathed the city [Rhodes] in golden snows through the skills of Vulcan.* Here, those who look only at the surface of things perceive nothing but a contemptible fable; but those who look with one eye at venerable Antiquity and with the other at the Nature of things, carve out from there a great mystery of Nature. 

Indeed, a sagacious chemist—if necessity drives him—can demonstrate with the help of Vulcan <note>In alchemical writing, "Vulcan" is frequently a metaphor for fire or the process of smelting and refining.</note> that within those snows trodden by the common people, there lies hidden an oil of golden color that instills fertility into the earth. Nor was it without reason that **Pindar** repeated in his *Isthmian Ode 7*: *The greatest of the gods snowing with gold.* Let those who have the leisure draw from this the story of **Danaë** <note>In Greek myth, Zeus visited Danaë as a shower of gold; the author suggests this might be a chemical allegory.</note>, regarding Jupiter turned into a shower of gold and raining into her beloved lap; and let them, with the clever **Sappho**, call gold "the child of Zeus," while I return to **Tubalcain**, or Vulcan.

He was certainly not called **Chrysor** <note>original: "Chryſor". A name from Phoenician mythology, often identified with the inventor of smithing or the "artisan" deity.</note> for no reason by **Philo of Byblos** (citing **Sanchuniathon** <note>An ancient Phoenician author whose works are preserved by Eusebius; he is cited here to link biblical history with pagan mythology.</note> in the works of **Eusebius**); nor was it without cause that **Memphis**, the most noble training ground for Chemistry, flourished for a long time in the temple of Vulcan, according to **Zosimus of Panopolis**, **Eusebius**, and **Synesius** <note>These are early authorities on alchemy and church history; Zosimus (c. 300 AD) is one of the earliest known alchemical writers.</note>.

<margin>What the Writing of Enoch transmits regarding the Inventors of Chemistry, and what Clement of Alexandria says.</margin>

**Zosimus of Panopolis** <note>An Egyptian-born Greek alchemist and Gnostic mystic (c. 300 AD), one of the earliest known authors of alchemical texts.</note>, who is esteemed by **Conring** <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a German polymath who wrote on the history of medicine and law.</note> as the most ancient of chemical writers, seems to refer the beginnings of Chemistry to a time before the Flood in a book titled *Imuth*. He relates that these arts were explained by Angels. The words of **Zosimus**—which **Scaliger** <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a leading scholar of the Renaissance known for his work on chronology and ancient history.</note> read in the manuscripts held in the Royal Library of Paris and previously published in his notes on the *Chronicle of Eusebius*—state the following:

> The sacred scriptures, or rather the books, O woman, say that there is a certain race of spirits that consorts with women. **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary figure to whom many foundational alchemical and philosophical "Hermetic" works were attributed.</note> mentioned this in his *Physics*, and nearly every account, both public and hidden, records this very thing. These ancient and divine scriptures report that Angels were seized by desire for women and, descending, taught them all the works of Nature. Because of this, they were cast out of heaven and remained outside, for they had taught men all things that are wicked and of no benefit to the soul. From these unions, the same scriptures say the Giants were born. Therefore, their first tradition regarding these arts is called *Chema* <note>original: χημᾶ</note>; they named that book *Chema*, and from this, the art itself is called **Chemistry** <note>original: χημία (chemia). In this period, chemistry and alchemy were considered the same discipline.</note>.

According to **Bochart** <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667), a scholar of biblical geography and languages.</note>, *Chema* means "to conceal" among the Arabs, because the art of Chemistry is surely an occult or hidden one.

**Clement of Alexandria** <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who often blended Greek philosophy with Christian doctrine.</note> expressed similar views in the fifth book of his *Stromata* [Miscellanies], though he refrains from using the word *Chemistry*. He says: "We shall set forth..."

Through their untimely gossiping, they profaned the majesty of these secrets, and thereby, among other things, ignited the wrath of the Divine Power against themselves. But even if someone more generous were to grant to **Zosimus** and **Clement** that the beginnings of Chemistry spread throughout the lands should be attributed to "earth-born spirits" <note>original Greek: εγγενεσιν (*engenesin*), referring here to local or innate spirits/genies.</note>, this would in no way detract from the "Queen of Arts" [Chemistry]. <header>### ON THE ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]</header>

What the most ancient **Manetho** wrote concerning **Chemistry**.
</margin>

The history and science of the Egyptian descendants of Ham <note>original: "Chamigenarum"; referring to the biblical Ham, son of Noah, traditionally associated with Egypt</note> have been compiled—not drawn from the often murky depths of library archives, nor from fables spread by the hands of the common people, but sought from pillars placed in the land of Seriadica <note>A legendary land mentioned by Josephus where the descendants of Seth allegedly inscribed their knowledge on pillars to survive the Deluge.</note>, faithfully translated from hieroglyphics into a more common language. On this matter, **Josephus** writes in Book I of *Against Apion*: <note>original: "Manethon itaq; Vir Ægyptius genere..."</note> *Therefore Manetho, an Egyptian man by birth, but educated in Greek learning, as is obvious—for he wrote a history of his country in the Greek tongue—interpreted it, as he himself says, from the sacred books; in many places, he rebukes Herodotus for having lied out of ignorance of Egyptian affairs.* A little later: *He teaches that the Hyksos—that is, the Shepherd Kings, or captive Shepherds (the Israelites)—held Egypt for five hundred and eleven years, etc.* **Suidas** <note>A 10th-century Greek encyclopedia.</note> says this: *Manetho of Diospolis in Egypt, or of Sebennytos, wrote on Natural Philosophy <note>original: "Φυσιολογικὰ"</note>, Astrological Effects in verse <note>original: "Ἀποτελεσματικὰ δι’ ἐπῶν"</note>, and some other astronomical matters.* From this Manetho, of whose work the injustice of time has left us only fragments, **Julius Africanus** drew his material; from Africanus came **Eusebius Pamphilus**, from Eusebius came **George Syncellus**, and from Syncellus came **Joseph Scaliger** <note>These were all famous chronologists and historians who attempted to reconstruct world history.</note>—so that you may recognize, Listeners, by what steps of laborious investigation we strive toward the truth. Therefore, as the writings of Eusebius teach, **Manetho** learned from these most ancient pillars that the first Egyptian **Thoth** <note>original: "Thoyth"; the Egyptian god of wisdom and writing, often identified with Hermes.</note> inscribed upon columns in hieroglyphic letters (but in the Sacred language or Egyptian tongue) the sciences which had flourished before the floodwaters—and what follows from that, **Chemistry**. This entire doctrine was translated after the flood into Greek and transferred again into books with hieroglyphic signs by **Agathodaemon**, the second Mercury and father of **Tat** <note>Figures in Hermetic literature; Tat is the son and disciple of Hermes Trismegistus.</note>.

<margin>
**Manetho** is vindicated.
</margin>

And yet these matters, though they radiate with the clearest light, have hardly been safe in these recent times from the claws of those two luminaries of Germany, **Conringius** and **Ursinus** <note>Hermann Conring and Johann Heinrich Ursinus, 17th-century scholars who were skeptical of the antiquity of Hermetic and alchemical writings.</note>...

### ON THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

<margin>from the plausible arguments of Conring</margin>
...[these records] could not have existed, which had preserved their integrity untouched through so many centuries. And first indeed, we shall not recount how many times Conring—otherwise a most noble writer—tripped himself up in that work *On Hermetic Medicine* <note>Hermann Conring’s 1648 book *De Hermetica Medicina*, which criticized the idea that chemistry originated with the ancient god-king Hermes Trismegistus.</note>. While he remains too indifferent toward chemical practice, he appeals now to his own judgment alone, and now to Hartmann <note>Johann Hartmann (1568–1631), the first professor of "Chymiatry" or chemical medicine.</note> and Angelo Sala of Vicenza <note>Angelo Sala (1576–1637), a pioneer of chemical analysis who rejected the more mystical aspects of alchemy.</note> as if they were oracles; but we shall demand a hearing for our dispute at the tribunal of Antiquity, a forum in which he seems to triumph.

Therefore, the first weapon <note>original: "amentum," referring to a leather strap used to throw a javelin; here used metaphorically for a launching point or argument.</note> of *Conring* is this: *that the Egyptian language is more recent than the Deluge <note>The Great Flood of Noah.</note>, and therefore nothing could have been inscribed on the Seriadis pillars in that idiom before the flood.* You see this sharp weapon, *Listeners*, but it is one that can be blunted. In the beginning indeed, Conring, in his wisdom, would not deny—I know well—that Egypt was a province so close to Mesopotamia and the borders of Paradise, and so fertile in all things, that it was frequented by people and not left wild before the times of those general floods. And should he deny it, he would be immediately rebuked by Ursinus <note>Johann Heinrich Ursinus (1608–1667), a scholar who wrote on the history of the Bible and ancient wisdom.</note>, who teaches that the descendants of Cain founded a kingdom in Egypt. Furthermore, from *Genesis VI*, we know that before the flood, "Tyrants" <note>The Nephilim or giants mentioned in the Bible, often interpreted as powerful rulers.</note> famous above the heavens reigned everywhere in the world; thus, nothing is more plausible than that Egypt, being closest to the first workshop of mankind, was possessed by them.

As for the claim made by some that Egypt was long covered by the sea and only grew and raised its head from the waves through the accumulation of Nile silt—this does not shield *Conring* even if he were to insist upon it. Firstly, because no appearance of truth ties this [geological process] to the times preceding that great public deluge; and secondly, because *Kircher* <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath and early Egyptologist.</note> learnedly demonstrates that this "covering" should by no means be understood as the whole of Egypt, but only as that small space lying between the Delta and the sea. Nor do the words of *Strabo* <note>A Greek geographer and historian (c. 64 BC – 24 AD).</note> look elsewhere...

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>ON THE ORIGIN AND</header>

...[in the first book of the] *Geography* <note>original: "l. 1. Geogr." referring to Strabo's *Geographica*</note>. *But even Egypt,* he says, *it is well known was in ancient times flooded by the sea as far as the marshes, which are at Pelusium, Mount Casius, and Lake Serbonis:* for all these things are shown below the Delta; and the plains near the Delta were inhabited formerly—if not before, then certainly by the nation of Israelites led there by Joseph. Therefore, *Conring* surely would not deny that those Egyptians, older than the flood, spoke. He will object that they spoke, but not in Egyptian, because that *mixture* <note>original: "crama," a Greek word for a blend or mixture, here referring to the confusion of languages at Babel after the flood.</note> of languages *is later than the flood*. I acknowledge, *Listeners*, that those first Egyptians did not speak in that language which the later ones did, but as *Manetho* <note>An Egyptian priest and historian (3rd century BCE) who wrote a history of Egypt.</note> teaches, in the Sacred language, which for the inhabitants of Egypt then was "Egyptian." Truly, what prevents this Sacred language, or the Eberite language <note>original: "Eberinam lingvam," referring to the language of Eber, the traditional ancestor of the Hebrews; the author implies the original language was a form of Hebrew.</note>, from also being called "Egyptian" when it was familiar to all Egyptians—that is, to everyone inhabiting that land which was later called Egypt? It is just as our Danish, which also spread through Norway, is there most appropriately named Norwegian; and the Slavic tongue is hailed as the Bohemian language in Prague, Polish in Warsaw, and Russian in Moscow, although it is nonetheless the same language, distinguished only by a few trivialities of dialect. 

The other argument of *Conring*, however much it may seem valid to others, hardly satisfies me. *Are not hieroglyphic letters,* he says, *signs of things, and not of sounds? How, therefore, could anything be written through hieroglyphic letters either in Egyptian or in Greek?* On the contrary, *Conring*, it certainly could; for hieroglyphics express sounds just as much as things: indeed, through sounds they represent a thing most suitably to the mind. For example, if I express on a tablet the face of the **Two-faced Janus**, inscribed with the number **XVIII**, that figure will signify, in the Danish hieroglyphic language, the **Athenians** <note>This is a phonetic pun or "rebus" typical of 17th-century scholarship. In Danish, the number 18 is "atten." By combining "Atten" with the image of Janus, the author creates a phonetic sound that resembles "Athenians."</note>; no other language receives the force of the figure from the sound...
