...he will achieve it. Thus, if a German were to render an image of a Bear <note>original: "Ursi"; a pun on the "Bar-" in Barneveldt.</note> rolling in a field <note>original: "campo"; a pun on the "-felt" in Barneveldt.</note>, to whose throat is painted the name **Leiden** <note>original: "Lugdunum Batavorum," the Latin name for the city where the statesman was imprisoned.</note>, a wiser German would understand from the sound alone the force of the hieroglyph: namely, that **Barneveldt** <note>Johan van Oldenbarnevelt (1547–1619), a Dutch statesman whose execution was a major political event.</note> would suffer at the throat. A Danish carver of hieroglyphs would express the same fate of Barneveldt with a boy <note>In Danish, "Barn" means child.</note> girded in a skin <note>In Danish, "Felt" can refer to a hide or skin.</note> and playing with a funeral bier.

But if, among theologians arguing about religion, there suddenly flashed on a wall—in the manner of Belshazzar—the figure of a right hand tightly clutching a pair of shears, no one else would unravel the mystery than a Danish interpreter of sacred things <note>original: "hierophantes."</note>, who would clearly gather from it that the religion established in Saxony through **Luther** must be held firmly. <note>The author is likely making a phonetic pun where the image of "clutching" (Lut-) and "shears" or "pure" (lutter) sounds like the name Luther.</note> Following this method, my most honored colleague **Mr. Moth** <note>Likely Matthias Moth (1649–1719), a Danish scholar and statesman.</note> has long since very readily taught the youth in various European Academies universal history, the Greek language, and other disciplines through "Germanic hieroglyphs." <margin>to his</margin> Meanwhile, the Parisians, looking at and hearing of those figures, were struck with amazement, thinking them to be Thessalian magic-wheels or the charms of the witch Canidia <note>A famous witch appearing in the works of the Roman poet Horace.</note> used to draw down the moon from the sky. 

Thus it is believable that the Eberite—or Egyptian—hieroglyphs, being older than the flood <margin>objections.</margin> and thus more sacred and divine than the subsequent mixtures of languages, were intended to express not just meanings, but also the sounds of the spoken words. But how could Mercury <note>The author uses "Mercurius" to refer to the Egyptian Thoth, the mythical inventor of writing.</note> translate those secrets of the columns into Greek immediately after the flood, when that language was not even born then? 

Furthermore, it must be recalled here from Cicero’s *On the Nature of the Gods*, Book III <note>original: "lib. III. de Nat. Deor."</note>, that five Mercuries were numbered in ancient times; who will prove that the last of them died before the Greek language was born? For as to his writing that the second one—the father of Tat—translated them, I will not deny that **Manetho** <note>An Egyptian priest and historian.</note> was hallucinating, confused by the number of Mercuries. ...**Conringius** <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a famous German polymath who often questioned the authenticity of "ancient" hermetic or chemical secrets.</note> stands firm: it seems scarcely probable to him that those columns, which comprised written doctrine, survived from the flood. But why wonder, **Conring**, if columns of bronze and stone despised the inundations of waters? More brilliant indeed is the testimony of **Josephus**, writer of the *Jewish Antiquities*, concerning that column which he places in Syria as a survivor of the flood, than that it should be lightened or trampled upon by the naked authority of a modern writer.

Because they were all endowed with a good nature and inhabited the fatherland without sedition, they spent their lives in perpetual happiness, and discovered the science of the stars and its more refined treatment.* <note>The Greek phrase used is: "they devised wisdom concerning heavenly things and their orderly arrangement."</note>

...they call **Stonehenge**) everyone has seen, that illustrious monument of former **Danish power** in **England** <note>The author, Ole Borch, was Danish; in the 17th century, it was a popular (though incorrect) theory that Stonehenge was built by the Danes during their occupation of England.</note>, which we ourselves observed with wonder long ago on the **Salisbury plain**; and yet those who have never seen England can rest assured on my word that such a thing exists in England.

<margin>columns</margin>

And while I would not fight for the antiquity of these Adamite columns, nor for the truth of the saying of that **"Annius-styled Berosus"** <note>Annius of Viterbo was a 15th-century friar famous for forging "ancient" texts, including those he attributed to the Babylonian priest Berosus.</note>: *that **Noah** entrusted many natural secrets to writing, which the Scythian Armenians entrust only to priests*; nevertheless, by frequent reading I am convinced that all the most ancient heroes and legislators entrusted the memory of things to columns. For, to say nothing of the **columns of Hercules** at the shores of Gades <note>Modern-day Cadiz, Spain.</note>, what is clearer than the words of **Diodorus Siculus**, *Book I, Chapter II*?

> **Osiris**, he says, in his journey through the world, set up columns in many places as witnesses of his expedition. Again, **Diodorus** says: **Hercules**, being Egyptian by race, after he had traversed a great part of the world by his valor, placed a column in Libya. **Strabo**, *Geography Book XVII*: **Sesostris** traversed all of **Ethiopia**, advancing as far as the cinnamon-bearing region; some of his monuments, columns, and inscriptions <margin>in public</margin> <note>original Greek: στήλαι κἀι ἐπιγραφαί (*stēlai kai epigraphai*), meaning pillars and inscriptions.</note> are still shown.

...indeed, he had transcribed those things from the best sources, as he claimed. But if <margin>He bites.</margin> <note>A marginal note indicating that Ursinus is being critical or "snappy" in his argument.</note> he had deceived, he [Manetho] adds, other Priests would have refuted him, if for no other reason than to win the favor of **King Ptolemy Philadelphus** for themselves; for there have never been those who would not rather it be well for themselves than for another. Soft words, **Ursinus**! I return your own words to you, foresightfully reversed: Who will guarantee that **Manetho** *was* refuted? Who will guarantee that **Hermappion** was *not* refuted? To which of mortals, finally, can the arguments of all the books that have perished be known? Not to mention that those **Kircherian** interpretations of obelisks are held more suspect by more discerning **Italians** even today, <margin>but unsuccessfully.</margin> <note>The Latin 'sed infeliciter' suggests Kircher's efforts were a failure.</note> and in **Rome** itself, more faith is still placed in **Hermappion** by many than in that **German**, **Athanasius** <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit scholar famous for his attempts to decipher hieroglyphs, which were later proven mostly incorrect.</note>.

A Roman idiom referring to a particularly massive or overwhelming wave; here it means Ursinus's "biggest" argument.</note> from **Ursinus**! These are cold arguments, **Ursinus**, and closer to audacity. For first, from where do you gather that the **Holy Bible** had already been translated into the **Greek language** when **Manetho** offered his writing, since **Ptolemy** lived for a long time? Secondly, the dynasties of **Manetho**, as **Scaliger**, **Vossius**, and others have already shown before, do not oppose the **Sacred Scriptures**, but to an observant antiquarian looking into the matter, they agree excellently, with a few exceptions. Thirdly, the **Egyptian Kings** already knew the innermost shrines of their sacred rites and all the secrets of the Priests, with whom, as is most evident from **Diodorus Siculus** and others, they were familiarly...

# ON THE ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]

...they were so occupied that the Holy Scripture, having been translated into Greek, could not have brought a "chill to the priests' kitchens." <note>original: "frigus inducere Sacerdotum culinis." A colorful idiom suggesting that the arrival of the Greek Bible did not threaten the Egyptian priests' "daily bread" or their cultural influence.</note> And who will guarantee that **Ptolemy** <note>Ptolemy II Philadelphus, the king credited with commissioning the translation of the Hebrew Bible into Greek (the Septuagint).</note> believed that those sacred Hebrew writings were of Divine authority? On the contrary, the opposite is manifest from the fact that neither **Ptolemy** himself nor a long line of his successors placed any value on the Jewish religion or joined themselves to it. Rather, the authority of the Egyptian Priests flourished to such an extent, even after the Bible was translated, that **Eusebius Pamphilus** <note>Also known as Eusebius of Caesarea, a 4th-century historian.</note> is a rich witness that the sacred rites of **Serapis** in **Egypt** lasted even until the age of **Porphyry**. <margin>Was the book</margin> **Ursinus** does not rest yet, but argues quite plausibly: "**Diodorus**," he says, "came to **Egypt** 230 years after **Manetho**, yet he does not hear of those things which **Manetho** wrote; instead, he hears mostly those things which **Herodotus** had written 159 years before **Manetho**: namely, that Gods and **Vulcan**, etc., reigned in **Egypt**." These points are timely, **Ursinus**, for neither **Herodotus** nor **Diodorus**—being Greeks and outsiders—were admitted to inspect the secret books of the **Egyptians**; those were reserved for the temples and the royal archives. <margin>of Manetho seen by Diodorus?</margin> Greek travelers, upon arriving, drew most of their information from the dregs of the foul commoners, and very little from the Priests—and even then, only the more insignificant matters. This would be easy to show from **Strabo**, **Origen**, **Clement of Alexandria**, and **Plutarch**, if necessity drove us to that point. Indeed, it is no wonder if **Diodorus** did not see **Manetho’s** book; many writers are lost in an interval of two hundred years, many are carried elsewhere, and many are preserved in the Museums of Princes, inspected by very few. Many, though they may have written the truth, are for certain reasons twisted into the appearance of fables. Nor did **Herodotus** mention the *Physics* of **Manetho**, yet it is established that they existed from **Diogenes Laërtius**, who, in his preface, showed that he used **Manetho’s** *Summary of Natural Philosophy* <note>original Greek: Φυσικῶν Ἐπιτομῇ (Phusikon Epitome)</note>. Nor did the *Apotelesma-*... <note>The text cuts off here; "Apotelesmatum" likely refers to Manetho's work on astrology.</note>

...whether **Diodorus** or **Herodotus** mentioned **Manetho**, the **Medici Library** <note>The Laurentian Library in Florence, famous for its collection of ancient manuscripts.</note> proves he still survives today. It was there in **Florence** that my most honored colleague, **Erasmus Bartholinus**, <note>A prominent Danish physician and mathematician (1625–1698) known for discovering the double refraction of light.</note> some time ago transcribed a fragment of **Manetho**'s *On Diseases*, which begins:

> Come now, O Libethrian Muse, and tell me
> all the pains that divine **Selene** brings in diseases. 
> <note>original Greek: "Νῦν δ' ἄγε μοι κούρη λειβηθριὰς ἔννεπε Μοῦσα, Ὅσσα φέρει νόσοις ἀλγήματα δῖα Σελήνη." This quote is from the *Apotelesmatica*, an astrological poem attributed to Manetho. The Libethrian Muses were nymphs associated with sacred springs; Selene is the Moon.</note>

<margin>
**Sanchuniathon**, 
a truthful

</margin>
<margin>
And the 
truthful

</margin>
Moreover, **Manetho** is praised by **Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155 – c. 220 AD).</note> in his *Apology*: "One must refer," he says, "to the archives of the most ancient nations: the **Egyptians**, **Chaldeans**, and **Phoenicians**. Through them, knowledge has been provided by such men as **Manetho** the Egyptian and **Berosus** the Chaldean, but also **Iromus**, the Phoenician King of **Tyre**." <note>original Latin: "Referanda... archiva... Manethon Ægyptius & Berosus Chaldæus, sed & Iromus Phœnix Tyri Rex."</note> Quite fittingly, **Philo of Byblos**—quoted in **Eusebius**—complains that the Egyptian priests cast a shadow of darkness over historical truth. Even though they know **Sanchuniathon** <note>A legendary Phoenician author said to have written about the history of the gods and the creation of the world before the Trojan War.</note> is a most truthful writer, they nevertheless ignored his work and turned back to fables. **Philo** explains that this is the reason why the **Greeks**, being ignorant of the mystical meaning of Egyptian words, spent their efforts interpreting those fables, often with poor results. 

Thus, **Manetho** and **Sanchuniathon** suffered the same fate. I only wish that our experience with the reliability of most modern historians was as certain as the testimony given for **Manetho**'s reliability by **Eusebius** and **Flavius Josephus**. **Eusebius** teaches that **Manetho** transcribed other things from the most ancient **Seriadic columns**; <note>Legendary pillars (often associated with the biblical Seth or Enoch) said to have been inscribed with ancient wisdom to survive a global fire or flood.</note> **Josephus** notes he drew from the "Sacred Books"—that is, the most ancient manuscripts of the priests kept in the temples. Let those modern historians, who so often disagree with one another in their telling of a single fact, see where they get their own information; meanwhile, I shall come to the aid of **Manetho** using **Strabo**'s *Geography*, Book 17. He says the Egyptian priests recorded things "in the sacred letters" <note>original Greek: "εἰς τὰ ἱερὰ γράμματα"—a reference to hieroglyphs or the sacred priestly script.</note> and confirmed...

...they admired <note>Continuing the verb "admira-bant" from the previous page.</note> things that possessed some more subtle knowledge. Behold the open springs from which **Manetho** drew! But such was the admirable frankness of **Manetho**, as **Josephus** himself relates in *Against Apion*, Book 1, that he confessed he had drawn certain things from the dregs. These are the words of **Josephus**: *But those things which Manetho added, not from the Egyptian writings but—as he himself confessed—from the fables of certain nameless people, I shall set forth in detail.* <note>original Latin: "Ea verò quæ Manethon non ex Ægyptiis literis, sed sicut ipse confessus est, è fabulis quorumdam sine nomine addidit, particulatim adjiciam."</note> But the spirited vehemence of **Conring** <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a German polymath who was highly skeptical of the antiquity of alchemy.</note> and **Ursinus** in contradicting the Ancients has carried us almost beyond our purpose; let us return to the path.

<margin>
Chemistry was a long-time guest in the family of **Ham**.
</margin>
We have said that **Chemistry** long ago chose its home in the family of **Ham** <note>The author uses the Latin form "Cham," referring to the biblical son of Noah.</note>, that is, in Egypt, the patrimony of the **descendants of Ham**. To prove this, it must first be observed that Egypt itself was long ago called the **land of Ham** <note>original Latin: "terram Cham"</note> not only in **David’s Psalm 105**, but even today it is called **Chemi** by the **Copts**, according to **Bochart** <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667), a French scholar of biblical geography.</note>; indeed, long ago it was called **Chemia** (as if it were **Chamiah**) by **Plutarch** in *Isis and Osiris*. Nor is the reason obscure; for since **Ham**'s son **Mizraim** took possession of Egypt as an invader according to **Genesis 11**, <note>The author likely refers to the "Table of Nations" in Genesis 10-11, where Mizraim is listed as the son of Ham and the progenitor of the Egyptians.</note> it is no wonder that some traces of his father's arts remained with him, and that Egypt was called **Chamiah** or **Chemia** after the name of his father. That something of his father’s knowledge stuck in the mind of **Mizraim** is clear, among other things, from Book 4 of the *Recognitions* of **Clement of Rome**, who says expressly: *One of the children of Noah, Ham,* he says, *passed on an ill-acquired discipline to one of his sons who was called Mizraim, from whom the race of the Egyptians and Persians is derived.* <note>original Latin: "Ex liberis Noë unus Cham, inquit, cuidam ex filiis, qui Mizraim appellabatur, à qvo Ægyptiorum, Persarumq; genus ducitur, malè compertam tradidit disciplinam." The *Recognitions* is an early Christian philosophical novel.</note> The nations of that time called him **Zoroaster**. These are the words of **Clement**.

<margin>
Great minds vindicated from the suspicion of Magic.
</margin>
Although **Clement** twists the discipline of **Mizraim** (or **Zoroaster**) toward the magical arts, the illustrious **Naudé** <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653), author of *Apology for all the Great Men Fatally Suspected of Magic*.</note> in a brilliant work vindicates those great minds: **Zoroaster**, **Hermes**, **Pythagoras**, **Virgil**, **Paracelsus**, **Cornelius Agrippa**...

# THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

He so manfully vindicated **Roger Bacon**, **Peter of Abano**, and others—who had been accused of "infamous magic" by the unlearned masses of scholars—from the crimes charged against them, that **Del Rio** <note>Martin Delrio (1551–1608), a Jesuit scholar famous for his works on magic and witchcraft.</note> and his associates are left in a state of hesitation, and are likely to remain so. Let us approach the cradles of **Chemistry** more closely. Let **Eusebius Pamphilus** <note>The "Father of Church History" (c. 260–339 AD).</note> therefore step onto the stage and speak through an ancient writer:

*Democritus of Abdera, the Natural Philosopher, flourished in Egypt, having been initiated by **Ostanes the Mede**—who was sent to Egypt by the Persian kings of that time to oversee the sacred rites—in the temple of **Memphis**, together with other priests and philosophers, among whom were a certain wise Hebrew woman named **Maria** <note>Maria the Jewess, a legendary foundational figure in alchemy, credited with inventing the water-bath or "Bain-marie."</note> and **Pammenes**. He wrote clearly about gold, silver, stones, and purple dye. Likewise Maria; they were taught by **Ostanes**, though they hid the art behind many wise riddles.* <note>original Greek: "Δημόκριτος Ἀβδερίτης... κρύψαντες τὴν τέχνην."</note>

**Synesius** records things very similar to these, as does that ancient author **Zosimus of Panopolis** <note>One of the earliest known alchemical authors (c. 300 AD).</note>; the latter showed himself to me in a more extensive form in a brilliant manuscript within the Royal Library of Paris. This evidence is so strong that it cannot be dismissed as mere fragments of **Syncellus** or **Panodorus**, as **Scaliger** and **Conring** <note>Hermann Conring, mentioned on the previous page as a skeptic of alchemical antiquity.</note> pointlessly try to suggest. **Michael Psellus**, a writer of celebrated name, says most clearly in a letter to the Patriarch **Xiphilinus**:

<margin>
Democritus and Ostanes, the ancient Chemists.
</margin>

*Democritus of Abdera,* he says, *after he had arrived in Egypt, was initiated into the sacred mysteries by the Great **Ostanes** in the temple of Memphis, along with other Egyptian priests.* And a little later: *Democritus wrote about the tincture of the Sun and Moon <note>In alchemical language, the "Sun" refers to Gold and the "Moon" to Silver.</note>, and about precious stones and purple.*

That same **Democritus** was the first to bring into Greece that golden maxim: *Nature rejoices in nature, nature overcomes nature, nature masters nature.* <note>original Greek: "ἡ Φύσις τῇ Φύσει τέρπεται, ἡ Φύσις τὴν Φύσιν νικᾷ, ἡ Φύσις τὴν Φύσιν κρατεῖ"</note> However, I will not maintain that **Democritus** was the first to write these words, for I remember noting in a manuscript of **Isis** in Paris that these very words were inscribed in sacred characters <note>The author likely refers to Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note> in the inner sanctuary of the temple of Memphis.

...once shone. As for the fact that **Scaliger** <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a towering figure in Renaissance scholarship who specialized in historical chronology.</note> criticizes **Ostanes** here as being by no means <term>contemporaneous</term> <note>original Greek: "σύγχρονον" (synchronon), meaning existing at the same time.</note> with **Democritus**, we consider this to be of no consequence, for there were undoubtedly several Medes named **Ostanes**, just as there were several **Scaligers** from Verona. **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, the 4th-century historian of the early Church.</note> mentions another in *Preparation for the Gospel, Book I, Chapter X*: *Furthermore, the same **Ostanes** teaches about God in the volume he titled the Octateuch [The Eight Books].* Certainly, **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of the encyclopedic Natural History.</note> mentions both a first and a second **Ostanes** in *Book XXX, Chapter I*. Moreover, **Diodorus Siculus** <note>A 1st-century BC Greek historian.</note> clearly shows in *Book III, Chapter VI and Book II, Chapter VI* that **Democritus** lived in Egypt, adding these words: *They believe that **Democritus**, in those five years he lived in Egypt, made the greatest progress in **Astrology**.*

<margin>
Astrology is twofold: one celestial, the other metallic.
</margin>
Lest we wander into error here, it must be observed that **Astrology** was long ago twofold: that higher sort sought from the rays of the celestial stars brought down to earth, and the lower sort derived from those shining stars of the great Mother Earth—that is, the brilliant nuggets of metals. And this is why we just observed from **Psellus** <note>Michael Psellus (11th century), a Byzantine philosopher who wrote on alchemy and the occult.</note> that **Democritus** wrote about the *Tincture of the Sun and Moon*, that is—to express it with a clearer name—about the subtle and colored liquid derived from gold and silver. But since certain petty critics—those **Aristarchs** <note>Refers to Aristarchus of Samothrace, an ancient grammarian; used here to describe pedantic or overly harsh critics.</note> who are strangers to the better parts of antiquity—frequently make a noise to bring envy upon this most healthy Art, claiming that **Chemists** play with words and greet metals with the names of stars by an example unusual to the ancients, an invention of **Paracelsus** <note>The 16th-century Swiss physician who revolutionized chemistry and medicine.</note> or, looking further back, of **Lull** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a philosopher and mystic often associated with early chemical treatises.</note>; come, let us defeat that thick and ignorant rashness using the evidence of the Ancients.

<margin>
The antiquity of Astrology is asserted.
</margin>
Let the ancient rites of the **Persians** come forth into the light, about which **Origen** <note>An influential early Christian theologian (c. 184–253 AD).</note> speaks so eloquently in *Book VI against Celsus*: *Then, so that in the book written against us **Celsus** might show off his varied learning, he inserts certain Persian Mysteries, where he says: "These things are indicated by both the doctrine of the Persians and the [mysteries] of **Mithras**..."*

# ON THE [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]

<margin>Metallic lore from various writers.</margin>
*...the sacred rite of **Mithras** <note>The mystery religion centered on the god Mithras, popular in the Roman Empire, which involved a series of initiatory grades.</note> is their ancestral custom: for in it there is a representation of the two revolutions of the sky—both that by which the fixed stars are carried, and that by which the planets travel—and the symbol of the soul’s transit through them is as follows: a ladder raised on high with seven gates, one above the other, up to the very highest arch of the eighth gate. The first of the gates is of lead, the second of tin, the third of bronze, the fourth of iron, the fifth of mixed coinage, the sixth of silver, and the seventh of gold. They make the first gate that of **Saturn**, marking the slowness of that star with lead; the second they make that of **Venus**, comparing to it the brightness and softness of tin; the third of **Jupiter**, being brazen and solid; the fourth of **Mercury**, for he is patient of labors and a merchant <note>original: "negotiator"</note>, celebrated among men just like iron; the fifth of **Mars** because of its unequal and varied mixture; the sixth of the **Moon**, being of silver; and the seventh of the **Sun**, being of gold, representing those stars by their own colors.* <margin>The error of the profane Celsus.</margin> *In which passage of **Celsus** <note>Celsus was a 2nd-century Greek philosopher and opponent of early Christianity; his work "The True Word" is preserved mainly through fragments quoted by Origen.</note> it is detected that a manifest error has crept in through the fault of the scribes: for the second gate, which was first called "of tin," is shortly thereafter called "of **Venus**" by someone ignorant of the art. Thus, according to the precepts of the art, the passage of **Celsus** ought to be reconstructed:* *they make the second gate that of **Jupiter**, comparing to it the brightness and softness of tin; the third of **Venus**, being brazen and solid; the fourth of **Mars**, for he is patient of labors, celebrated among men just like iron; the fifth of **Mercury** because of its unequal and varied mixture, and because he is a merchant; the sixth of the **Moon**, of silver; the seventh of the **Sun**, of gold.*

But why are those metallic planets introduced into the sacred rites of **Mithras**? **Kircher** <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath who attempted to link Egyptian, Persian, and Christian mysteries.</note> proves quite appropriately in his *Pamphilian Obelisk* that **Mithras** was none other than **Mizraim** <note>In the Bible, Mizraim is the son of Ham and the progenitor of the Egyptians.</note>, the name having been corrupted by the Persians. Therefore, since **Hermes** (or **Canaan**), that father of **Chemistry**, was joined to his brother **Mizraim** by the closest bond of love, he undoubtedly revealed various secrets of metals to him. It is no wonder, then, that a mystical representation of this matter exists in the rites of **Mizraim**, or...

# ON THE ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]

->DE ORTU ET<-

...mention of **Mithras** was made. Although it is not to be denied that later, as with all other things, the name of **Mithras** was adapted to signify something else, and was finally used for the **Sun**. Hence the ancient inscription in **Gruter** <note>Jan Gruter (1560–1627) was a Dutch scholar famous for his massive collection of ancient Roman inscriptions.</note>: **TO THE SUN MITHRAS**. And shortly after: **TO THE OMNIPOTENT GOD MITHRAS, PISONIANUS, FATHER-OF-FATHERS** <note>original: "Pater Patratus," the highest grade of initiation in the Mithraic mysteries.</note>. It is certain that the same thing happened to **Serapis** or **Sarapis**: this naming was initially attributed to an Egyptian King, then to an Egyptian God, and finally to the Sun. Thus we may read in the same **Gruter**: *To Zeus Helios the Great Serapis and the gods who share his temple* <note>original Greek: "ΔII HΛIΩ MEΓAΛΩI ΣAPAΠIΔI KAI TOIΣ ΣYNNAIOIΣ ΘEOIΣ"</note>. Indeed, the word for gold was already publicly substituted for the Sun long ago in a Gruterian inscription: **TO THE UNCONQUERED GOD OF GOLD, SECUNDINIUS DONATUS, GRAIN-OFFICER** <note>original: "Frumentarius," a Roman official responsible for the grain supply or, occasionally, imperial intelligence.</note>.

From this, it is no wonder that, amidst such frequent travel back and forth, a part of that **metallic astrology** reached even as far as the **Indians**. This we may establish even from **Philostratus** <note>Flavius Philostratus, who wrote a famous biography of the miracle-worker Apollonius of Tyana.</note>: **Apollonius**, he says, having philosophized in secret with **Iarchas the Brahmin**, received from him as a gift **seven rings** named after the **seven planets**, each of which he was to wear on the days subject to those planets. Namely, he was to wear a **gold ring** on the **day of the Sun** (Sunday), **silver** on the **day of the Moon** (Monday), **iron** on the **day of Mars** (Tuesday), **quicksilver** on the **day of Mercury** (Wednesday), **tin** on the **day of Jupiter** (Thursday), **bronze** on the **day of Venus** (Friday), and **lead** on the **day of Saturn** (Saturday), because a specific metal corresponds to each individual planet. **Didymus** <note>Didymus Chalcenterus, a prolific scholar and grammarian of late Hellenistic Alexandria.</note> rightly observes regarding the **Iliad**, Book 5: *For the star of Ares [Mars] is called the iron one* <note>original Greek: "Ἐπεὶ γὰρ ὁ Ἄρειος ἀστὴρ, ὁ σιδήρειος καλέμενος"</note>; that is, he calls the star of Mars "iron."

It appears that **Plato** also looked toward the same concept in his ***Critias***: *The walls of the Atlantic city,* he says, *covered their entire exterior surface with thin bronze, the interior with tin, and finally the wall surrounding the citadel with fiery-glowing orichalcum* <note>Orichalcum was a legendary gold-colored metal, often identified as a high-quality brass or copper alloy.</note>. *As for the Royal Palace constructed against the citadel, it was like this: in the middle, a sacred and in-*

<meta>The catchword "accedum" indicates the text continues with "inaccessible" (inaccessum).</meta>

It demonstrates the widespread historical belief in "metallic astrology," where gold represents the Sun and other metals correspond to the planets.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the antiquity of chemical and astrological knowledge, specifically the symbolic link between celestial bodies and metals. # ON THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

->PROGRESSU CHEMIÆ.<-

...access to the temple of **Clito** and **Neptune**, which was surrounded by a golden circuit. They adorned all the exterior gables of the temple of **Neptune** with silver, and the roofs themselves and the peaks with gold. He had said previously: *the Soldiers lived in the upper parts of it, alone near the temple of Minerva and Vulcan: they inhabited its locations turned toward the North.* Regarding this passage, the most learned **Marsilio Ficinus** <note>A famous Italian Renaissance philosopher who translated Plato's works into Latin.</note> once remarked: he mentions metals, he says, so that through those seven metals we may understand the influences of the seven planets governing the generation of all things. The **Platonists** assign gold to the Sun, silver to the Moon, lead to Saturn, electrum <note>A naturally occurring alloy of gold and silver.</note> to Jupiter, iron and bronze to Mars, orichalcum <note>A legendary golden-colored metal, likely a form of brass or high-quality bronze.</note> to Venus, and tin to Mercury.

Lest we think these Platonic ideas were drawn from anywhere else than from the Egyptians, **Plato** himself in the *Critias*, before he undertakes the description of the island of **Atlantis**, says: *if we remember enough, and report now to you, what was once handed down by the Priests [namely, the Egyptians] and brought here by Solon, we shall seem to have fulfilled our duty to this theater, as I believe.* And it is remarkable how everything here concerning the temple of **Vulcan**, and concerning the dwellings of the inhabitants facing the North, agrees with **Herodotus** <note>The Greek "Father of History" who wrote extensively on the monuments and customs of Egypt.</note>.

<margin>
The error of the Platonists in Metallic Astrology.
</margin>
One thing I note here: **Solon**, through those secretive arts of **Memphis**, was not able to penetrate as far as **Apollonius of Tyana** did later from **Iarchas** <note>Iarchas was a legendary Indian sage whom Apollonius visited to learn secret wisdom.</note>, who very fittingly aligns the superior stars with the inferior stars—however much he is, and deservedly so, lashed for other reasons in the works of **Eusebius** and **Tortoletus**. For the **Platonists**, along with **Solon**, erred in this: that they attribute orichalcum only to Venus, electrum to Jupiter, and tin to Mercury; whereas, from the practice of the Egyptians already known through the Arabs, bronze <note>Copper</note> is owed to Venus, white lead <note>Tin</note> to Jupiter, and quicksilver <note>Mercury</note> to Mercury. For orichalcum refers to nothing other than bronze, since it is nothing else than bronze already fused in its own vein of calamine... <note>original: "cadmia," a zinc-rich ore used in antiquity to turn copper into brass.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the antiquity of chemical and astrological knowledge, moving from the Platonic and Herodotus-based descriptions of metals on the previous page to the specific symbolic and physical properties of electrum and the origins of astrological symbols in Egyptian hieroglyphics.</meta>

# ON THE ORIGIN AND
->PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY<-

...penetrated with soot, as occurs in Darien <note>A reference to the Isthmus of Darien in modern-day Panama, often cited in early modern texts regarding gold mining.</note>; now rendered more brilliant by craftsmen through the mixing of calamine <note>original: "cadmiâ"; a zinc-rich mineral used in making brass.</note>. However, *electrum* is not a distinct species of metal, but a mixture of four parts gold and one part silver, whether joined by artifice or by the spontaneous action of nature, as *Pliny* teaches among others. Hence, when according to the fifth book of the *Stromata* by *Clement of Alexandria*, the Egyptians form a circle when they wish to express the sun in a hieroglyphic character, and when they intend to represent the Moon, they shape a figure bearing the appearance of the Moon's horns; those very characters are today commonly used to denote gold and silver.

<margin>What the Isiac Cross signifies when resolved into its parts.</margin>
Hence, from that mystical *Isiac* cross (which some call *Hermetic*, others *Thautic* <note>Referring to Thoth, the Egyptian god of wisdom.</note>, which is the same thing), seen even today in almost all Egyptian statues, others in the past—and among them *Francis Dee* of London <note>Likely referring to the famous polymath and occultist John Dee, author of the *Monas Hieroglyphica*.</note>, and recently *Kircher* in his *Oedipus*, and *Kriegsmann*—have derived all the figures of the celestial and terrestrial planets by a most convenient unraveling. Furthermore, it is clear from *Manilius* of the Augustan age that it was believed that parts of the human body have a certain kinship with the celestial stars; hence he attributes individual parts of the body to each of the twelve signs of the zodiac: the neck to Taurus, the shoulders to Gemini, the breast to Cancer, and so on. Indeed, from almost every age, those unions of heaven and earth, of the upper and lower planets, seem to have been understood by the wise.

<margin>The marriage of heaven and earth.</margin>
Fittingly, *Elluchasen the Arab* <note>Ibn Butlan, an 11th-century Christian Arab physician known for the *Tacuinum Sanitatis*.</note> says in his book *On Egyptian Rites*: "The Egyptian philosophers, as I found in their histories, investigated with the greatest zeal that Great Chain by which the superior world is bound to the inferior; that is, they attempted to explore that bond by which the Angelic world is joined to the sidereal world—namely, to the twelve signs of the Zodiac and the seven planets—and how this world is connected to the elementary world, and from all—"

...from the parts which are subject to it—from minerals, stones, herbs, plants, and animals, as well as their properties and all their operations—they finally learned through long use, experience, and reasoning that all the forms and likenesses of the lower things are joined to the forms and likenesses of the higher things; and in this way they provided many and admirable things in medical practice. Iamblichus expressed the same things long ago in few words in *On the Mysteries of the Egyptians*. "The world," he says, "is a single living being, in which the parts, though distant in location, are nevertheless drawn toward each other because of their shared nature; specifically, so that the Sun, the heart, and gold correspond to one another in many ways, just as do the Moon, the brain, and silver, etc." Now let the gloomy detractors of Chemistry go and ignorantly shout that both its principles and its vocabulary were born just the day before yesterday. Let
<margin>Metals were composed of sulfur and mercury even according to the Ancients.</margin>
Conring <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a German physician and scholar who famously argued against the antiquity of alchemy.</note> go and deny that metals were composed of sulfur and mercury before the age of Paracelsus <note>The 16th-century Swiss physician who championed chemical medicine.</note>, or at the very most, before the time of Isaac Hollandus and Lullius <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a philosopher to whom many later alchemical texts were attributed.</note>. What would Hesiod respond to this? What would Pindar? What would Diodorus Siculus respond, if they properly understood what they had received from the Egyptians? Hesiod indeed recounts in his *Theogony* that Theia, daughter of the Sky, bore the Sun and the Moon by Hyperion. Diodorus Siculus relates similar things in the fourth book of his *Antiquities*, chapter five. However, Pindar, that architect of profound meanings, is even more explicit; for right at the beginning of his abrupt work he exclaimed: "Water is best, but gold is a blazing fire," <note>original Greek: "ἄριστον μὲν ὕδωρ, ὁ δὲ χρυσὸς αἰθόμενον πῦρ"</note> and thus, for those who examine everything more closely, he suggested mercury <term>hydrargyron: the ancient name for mercury or quicksilver</term> by "best water," and by "blazing fire" he suggested glowing sulfur, as if they were the inward principles of gold. Finally, in the *Isthmian Odes*, he pursues the matter even more clearly: "O Mother Theia of many names, mother of the Sun," he says, "because of you, men have also regarded gold as more powerful than other things." <note>original Greek: "Μᾶτερ, ἁλίου πολυώνυμε Θεία, σέο γ’ ἕκατι καὶ μεγασθενῆ νόμισαν χρυσὸν ἄνθρωποι περιώσιον ἄλλων."</note> That is: "Most celebrated Theia, mother of the Sun, for your sake men have even considered gold to be more powerful than other metals." <margin>
The secrets of Nature were once veiled in fables.
</margin>
Those who see more sharply perceive the <term>divine element<note>original: *τὸ θεῖον* (to theion). In Greek, this word means both "divine" and "sulfur," a linguistic overlap that alchemists used to claim that ancient "divine" myths were actually chemical secrets.</note></term> of metals, which is *sulfur*; and through Hyperion, they see *Mercury*. For what climbs the heights faster than Mercury, if you apply even the most modest fire? Indeed, listen: the fables of the ancients are not mere stories, but the storehouses and stewards of natural secrets. Plutarch writes on this matter in *On the Physical Theology of the Greeks*: "The ancient theology," he says, "among both the Greeks and the Barbarians, was a physical discourse veiled in myths" <note>original: *ὅτι μὲν οὖν ἡ παλαιὰ Θεολογία καὶ παρ' Ἕλλησι καὶ Βαρβάροις λόγος ἦν Φυσικὸς ἐκκεκαλυμμένος μύθοις*</note>. Soon, pursuing this theme, he explains *Latona* as the night, *Juno* as the earth, *Apollo* as the Sun, and *Jupiter* as heat. After a few more remarks, he adds: *The Egyptians say Osiris is the Sun, Isis is the Moon, Jupiter is the Spirit permeating all things, and Vulcan is fire, etc.* <note>Manetho (3rd century BCE) was an Egyptian priest whose works on history and religion were frequently cited by later scholars.</note> Manetho follows all of this more extensively. Thus, Tzezes <note>Joannes Tzezes, a 12th-century Byzantine scholar known for his commentaries on classical literature.</note> opportunely interprets the fable of Perseus as being about the Sun and the exhalations of water.

<margin>
Eusebius is noted for being critical of fables.
</margin>
Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339), a Christian historian who often dismissed pagan mythology as mere superstition or corrupted history.</note>, indeed, being intent on sacred matters, finds it difficult to accept that the fables of the ancients are used to explain the nature of things. But we must forgive the venerable Father, for he shows little regard here for that saying of Seneca: *In the same meadow, the ox finds grass, the dog the hare, and the stork the snake.* If, says Eusebius in *Preparation for the Gospel*, book III, chapter XIII: *If they want Aesculapius to be the Sun, how is it that he was consumed by Jupiter’s lightning because of shameful profit and infamy, as Pindar sang?* I will not labor the point that there were several figures named Aesculapius, which is easy to show from Cyril and Clement of Alexandria. We learn from Apollonius Rhodius that the Greek Aesculapius took part in the expedition of the Argonauts with the Dioscuri; however, Clement of Alexandria, in the first book of his *Stromata*, introduces another Egyptian one from Memphis, and in his *Exhortation to the Greeks*, mentions he was struck by lightning, according to Pin-

...teaches that it lies in the <term>Little Bear<note>original: *Cynosuridæ*. The Little Bear constellation, often used in navigation and symbolic of a guiding principle or fixed point.</note></term>. But as to the point at hand, the Egyptian priests (as Origen teaches in Book I of *Against Celsus*) fed the populace with fables, while they themselves philosophized under the names of their ancestral gods. Nor is it necessary for fables to correspond in every detail; nor would it be difficult to interpret that great burning of the Sun or of Phaethon according to the laws of nature, if necessity called for it. But Eusebius insists again in Book II, Chapter VIII: "From this we understand," he says, "that the Romans never wished to approve that new physiology of the Greeks used in unraveling the fables of the Gods." But what had those Romans, those "sons of Mars," to do with the inner sanctuary of Nature? It was the Roman way to do and to endure great things; the ingenious and subtle belonged to other nations. Hence, for all things curious, they utilized the intellect and the hands of the Greeks.

<margin>Conring finally grants chemistry to the Egyptians</margin>
So far, the antiquity of chemistry has been vindicated by Zosimus, Synesius, Psellus, and others, and its first births assigned to Egypt. Yet it might be helpful to hear an express confession, almost against his will, from <term>Conring<note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a German physician and polymath who was famously skeptical of the ancient origins of alchemy.</note></term>: "On the whole, however," he says, "I myself am of the opinion that the Egyptian <term>hierophants<note>High priests or revealers of sacred mysteries.</note></term>, the foremost of all mortals, boasted of <term>gold-making<note>original: *χρυσοποιΐαν* (chrysopoeia), the alchemical art of transmuting base metals into gold.</note></term>, and that from them the beginnings of chemistry flowed." These are the words of Conring, who later, as if forgetting himself, concedes this, yet seething with hatred for the Egyptians and the chemists, he belittles, tramples, and ruins all the wisdom of the Egyptians, however much it was praised in Moses and Solomon by the Spirit of God.

<margin>Conring attacks the knowledge of the Egyptians</margin>
"There came from the Egyptians to the Greeks," he says, "only those things which pertain to religion, magic, civil laws, and mathematical disciplines; likewise: the entire wisdom of the Egyptians was generally both thin and full of impious superstition, not to be compared with the studies of the Greeks." Where then, Conring, do you categorize the following Egyptian inventions? <term>Panopolis<note>An ancient city in Upper Egypt, modern Akhmim, known in antiquity for its skilled artisans and association with alchemical traditions.</note></term>, that "ancient dwelling-place of stoneworkers" <note>original: *λιθουργῶν κατοικίαν παλαιάν* (lithourgōn katoikian palaian)</note> according to Strabo in Book XVII...

This *Eben Rhodam* writes on the authority of *Kircher*. <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath who published extensively on Egyptology and "Egyptian secrets."</note> To what do you attribute those things? To what do you attribute the study of anatomy, familiar to the Kings of Egypt according to *Pliny*? To what do you attribute the art of statuary, derived from the Egyptians by the Greeks? Concerning this, *Diodorus* again says: *It is well known,* he says, *that all <term>sculptors<note>original Greek: ἀγάλματοποιοὺς (agalmatopoious) — makers of cult images or statues.</note></term> of any great value frequented Egypt, and among others Teracles and Theodorus, who completed that statue of the Pythian Apollo for the Samians.* Let us close this scene with the words of *Diodorus Siculus* in his *Antiquities, Book II*: *We also see,* he says, *that the arts among the Egyptians are remarkably cultivated and brought to the highest peak.* Moreover, *Diodorus* himself had seen Egypt when Julius and Octavian Augustus were governing the Roman world in a most flourishing state of affairs and arts. I ask that the great *Conringius* <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a famous German scholar who doubted the ancient Egyptian origins of chemistry. The author is directly debating him here.</note> examine these and similar facts, so that he might acknowledge that something else besides politics, mathematics, religion, and magic lay hidden in Egypt. Let him see that, however much he may resist, the wisdom of the Egyptians was more illustrious than that of the Greeks. *Diodorus* states this most clearly.

# ON THE ORIGIN AND
->PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY<-

<margin>
Greek science owes its origin to the Egyptians
</margin>
*In Book II, Chapter VI, he says: Orpheus, Musæus, Daedalus, Homer, Lycurgus, Solon, Plato, Pythagoras, Eudoxus, and Democritus the Abderite <note>Democritus of Abdera, the philosopher famous for the atomic theory.</note> set out for Egypt. In Egypt, they certainly perceived all those things which made them admirable among the Greeks.*

<margin>
The Greeks obtained only the more rudimentary parts of Egyptian Philosophy.
</margin>
And yet, these Greeks perceived only the scraps and almost the toys of the Egyptians, while the "Barbarians" <note>original: *Barbaris*. In the classical and early modern sense, this refers to non-Greeks—here specifically the Egyptians—whom the author views as the true masters of ancient knowledge.</note> kept the greatest part for themselves, as is proven by Strabo and others. And why does Conringius <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681) was a prominent German scholar who challenged the "Hermetic" tradition and argued that chemistry did not originate in ancient Egypt.</note> extol the Greeks so much? They were indeed industrious men, but they were dependent on the whims of others, plucking only mutilated bits from the geniuses of others and thrusting them upon their own country as if they were miracles, when they were rarely able to grasp the whole, true, and solid knowledge. Hence that Saitic <note>From the Egyptian city of Sais.</note> priest of the Egyptians, as recorded in Plato's *Timaeus*: *“O Solon, Solon,” he said, “you Greeks are always children; no one among you is an old man, nor is there any kind of ancient discipline among you.”* <note>This famous passage suggests that Greek culture was young and lacked the deep, recorded history and science of the Egyptians.</note> Hence Eusebius in his *Preparation for the Gospel*, Book X: *“We find,” he says, “that the most ancient Greeks, by wandering through remote regions with the greatest labor, fashioned their own Theology from the scraps of the teachings they begged for, in which the Barbarians flourished.”* And hence it is that we do not read by chance that either Pythagoras, or Solon, or Plato, or any other of the most ancient Greeks understood the Egyptian hieroglyphics, even though they were visible to everyone’s eyes on so many obelisks. Diodorus rightly says in Book IV: *“The letters which they call sacred (that is, hieroglyphics) are known only to the priests, having been received privately from their parents.”*

<margin>
Why did the ancient Greeks notice so little of Chemistry in Egypt?
</margin>
However, someone more spirited might object that it seems unlikely that Chemistry flourished in Egypt long ago; for otherwise, Plato, Solon, Pythagoras, Diodorus, and almost countless Greeks who once traveled through Egypt on anxious journeys would have discovered some of those mysteries, and in their writings [they would have mentioned] certain things beyond...

<meta>This page continues the argument that Greek philosophy and science were not original inventions but were borrowed from the older, more secretive traditions of the Egyptians and Hebrews. It picks up from a discussion about the Greek philosophers' travels to the East.</meta>

# THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

[...scarcely] left any traces. Strabo will parry this blow for my benefit and shatter that flashy weapon: "Eudoxus," he says in *Geography, Book XVII*, "set out for Egypt with Plato, and both spent thirteen years in the company of the Priests, as some have recorded. These same Priests, though they excelled in the knowledge of heavenly matters, kept it among their secrets and did not deign to share it with anyone; nevertheless, overcome by time and devotion, they revealed some precepts, while the Barbarians <note>original: *Barbaris*. In this context, the author uses the classical term for non-Greeks—here specifically the Egyptians—to emphasize that they were the keepers of knowledge the Greeks sought.</note> meanwhile hid most things entirely." It is clear that the Greeks were regarded as being among the profane masses, much like those whom the Eleusinian mysteries <note>The secret religious rites of ancient Greece which were closed to the uninitiated.</note> shut out from their sacred rituals; and yet these most clever Greeks believed themselves to be friends admitted into the Egyptians' inner circle. Hence, in Clement of Alexandria’s *Stromata* <note>original: *Stromatum*. Translated as "Miscellanies," a major work of early Christian philosophy.</note> Book I, Pythagoras is recorded as a disciple of Sonchedis, the Egyptian High Prophet; Plato as a disciple of Sechnuphis of Heliopolis; and Eudoxus as a disciple of Conuphis the Egyptian. Therefore, it is no wonder that Strabo discusses this in *Geography, Book XVII*: "At Heliopolis, we saw large houses in which the Priests had lived—men devoted to Astronomy and Philosophy; the dwellings where Eudoxus and Plato had stayed were also shown." However, it is very likely that Plato recognized not only Egyptians but also Hebrews as his masters. For Eusebius says in *Preparation for the Gospel*, Book XI, Chapter VIII: "It is certain that Plato was staying in Egypt at that time when the Hebrews, having been driven from their homeland a second time, were living in Egypt under Persian rule." To these, add those famous words of Origen in *Against Celsus*, Book I: "It seems to me," he says, "that he acts just as if someone traveling in Egypt—where the Egyptian sages, learned in their native literature, philosophize much about their ancestral gods, while the common people hear fables of which they do not know the underlying reason, yet nevertheless take great pleasure in their knowledge of them—were to think he had exhausted all the wisdom of the Egyptians from the conversations of the common folk, when he..."

The author refers to non-Greeks, particularly Egyptians.</note>—who dealt with Divine matters [that is, great and wonderful things], concealed the principles of things <note>original: *τὰς ἀρχὰς τῶν πραγμάτων ἀπεκρύψαντο*</note>; instead, they handed down the truth through enigmas, signs, symbols, allegories, metaphors, and figures of speech."

The words of Diodorus Siculus <note>A Greek historian of the 1st century BCE.</note> fit this point well: "What the priests [namely, the Egyptians]," he says, "know as secrets, they do not wish to spread to many, lest the truth become known; they even impose a penalty on those who would betray these things to the common people." <meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of ancient wisdom, moving from the Greek philosophers mentioned on the previous page to the education of Moses in Egypt. It addresses the debate over whether Moses studied common sciences or secret, priestly arts.</meta>

-># ON THE ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]<-

...also soft, effeminate, and lingering upon the eyes and ears, which are neither for common use, nor deserve to be. For that masked <note>The author uses "personatus" (masked) because the work *Questions and Responses to the Orthodox* was traditionally attributed to the early Christian writer Justin Martyr but is now believed by scholars to be the work of a later, anonymous author (Pseudo-Justin).</note> *Justin Martyr, author of Questions and Responses to the Orthodox*, is greatly mistaken <margin>Was he an astrologer?</margin> when he teaches that Moses did not at all learn Astrology and Geometry from the Egyptians as if they were common <term>proletarian: here meaning arts practiced by the lower classes or commoners</term> arts, but only hieroglyphic mathematics, to be offered only to the initiated in the more secret inner sanctuaries <term>adyta: the most sacred, private parts of a temple, forbidden to the uninitiated</term>. He is mistaken first because Astrology and Geometry were by no means numbered among the common arts of the Egyptians; they were understood only by Kings and Priests, as is clear from *Diodorus*, *Pliny*, and others. *Origen* correctly says in his *Commentary on the 3rd chapter of Romans*: *Among the Egyptians,* he says, *no one explores the secrets of Astrology unless they have undergone circumcision. No one learns hieroglyphic letters unless he is circumcised <note>original: "recutitus," a Latin term literally meaning "re-skinned," used here to refer to those who have undergone circumcision.</note>.* Furthermore, knowing those vain trappings <note>original: "syrmata." This refers to the long, trailing robes worn by tragic actors, used here metaphorically to describe the elaborate but empty superstitions that often accompanied ancient astrology.</note> that usually accompany astrology is a fault to no one—it is only a fault to have practiced them. Therefore, the knowledge of profane arts in Moses cannot be blamed, since no one has rightly reproached him for the use of them. *Clement of Alexandria in book 1 of his Stromata* defines Moses's Egyptian knowledge more fully: *When he was already older in age, he was taught Arithmetic and Geometry, Rhythm and Harmony, and furthermore Medicine along with Music by those who were more distinguished among the Egyptians; and besides these, that philosophy delivered through symbols and signs, which they show in hieroglyphic letters. But the Greeks in Egypt taught him the rest of the circle of learning as if he were a royal youth, as Philo says in the Life of Moses. Moreover, he learned the letters of the Egyptians, and the science of heavenly things from the Chaldeans and from the Egyptians.* <margin>Was he a grammarian?</margin> From this, it is said in the account of his deeds that he was learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians. Indeed, *Eupolemus* says in his book *On the Kings of Judea* that *Moses was the first wise man, and he first delivered Grammar to the Jews, and to the Phoenicians...*

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the lineage of ancient wisdom, specifically refuting the claim that Moses was indebted to Greek learning. It establishes a "genealogy of knowledge" that traces the arts and sciences from God to Adam, through Noah and Abraham, to the Egyptians and eventually the Greeks.</meta>

-># ON THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY<-

...[the Phoenicians] received [these] from the Jews, and the Greeks from the Phoenicians. I cannot here sufficiently wonder at the character of *Johann Heinrich Ursinus*, <margin>*Ursinus is criticized.*</margin> who—to prove his claim that *Moses* learned nothing of great importance from the Egyptians except the arts of governing and (quoting *Ezekiel the Tragedian*) "royal education" <note>original Greek: παιδεύματα βασιλικὰ (paideumata basilika)</note>—wrote this among other things in his book on *Trismegistus*, etc. When *Clement of Alexandria* had recited this passage from Philo, he added a censorial mark from *Eupolemus* <note>Eupolemus was a Jewish historian of the 2nd century BCE who argued that Moses invented the alphabet.</note>. First, *Clement* does not simply cite Philo’s words, but adds from others: <margin>*Moses as Physician.*</margin> *Did Moses also learn medicine along with music from the distinguished men of the Egyptians?* <note>original Greek: "Μωσῆς ἔτι τὴν ἰατρικὴν ἅμα καὶ Μουσικὴν παρὰ τοῖς διαπρέπoυσιν Αἰγυπτίων ἐδιδάσκετο;"</note> I ask that *Ursinus* henceforth recognize that *Moses* was also numbered among the Physicians. Second, *Clement* by no means denies that *Moses* learned all those things which *Philo* relates; but rather, it is the claim that *Moses* was taught the "encyclopedia of arts" <term>encyclopedia: here referring to the "circle of learning" or the standard curriculum of liberal arts</term> by the Greeks—that <margin>*Moses learned nothing from the Greeks.*</margin> is precisely what he, following *Eupolemus*, most providently denies, being well aware that Greek letters were derived from the Phoenicians. (*Josephus, Book 1 Against Apion: The Greeks are believed to have had the most ancient use of letters from the Phoenicians, and they boast that they learned from Cadmus.*)

The Phoenicians [received them] from the Jews; the Jews from *Moses*; *Moses* from the Egyptians; the Egyptians from the Chaldeans and *Abraham* [who, in the judgment of *Artapanus* as cited in *Eusebius*, was the first to teach the Egyptians Astrology]; *Abraham* from the descendants of *Noah* and *Seth*; these from *Adam*, and he from God. Regarding *Abraham*, *Josephus* writes explicitly in *Antiquities Book 1, Chapter 9*: <margin>*Abraham refutes the Egyptians.*</margin> *The King, having gifted Abraham a great sum of money, gave him the power to meet with the most excellent and learned of the Egyptians; by which it happened that, on account of his virtue, he came into the highest esteem. # ON THE ORIGIN AND...

...even those effeminate and shameful things that distract the eyes and ears, which are not for common use, nor do they deserve to be. For that masked *Justin Martyr*, <note>original: "personatus ille Justinus Martyr." The author uses "masked" to indicate that the work "Questions and Responses to the Orthodox" was falsely attributed to the famous martyr.</note> the *Author of the Questions and Responses to the Orthodox*, is greatly hallucinating when he teaches that Moses by no means drank in Astrology and Geometry from the Egyptians—viewing them as low-class arts—but only the "hieroglyphic mathematics" intended only for the initiated in the more secret inner sanctuaries. <margin>Was he an Astrologer?</margin> This is because Astrology and Geometry were by no means numbered among the common arts of the Egyptians; they were understood only by Kings and Priests, as is clear from *Diodorus*, *Pliny*, and others. *Origen* notes correctly in his *Commentary on the 3rd chapter of Romans*: "Among the Egyptians," he says, "no one explores the secrets of Astrology unless they have undergone circumcision. No one learns hieroglyphic letters unless they are circumcised." <note>original: "recutitus," a Latin term literally meaning "re-skinned," used as a synonym for circumcised.</note> Furthermore, knowing those vain trappings that usually accompany Astrology is not considered a vice in anyone; it is the *practice* of them that is. Therefore, the knowledge of "profane" arts in Moses cannot be criticized, since no one can rightfully reproach him for the *use* of them. *Clement of Alexandria*, in the *1st book of his Stromata* [Miscellanies], defines Moses’s Egyptian knowledge more fully: "When he was of a greater age, he was taught Arithmetic and Geometry, Rhythm and Harmony, and furthermore Medicine along with Music, by those who were more distinguished among the Egyptians; and additionally, that Philosophy which is handed down through symbols and signs, which they show in hieroglyphic letters. However, the Greeks taught him another circle of learning <term>circle of learning: the "encyclopedia" or standard curriculum of liberal arts</term> in Egypt, as if he were a royal child, as Philo says in the *Life of Moses*. He learned the letters of the Egyptians and the knowledge of heavenly things from the Chaldeans and from the Egyptians. Whence it is said in the record of his deeds that he was <margin>Was he a Grammarian?</margin> *instructed in all the wisdom of the Egyptians.*" <note>This refers to the Biblical account in Acts 7:22.</note> *Eupolemus*, in his book on the *Kings of Judea*, says that Moses was the first wise man, and first handed down Grammar <note>In this context, "Grammar" refers to the invention of the alphabet and the rules of language.</note> to the Jews, and then to the Phoeni-

### PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.

...[that the] sciences <note>The text begins mid-word, continuing from the previous page's discussion on the origins of knowledge.</note> were received from the *Jews*, while the *Greeks* received them from the *Phoenicians*. 

<margin>Ursinus is critiqued.</margin>
I cannot sufficiently wonder here at the spirit of *Johann Heinrich Ursinus*, <note>Ursinus (1608–1667) was a German scholar who argued against the idea that ancient wisdom—specifically "Hermetic" or Egyptian knowledge—was the source of true religion or science.</note> who, in order to teach what he intends—namely, that *Moses* learned nothing much from the *Egyptians* except the arts of governing, and from *Ezekiel the Poet* <note>original: "Ezechielo Tragico," referring to Ezekiel the Tragedian, a Jewish poet who wrote "The Exodus" in Greek verse.</note> the "royal education"—writes, among other things in his book on *Trismegistus*, etc.: "When *Clement of Alexandria* had recited this passage from *Philo*, he added a critical mark from *Eupolemus*." <note>The "critical mark" or *virgula censoria* was a symbol used by ancient scholars to mark a passage as doubtful or needing correction.</note> 

First, *Clement* does not simply cite *Philo’s* words, but adds from others: <margin>Moses the Physician.</margin> "Was *Moses* also taught medicine together with music by the distinguished men of the Egyptians?" <note>original: "Μωσῆς ἔτι τὴν ἰατρικὴν ἅμα καὶ Μουσικὴν παρὰ τοῖς διαπρέπουσιν Αἰγυπτίων ἐδιδάσκετο;"</note> I pray that *Ursinus* may henceforth recognize that *Moses* was also numbered among the Physicians.

<margin>Moses learned nothing from the Greeks.</margin>
Secondly, *Clement* by no means denies that *Moses* learned all those things that *Philo* relates; but rather, that *Moses* was taught the "encyclopedia of arts" <note>The full circle of education or liberal arts.</note> by the Greeks—that is precisely what he denies, most providently, being well aware that the letters of the Greeks were derived from the Phoenicians. (*Josephus, Book 1 Against Apion: The Greeks are believed to have had the most ancient use of letters from the Phoenicians, and they boast that they learned from Cadmus.*) The Phoenicians received them from the Jews, the Jews from *Moses*, *Moses* from the *Egyptians*, the *Egyptians* from the *Chaldeans* and *Abraham* [who, according to *Artapanus* in the works of *Eusebius*, was the first to teach the *Egyptians* Astrology], *Abraham’s* knowledge came from the descendants of Noah and *Seth*: theirs from *Adam*, and his from *God*.

<margin>Abraham refutes the Egyptians.</margin>
Regarding *Abraham*, *Josephus* says explicitly in the *Antiquities of the Jews, Book 1, Chapter 9*: "The King, having gifted *Abraham* with a great sum of money, gave him the power to meet with the most excellent and learned Egyptians. <margin>The Egyptians learned arithmetic and astronomy from Abraham.</margin>

Eupolemus, as cited in Eusebius’s *Preparation for the Gospel*, Book 9, Chapter 17: <note>Eupolemus was a 2nd-century BCE Jewish historian who wrote in Greek; Eusebius was a 4th-century Christian bishop and historian.</note> "Abraham was born in Camarina—which is a Babylonian city, and which some call Urfen, and the Greeks call Chaldaeopolis <note>original: "Chaldaeopolin," meaning "City of the Chaldaeans."</note>—and since he surpassed everyone in wisdom, he actually discovered Astrology and the Chaldaean science. # ON THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

...if Conring <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681) was a German polymath who often argued against the antiquity of alchemy.</note> is pleased to speak in fables. For he says: *"It is much less important that we dwell on the fables of some, as if Moses, in breaking up the golden calf, acted as a Chemist."* But why call these "fables," Conring? I would dare to guarantee that no one except a noble Chemist could achieve what the words of the Spirit of God teach that Moses performed here, if they are to be interpreted as they stand. Let us hear *Exodus 32: Moses took that golden calf which they had made, burnt it with fire, and ground it into fine powder, and scattered it upon the water, and made the children of Israel drink it.* Let us hear *Deuteronomy 9: I took the calf, says Moses, and burned it with fire, and broke it into very small pieces until it was like dust, which I threw into the stream flowing down from the mountain.*

From this, there were those whom Conring attacks, who concluded that Moses, by the Chemical art, had so divided the gold that it was reduced to a fine powder dissolvable in water; for gold cannot in any way be burnt into powder by itself, nor can its powder dissolve in flowing water or be drunk. Sennert <note>Daniel Sennert (1572–1637) was a physician and chemist who sought to reconcile Aristotelian philosophy with atomism and alchemy.</note>, in his work *On Agreement and Disagreement*, suggests that the word *saraph* <note>Hebrew: שרף (*saraph*)</note> denotes not only "to burn," but also "to cast," "to melt," and to crush into powder by any means. Let us grant this to Sennert [although not only did the Seventy Interpreters <note>The Septuagint, the influential 3rd-century BCE Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note> in *Exodus 32* expressly render it as "he burned it in fire" <note>original Greek: κατέκαυσεν αὐτὸν ἐν πυρὶ (*katekaysen auton en pyri*)</note>, and likewise in *Deuteronomy 9* "he burned it in fire" <note>original Greek: κατέκαυσεν αὐτὸν ἐν πυρὶ (*katekaysen auton en pyri*)</note>, and Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629), a celebrated Hebraist.</note> and others explain it as "to burn up" or "to set on fire"], yet this does not so much loosen the knot as tie it tighter. For no one, by melting, liquidizing, or burning with a simple fire, could so eviscerate the innermost parts of gold that it becomes drinkable and does not immediately sink to the bottom when thrown into a river, unless by Chemistry and some more secret Guide. Let that gold be reduced, if there is time, to the finest powder either by filing, pounding, or grinding; let it be burnt even ten times, then poured into water—it will seek the...

<meta>This page continues the argument that gold and other metals are "corruptible" (capable of being broken down or consumed) by providing historical accounts of animals eating or gnawing on metallic objects, refuting the idea that gold is indestructible.</meta>

# PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY
G
<meta>catchword: almost</meta>

...metals, and that metallic juices are corroded. Furthermore, regarding this, *Julius Obsequens* <note>A 4th-century Roman author who compiled a list of "prodigies" or supernatural omens.</note> says in *Observation 44*, that during the consulship of *Servilius Caepio* and *Servilius Geminus*, crows on the Capitoline Hill not only swallowed gold with their beaks but actually ate it. Likewise, mice at *Antium* gnawed on a golden crown. The same author in *Observation 39* notes that mice gnawed gold in the *Temple of Jupiter*. In *Observation 89*, during the consulship of *Publius Africanus* and *Gaius Laelius*, mice gnawed sacred gold at *Frufino*. While the credulity of the superstitious might judge these events to be omens, we have learned that one should not rashly call upon a "God from the machine" <note>original: *Deum è machinâ*. A reference to the theatrical "deus ex machina," meaning the author believes these are natural chemical occurrences, not divine miracles.</note> to explain them. 

On this matter, *Borellus* <note>Pierre Borel (c. 1620–1671), a French physician and chemist.</note> says in his *Medical Histories and Observations, Century III, Observation 85*: "The Lord *la Pierre*, a nobleman of Castres and a director and guardian of war machines, relates that mice destroyed and devoured over one hundred pounds of lead bullets meant for cannons." This same *Borellus* confesses that a tin vessel of his was corroded by mice during a time of plague. It was also observed in Florence that the stomach of an ostrich finally overcomes the surface of iron. Thus we also read of silver shields at Lavinium being gnawed by mice, and iron being gnawed in the iron-workshops among the *Chalybes* <note>An ancient tribe of the Black Sea region famous in antiquity for their mastery of ironworking.</note>. 

One might be inclined to think that the singular sweetness found in lead and tin—a quality which the chemical fire detects even without the mixture of acids (though the matter remains largely unproven regarding other metals)—is pleasing to the palate of mice, even though it threatens human life like a poison if taken too copiously into the mouth. <note>The author is likely referring to lead acetate, also known as "sugar of lead," which has a sweet taste but is highly toxic.</note> 

I will pass over the fact that not all gold is as stubborn against the teeth as our European gold. An eyewitness, *Flacourt* <note>Étienne de Flacourt (1607–1660), a French governor of Madagascar who wrote a history of the island.</note>, appropriately notes in his *History of the Island of Madagascar*: "What is remarkable," <note>original: *Ce qui est de remarquable...*</note> he says, "is that there is no great leader in all this island who does not have gold from his ancestors, and this gold is in no way similar to the gold we have in Europe, being paler, but..."

But it is time to draw in these sails <note>original: *contrahenda jam vela istæc*. A nautical metaphor meaning to conclude this particular line of discussion.</note>.

But one might opportunely ask by whose authorship Chemistry first began in Egypt. Indeed, we do not hesitate to ascribe the origin of both Medicine and Chemistry to Hermes (or Mercury), despite whatever Conring <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a German physician and scholar who was notoriously skeptical of the "Hermetic" origins of science.</note> might argue against both. He is driven by such a hatred for Hermes that he leaves it undecided whether there was once a Hermes of human blood, or rather some Demon, and so on. He moves heaven and earth <note>original: *supera & infera movenda*</note> just to find a pretext for his indignation. But we move on. The Egyptian priests acknowledged Hermes as the discoverer of all disciplines and arts, according to *Diodorus Siculus, Book II, Chapter I* and from *Strabo, Book XVII*. That Medicine and Chemistry are among the Egyptian arts has already been shown; and Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath who studied Egyptian culture.</note> demonstrated this laboriously in his *Egyptian Oedipus*. Again, *Diodorus* in *Antiquities, Book I, Chapter II* says: "The Egyptians honor Mercury as the discoverer of many things by which the lives of men might be sustained." He was especially an observer of the courses of the stars and invented the harmonies of voices. They also maintain he was the inventor of wrestling, of numbers, and of the medical art for caring for bodies.

...to care for the formation <note>original: πλάσεως ἐπιμεληθῆναι (*plaseos epimēlethenai*), referring to the physical development or "shaping" of the human form.</note>; for certainly the fitting formation of the body <note>original: πρέπουσα πλάσις σώματος (*prepousa plasis sōmatos*)</note> is the work of a Physician. The same is stated by Diodorus <note>Diodorus Siculus, a Greek historian of the 1st century BCE.</note> in Book I, Chapter II. He says that the Egyptians also assert that Isis was the inventor of many medicines for diseases; but from where did Isis draw her knowledge? Diodorus responds: it is handed down that there exist in Nysa of Arabia the tombs of Isis and Osiris, and that a column dedicated to each stands there inscribed with sacred characters, and on the column of Isis these words are read: *I am Isis, Queen of Egypt, taught by Mercury*, etc. 

Naturally, in that great magnitude of his genius, Hermes <note>The author uses Mercury and Hermes interchangeably here.</note> did not seem to diminish his own standing if he left to the Queen some part of his knowledge regarding Medicine so that she too might become famous; he was well aware that the fairer sex <note>original: *sequiorem sexum*, literally "the later sex," a common early modern term for women.</note> also seeks laurels in this temple of the Muses. Following Isis, and struck by that same staff of praise <note>original: *thyrso*, the wand of Dionysus, used here as a metaphor for inspiration or divine calling.</note>, were Cleopatra in Egypt, Artemisia in Caria <note>Famous for building the Mausoleum and her knowledge of botany.</note>, and a certain Mary in Palestine <note>Refers to Maria Prophetissa, or Mary the Jewess, an early alchemical authority.</note> [whose writing was shown to me in Paris, although perhaps it is counterfeit]. 

And I will not even mention more recent women: Isabella Cortese <note>An Italian alchemist who published a popular book of "secrets" in 1561.</note> of the Italians, the Duchess d’Aiguillon <note>Marie de Vignerot, a French noblewoman known for her medical charities.</note> of the French, the Countess of Kent <note>Elizabeth Grey, whose medical recipes were published as "A Choice Manual."</note> of the British, Berta Friis of the Danes, Anna Wecker <note>Author of a 16th-century German medical cookbook.</note> of the Germans, and countless others made famous in the medical art through the assistance of their husbands and families. 

But let us return to Mercury. Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> writes clearly in *On the Mysteries of the Egyptians*: "The Egyptian writers," he says, "believing all things were discovered by Mercury [therefore also Chemistry], also inscribed their own books to Mercury." To this point, Julius Maternus Firmicus <note>A 4th-century writer on astrology.</note> says in Book III, Chapter I, concerning Petosiris and Nechepso <note>Traditional founders of Egyptian astrology.</note>: "They wished the world to have a birth, following Aesculapius and Anubis,

to whom the most powerful divinity of Mercury committed the secrets of that science." # ON THE ORIGIN AND...
<page-num>52</page-num>
->**ON THE ORIGIN AND...**<-

For concerning *Apollo*, whom they likewise recognize as the first Master of Medicine, the matter is certain; for *Diodorus Siculus* <note>A Greek historian of the 1st century BCE who wrote the *Bibliotheca historica*.</note> states most clearly: *Isis, he says, did not only restore to life her son Horus, who had been killed by the Titans and found in the waters, but rendered him immortal.* They interpret this Horus as *Apollo*, and say that he was instructed in the art of healing and divination by his Mother, Isis, and brought much benefit to mankind through both oracles and remedies. Therefore, Horus or Apollo [from whom the writer of hieroglyphics, *Horapollo*, takes his name] drew his knowledge of Medicine from *Isis*; and *Isis*, as noted before, from Mercury <note>Hermes</note>. It is so evident, not only as *Clement of Alexandria* <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150–215 CE) who sought to unite Greek philosophy with Christian doctrine.</note> beautifully demonstrates, that *Moses* is more ancient than most of the Greek Gods, but also that *Mercury* must be hailed as more ancient than the other inventors of Medicine, and indeed as their Master. For no one has doubted that Hermes is older than even the ancient Apis.

Indeed, *Clement of Alexandria* teaches in the *first book of the Stromata* <note>The *Stromata* or "Miscellanies" is one of Clement's major works.</note> that Medicine was first invented by a certain Egyptian named Apis, and they say it was later increased by Aesculapius. But that must be explained in the same way as another statement by the same Clement: *that the Noricans <note>People of a Celtic kingdom in the Eastern Alps.</note> were the first to possess the method of smelting copper and refining iron*; this is stated, of course, without prejudice to the authority of Tubal-cain <note>A biblical figure described in Genesis as the first smith.</note> and Hermes, who stand as the very first of all. *Clement of Alexandria* himself, in the *first book of the Stromata*, citing the *Arcadics of Aristippus*, teaches that the famous Apis, King of the Argives, founded Memphis; from this, it is proven that Hermes, or Canaan, being of Memphis and following in sequence, is far more ancient than Apis.

Clement also records in the *sixth book of the Stromata* that Hermes wrote on medical matters among other subjects. There are, he says, *forty-two books of Mercury which are highly necessary; of these, thirty-six, which contain the entire philosophy of the Egyptians, are learned by those mentioned before. The remaining six books are learned by the* Shrine-bearer <note>original: ὁ Πασθοφόρος (*ho Pasthophoros*), a class of Egyptian priests who carried the sacred shrines in processions.</note>*, as they pertain to Medicine; namely, concerning the construction*—
