...the composition of the body, on diseases, on instruments, on medicines, on the eyes, and finally on women. <note>This concludes a list of the forty-two sacred books of Hermes Trismegistus, which covered all human knowledge including law, religion, and science.</note>

<margin>Hermes</margin>
That Chemistry <note>In this context, *Chemia* refers to the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy—the study of transmuting substances and understanding the nature of matter.</note> owes its origins to Hermes is established not only because Egyptian wisdom itself is called "Hermetic" (as Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a famous Jesuit scholar who wrote extensively on Egyptian hieroglyphs and oriental studies.</note> teaches), but it can also be easily gathered from that inscription in Coptic letters carved on a stone near Memphis. <margin>Author of *Aegyptiaca*</margin>This was described by Michael Schatta, a Copt from Memphis; the words, rendered into English <note>original Latin: *Cælum sursum, cælum deorsum: astra sursum, astra deorsum: quod sursum, omne id deorsum: hæc cape, & beaberis.*</note>, read as follows: *Heaven is above, heaven is below; the stars are above, the stars are below; everything that is above, all that is below: take these, and you shall be blessed.* These words are as similar to the *Emerald Tablet* <note>The *Tabula Smaragdina*, a foundational text of Hermeticism and alchemy said to contain the secret of the "prima materia."</note> of Hermes as one egg is to another, so much so that they seem to have been transcribed from that tablet onto the stone. But more on the Emerald Tablet shortly.

<margin>Of Chemistry.</margin>
We shall also establish from the *Alexandrian Chronicle* <note>The *Chronicon Paschale*, a 7th-century universal chronicle of the world.</note> that Hermes, among his other great intellectual achievements, was skilled in the nature of gold. It states: *They affirm that Janus was born of Picus Jove, the King of Italy and the West; the name of the planet Mercury <note>Hermes</note> was bestowed upon him. He discovered gold and the method for smelting it; and having gathered his possessions and a great weight of gold, he fled to Egypt. There he was received by King Misremus (that is, his brother Misraim), a descendant of Ham; since he was held in great honor because of his wisdom and various arts, he eventually succeeded the king upon his death.* But why do we hesitate to claim Chemistry for Hermes on the authority of Homer? Lest we think that the storyteller Melesigenes <note>A traditional name for Homer, referencing his supposed birth near the river Meles in Smyrna.</note> was always merely trifling, it must be noted at the outset from *Diodorus Siculus* (Book 1, Chapter 2) that *Homer, having traveled to the Egyptians, learned many things from their priests.* For indeed, he learned not only that mystical union of Mars and Venus in the eighth book of the *Odyssey* <note>original: *Odyss. ϑ.* (theta being the 8th letter of the Greek alphabet). This myth was often interpreted alchemically as the joining of iron (Mars) and copper (Venus).</note>; he learned not only those profound subtleties regarding the "black beasts" and the "black golden field"—matters which can be demonstrated to the eye by a Chemist alone—

And the things added thereafter are the most manifest traces of *Chemistry*, unless we are blind. Here, lest anyone insist that it is doubtful whether those Hymns sewn onto the Homeric work actually owe their birth to Homer, it will be enough for me in this place that they are held as Homeric by most and are of great antiquity. For no mortal today can know precisely which parts are truly Homer's, even in the *Odyssey* and the *Iliad*, nor was this sufficiently understood even in ancient Greece. Nor is there time here to recall from Laërtius <note>Diogenes Laërtius, a biographer of the Greek philosophers.</note> and others how most writings, even those attributed to *Aristotle*, have been brought under suspicion and placed among the spurious works. 

But setting aside the hymns of Homer, if we look with an attentive eye even at the first verse of Book 24 <note>The Greek letter ω (omega) denotes the 24th book of the Odyssey.</note> of the *Odyssey*:

For truly, if Mercury <term>the quicksilver of the alchemists</term> is ingeniously joined to the prepared "males" of the metals <note>In alchemical theory, metals like iron or gold were often classified as "masculine" or possessing a "masculine" spirit.</note>, it easily calls forth their souls by a preceding sublimation and carries them up with itself. 

That bond which Otus and Ephialtes, the sons of Aloeus, cast upon Mars in Book 5 <note>The Greek letter ε (epsilon) denotes the 5th book of the Iliad.</note> of the *Iliad* agrees kindly with this; Mars was finally vindicated from that bond after thirteen full months, but only by the help of Mercury. *At last*, says the Poet, *Mercury stole Mars away*, that is, he raised him into the heights. From here, as Laërtius witnesses, the Mer- of Pythagoras...

<header>PROGRESS IN CHEMISTRY.</header>
<page-num>55</page-num>

—cury is called the **steward of souls**, <note>original Greek: ταμίας τῶν ψυχῶν</note> and that, unless I am mistaken, most appropriately; and in Homer's *Odyssey*, book 10, he is called **with the golden wand**. <note>original Greek: χρυσόῤῥαπις</note> Therefore, that saying of **Servius** <note>A 4th-century commentator on Virgil.</note> in his commentary on the first book of Virgil’s *Georgics*: *It must be known regarding the five planets, that two are harmful, Mars and Saturn; two are good, Jupiter and Venus; but Mercury is such as the one he is joined to, whence he is also imagined to be the minister of the Gods, because he obeys their power*, should by no means be understood only regarding the celestial planets, but most fittingly also regarding the terrestrial ones <note>The "terrestrial planets" refer to the seven metals of alchemy, where Mercury (quicksilver) acts as a transformative agent that takes on the properties of other metals.</note>. But more on these things elsewhere.

<margin>Hermes</margin> Indeed, how great that Egyptian Hero **Hermes** was can be judged even from the fact that Egypt itself is called **Hermochymis** <note>original Greek: Ἑρμοχύμις; a portmanteau suggesting "The Chemistry of Hermes."</note> by Stephanus of Byzantium <note>A 6th-century author of a geographical dictionary.</note> under the entry *Aegyptus*, or, as **Vossius** <note>Gerardus Vossius, a Dutch scholar of the 17th century.</note> prudently corrects in his work *On Idolatry*, **Hermochemis**. <note>original Greek: Ἑρμοχήμις; linking Hermes to "Khem," the ancient name for Egypt and the root of the word "Chemistry."</note>

<margin>By the Nations</margin> To this is added the judgment of other nations concerning Hermes. The **Gauls**, according to **Julius Caesar’s** *Commentaries on the Gallic War*, Book 7: *They worship the god Mercury most of all; they report him to be the inventor of all arts.* Whence did the Gauls get this? Did some rare messenger returning to the Gauls from the remains of the defeated Brennus <note>A Gallic chieftain who sacked Rome.</note> imbue them with that reverence for Hermes? Or did the Druids drink it in from the remnants of the Gauls returning from Galatia? In Greece, too,
<margin>adored.</margin> Mercury was worshipped. **Strabo**, in Book 8, says: *Toward Olympia, there are many gifts to Mercury along the roads*, that is, *Hermeia* <note>original Greek: Ἑρμεῖα; stone heaps or pillars used as road markers and shrines.</note>. The Hebrews also have a proverb: *to scatter stones in Merkolis*, **that is**, in honor of Mercury, concerning which **Drusius** <note>Johannes Drusius, a Flemish Protestant scholar.</note> wrote. To this, that saying of **Solomon** in *Proverbs 26[:8]*—*as he who throws a stone into a Margemah-heap* <note>The Hebrew word "Margemah" (מרגמה) was often interpreted by Renaissance scholars as referring to a "Mercury-heap" or "Herm."</note>—is believed to have referred. That this practice of throwing stones was also familiar to the Indians in honor of Mercury is testified by **Vincent of Beauvais** in Book 4 of the *Historical Mirror*. The Saxons erected a column to **Hermes** called the **Irmensul**, as if it were *Hermens-Seul* <note>Hermes's Pillar.</note>; this was destroyed by Charlemagne after he captured Eresburg. The people of Ghent built an altar to Hermes, as reported by **Theodoric, Abbot of St. Trudo**; and Walcheren in Zeeland dedicated to him an ido—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the identity of Hermes Trismegistus, moving from his symbolic/astrological associations to his historical reality as a human being. It counters the skepticism of modern scholars by citing ancient authorities who regarded Hermes as a flesh-and-blood teacher and inventor.</meta>

<header>ON THE ORIGIN AND PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY</header>
<page-num>56</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Smyrna,</meta>

...idol, as it is found in the *Chronicle of Zeeland*. Regarding the reasons for the stones thrown at the statues of Mercury <note>The "herms" or "mercurial statues" were often surrounded by heaps of stones (hermaia) left by travelers.</note>, however not inappropriately commented upon by both **Cornutus** in *On the Nature of the Gods* and **John of Salisbury** in the *Policraticus*, they would undoubtedly have cleared the matter up more correctly with the help of **Chemistry**.

<margin>Nevertheless, Hermes was a man.</margin> Since authors report that altars, columns, and statues were erected to Mercury, **Conringius** <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a German physician and scholar who was skeptical of the ancient origins of alchemy.</note> took the opportunity to doubt: *whether he was once a man, or from the phalanx of demons*; although **Plutarch**, **Diodorus**, and others dispel every doubt—namely, that he was a man of the most celestial genius and a benefactor, the sacred secretary of **Osiris**, the teacher of **Isis**, indeed with a shorter arm, just as **Typhon** was red, **Mars** whiter, **Osiris** blacker, etc. 

**Clement of Alexandria** also deserves to be heard in the first book of the *Stromata*: *Furthermore*, he says, *among those who were once men in Egypt, but in the opinion of men became Gods, were Hermes of Thebes and Aesculapius of Memphis, and again Tiresias and Manto at Thebes.* <note>original: "Quin etiam ex iis, qui aliquando fuere homines in Ægypto, opinione autem hominum Dii..."</note> Likewise **Augustine** in Book 8 of *The City of God*: *They do not notice, in all the literature of the pagans, that gods who were not men are either not found or are scarcely found.* <note>original: "Non attendunt, in omnibus literis paganorum... Deos, qui non fuerint homines."</note> **Plato** in the *Philebus*: *After some God or a Divine man devised the unprobed voice, such as a certain Theuth is said to have been among the Egyptians.* <note>"Theuth" or "Thoth" is the Egyptian name for the figure the Greeks called Hermes.</note> 

Therefore Hermes, or Mercury, is believed by Clement to be **Theban**, that is, born in Egyptian Thebes; to **Sanchuniathon** (cited in **Eusebius**) he is a Phoenician, to others a Greek. This diversity of opinion finally drove the prone-to-darker-judgments Conringius to write confidently: *it cannot even be safely believed that there was any Hermes Trismegistus among the Egyptians, the author of much wisdom among them.* Is it so, most excellent Conring, that we must call into doubt whether **Homer** existed in the nature of things, simply because the Greek cities debated over his homeland—

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Hermes Trismegistus’s historical existence. The author argues that a lack of consensus on a figure's birthplace does not negate their reality, comparing Hermes to other disputed historical and legendary figures.</meta>

<header>ON THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY</header>
<page-num>57</page-num>

->*Smyrna, Rhodes, Colophon, Salamis, Ios, Argos, Athens.*<- <note>These seven cities famously contested being the birthplace of the poet Homer.</note>

Should we think that **Thomas à Kempis** <note>Thomas à Kempis (c. 1380–1471), the monk traditionally credited with writing *The Imitation of Christ*.</note> never lived, simply because the homeland of that author is still a matter of dispute? Should we think there was no **Petronius Arbiter** <note>A Roman courtier and author during the reign of Nero.</note>, just because **Valesius** <note>Henri Valois (1603–1676), a French classical scholar.</note> recently claimed he was from Marseille, despite everyone else holding the contrary opinion? Have the centuries never seen a **Cadmus** <note>The legendary founder of Thebes, credited with introducing the alphabet to Greece.</note>, because **Diodorus** records him as originating from *Egyptian Thebes*, while others place him in *Phoenicia*? Indeed, because **Hermes** flourished not long after the Great Flood—a time when writers were rare or non-existent everywhere—it is no wonder that the descendants of the Egyptians harbor diverse opinions regarding his homeland. Since we are moved by arguments to be presented later to believe that **Hermes** was none other than **Canaan** <note>The grandson of Noah. Early modern scholars often tried to synchronize biblical genealogy with pagan mythology.</note>, we ask **Conringius** to precisely define for us the native soil of Canaan, and in return, we will promise to define the homeland of **Hermes** with the utmost accuracy.

<margin>Where Hermes was from.</margin>
What has already been said should suffice to overturn **Conringius’s** reasons and to show that both Medicine and Chemistry in Egypt proceeded from Hermes; however, as a small addition <note>original: "mantissæ loco," a Latin idiom for adding a "make-weight" or a little extra.</note>, we shall add a few points: To **Tertullian**, Hermes is the *Master of Natural Philosophers* <note>original: "Physicorum Magister." Tertullian was an early Christian author.</note> in his *Treatise on the Soul* (chapter 2) and *Against the Valentinians* (chapter 15). To **Diodorus Siculus** (chapter 16), he is the inventor of the olive tree. And when **Isis**, having been instructed by Mercury, restored her son **Horus** to life with a medicine of **Immortality** <note>original: "’Αθανασίας" (Athanasias)</note>, it follows clearly from this that **Mercury** was the original architect of that medicine. ### THE EGYPTIAN VULCAN

...of the Egyptians <note>The sentence starts mid-thought from the previous page.</note>, Vulcan, the inventor of fire, is to be counted as the first King of the Egyptians, and that Saturn succeeded him in power. The same is recorded by **Eusebius** <note>A 4th-century Christian historian</note> in *Book III, Chapter XI*: the Egyptians tell that the god **Cneph** <note>Kneph or Chnum, an Egyptian creator deity often depicted as a ram-headed potter.</note> produced an egg from his mouth, and from it was born another god whom they call **Phtha** <note>Ptah, the artisan-creator god of Memphis.</note>, and the Greeks call **Vulcan**; they interpret that egg as the world itself. But is Vulcan the author of every kind of fire? Not all, according to the authorities. For that purer and celestial fire, in the judgment of **Phurnutus** <note>Lucius Annaeus Cornutus, a Stoic philosopher known for interpreting myths.</note>, is **Jupiter** to the Greeks, named *Zeus* from the word *zeein* <note>original Greek: ζέειν, meaning "to glow" or "to boil."</note>. However, that more "impure" fire—whether it flashes as lightning among the clouds, or burns within houses, or shines forth in fire-vomiting mountains <note>volcanoes</note>, in sacrifices, and in the workshops of smiths—is **Vulcan**. He is called *Hephaistos* by the Greeks from the word *hephthai* <note>original Greek: ἥφθαι</note>, which means *to be kindled*. Furthermore, the fire of the hearths, especially the public ones, was called **Vesta**. In the meantime, it would not be out of place to inquire more carefully into who that Vulcan was who was worshiped in Egypt.

<margin>How many Vulcans there were of old.</margin>

**Cicero**, in *Book V of On the Nature of the Gods* <note>While modern editions of Cicero’s work have only three books, early modern scholars often used different divisions or referenced larger compendiums.</note>, counts four Vulcans: the first born of Heaven, who, living before the waves of the Deluge <note>The Great Flood of Noah.</note>, could be seen as **Tubal-cain** <note>The biblical descendant of Cain described in Genesis 4:22 as the "forger of all instruments of bronze and iron."</note>; the second born of the Nile, the protector of Egypt; the third born of Jupiter and Juno; the fourth born of Maenalius, the lord of the Vulcanian Islands <note>The Lipari Islands near Sicily, famous for volcanic activity.</note>, whom you might rightly judge to have been invented by the Greeks in imitation of the others. 

Those two middle figures <note>The second and third Vulcans mentioned by Cicero.</note> merge into a single person; for that Vulcan born of Jupiter, who was soothed with such magnificent worship by the Egyptians, is the very same one whom **Cicero** says was fathered by the Nile and whom he establishes as the protector of Egypt. Indeed, it is more than likely that both are that one **Mizraim** <note>The biblical grandson of Noah; in Hebrew, *Mizraim* is the word for Egypt.</note>, the son of **Ham** 

<margin>The Egyptian Vulcan</margin>

(that is, the son of Jupiter) <note>Early modern scholars frequently identified the biblical Ham with the god Jupiter/Amun.</note>, who was the first monarch of Egypt after the Flood and the propagator in Egypt of the "Vulcanian arts" <note>metallurgy and chemistry</note> handed down by his father, as was touched upon before. Concerning him, **Diogenes Laertius** <note>A 3rd-century biographer of Greek philosophers.</note> writes clearly in *Book I*: *Vulcan, the son of the Nile...*

### THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY <note>original Latin: PROGRESSU CHEMIÆ. This is a running header indicating the book’s theme: the historical development of alchemical and chemical knowledge.</note>

...the Egyptians revealed the principles of **Philosophy** <note>In this context, "Philosophy" refers to "Hermetic Philosophy" or Alchemy.</note>. Namely, it was done in such a way that his brother **Hermes** <note>The Greek name for the Egyptian Thoth, legendary founder of alchemy.</note> handled everything more correctly and more solidly. <margin>Mizraim was the one from the</margin> Indeed, it is established from the aforementioned sources that in sacred scripture, Egypt is called the "Land of **Ham**," and by **Plutarch** <note>A Greek historian and philosopher.</note> it is called ***Chemia***. Moreover, that **Ham** is the same figure whom the pagans call **Jupiter** <note>The king of the gods.</note> is shown not only by **Herodotus** in his book *Euterpe*: <margin>above.</margin> *For the Egyptians call Jupiter "Ammon."* <note>original Greek: ’Αμμῦν γὰ Αἰγύπtioi καλέουσι τὸν Δία. But the very power of the word testifies to the same thing: for what does **Ham** mean to the Hebrews but "to glow" or "to be hot"? And what is ***Zeus*** to the Greeks—derived from *zeein* <note>original Greek: ζέειν, meaning "to boil" or "to glow."</note>—but "to boil"? I might also mention that Jupiter is fashioned as the "Lord of the Sky" because Africa, scorched by the vertical sun, fell to the lot of Ham. Finally, nothing is more frequently read in the works of **Phurnutus** <note>A Stoic philosopher who interpreted myths as allegories for natural forces.</note> and other authors than the story that Jupiter castrated his own father. The very same thing happened with **Ham**, who defiled his eyes by gazing upon his father’s nakedness; and an opportunity for the Poets to invent fables arose from the Hebrew word which signifies both "to uncover" and "to cut off." <note>The author is suggesting that the Greek myth of Jupiter/Zeus castrating Saturn/Cronus is a misunderstood version of the biblical story of Ham seeing the nakedness of his father, Noah.</note>

Furthermore, that **Saturn** is no other than **Noah** is urged with such brilliant arguments by the most learned **Bochart** <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667), a scholar of biblical geography and animals.</note> in his work *Phaleg* that to disagree would be judged almost reckless. Having now shown that the **Vulcan** who lived after the flood was actually **Mizraim** <note>The Hebrew name for Egypt and the son of Ham.</note>, it now remains to be shown that **Mercury**—the inventor of so many arts in Egypt—must be considered the brother of that Vulcan: that is, **Canaan**. <margin>Hermes</margin>

Many things persuade us of this: First, both are handed down as sons of **Jupiter**; for both were fathered by **Ham**. Second, trade was the concern of both; for "**Canaan**" signifies a "Merchant," and **Mercury** is recognized as the "God of Merchants." <margin>was Canaan.</margin> Third, in sacred scripture, Canaan is condemned to the service of his brothers, and in pagan myths, Mercury is condemned to the service of Jupiter and the other gods.

# ON THE ORIGIN AND <note>original: DE ORTU ET. This is part of the running title "On the Origin and Progress of Chemistry."</note>

Fourthly, note that the cunning thievery of **Mercury** expresses the craftiness of the Canaanites or Phoenicians in commerce. Fifthly, **Mercury**—according to **Sanchuniathon**, **Diodorus**, and others—was the first to discover letters and matters of Astronomy; **Cadmus** the Canaanite or Phoenician, according to **Pliny**, was the first to bring letters into Greece. From this, the Phoenicians, skilled in the stars, directed their maritime courses—by which, following the example of the modern **Dutch** <note>original: *Batavorum*. The author compares the ancient Phoenician sea-power to the Dutch maritime dominance of the 17th century.</note>, they traversed all the seas—toward the stars, as **Bochart** explains in his *Phaleg* <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667), a French scholar who wrote a famous geographical commentary on the descendants of Noah.</note>; from them, Astronomy was finally passed down to the Greeks, on the authority of **Strabo** in *Geography, Book XVI*. Finally, sixthly, **Canaan** in sacred history is thought to have amused himself with a shameful spectacle while assisting his father **Ham** <note>A reference to the biblical "Curse of Ham" involving Noah's sons.</note>; no differently in pagan myths is **Mercury** imagined to stand by **Jupiter** as a minister of lust and a mediator of his love affairs.

<margin>Do any of Hermes' writings survive?</margin>
Of the writings of this **Mercury**, or **Hermes**, nothing has come down to our hands that we dare to guarantee safely and affirmatively as having originated from **Hermes** himself. That they once existed, we do not doubt: **Diodorus Siculus** rightly says in Book II, Chapter IV, *The priests of Memphis have certain more secret writings.* That those were the works of Hermes is demonstrated by **Clement of Alexandria**. **Plutarch**, a writer of Trajan’s era, clearly states in *On Isis and Osiris*: *it is recorded*, he says, *in the books which are called Mercury’s*. For we do not think—along with **Ursinus** <note>Johann Heinrich Ursinus (1608–1667), a scholar who argued that the Hermetic corpus was a later forgery.</note> of the most imperious style—that either **Hermes** should be counted among fables, or that all the books sold under the name of **Hermes** are entirely spurious in every part, leaving the judgment to the learned. **Conringius** <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a polymath who also challenged the antiquity of the Hermetic texts.</note> sang almost the same tune before him. The literary world owes much to both, but it also remembers that the Oracles have now ceased, and it is permitted to appeal from any **Aristarchus** <note>A reference to Aristarchus of Samothrace, a famous critic; used here to mean any severe scholarly critic.</note> to a new tribunal. Let us therefore examine those sharp weapons of **Ursinus**: *First of all*, he says,

# PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY <note>original: PROGRESSU CHEMIÆ. This is the continuation of the running title "On the Origin and Progress of Chemistry."</note>

<margin>Ursinus <note>Johann Heinrich Ursinus (1608–1667), a German scholar who argued against the extreme antiquity of Hermetic and "Zoroastrian" writings.</note> opposes Philo of Byblos and Sanchuniathon.</margin>

But unless the memory of **Mercury** had then been fresh, and he had written something, it would have stood that **Mnevis**—a King otherwise wise—had acted quite imprudently by appealing to laws delivered to him by **Mercury**. For at that time, **Mercury** could not have been esteemed as a God by the Egyptians in any other way than as one of those who were recently known to have been men, as has been demonstrated from **Plutarch** and **Clement of Alexandria**. # ON THE ORIGIN AND

<margin>Vindica-</margin> *Philo* is defended, he says. But why? The very name indicates a forgery. To me, it seems to have been spoken as a mockery of the Christian name, like a rock prepared for a she-ass; for three Hebrew words signify this: <margin>Philo is defended,</margin> *San-chuni-athon* <note>The critic Ursinus argued the name was a Hebrew pun intended to insult Christians, possibly linking the suffix to the word for a "she-ass" (athon)</note>. But this is surely a stretch, *Ursinus* <note>Johannes Heinrich Ursinus (1608–1667), a German scholar who questioned the antiquity of Sanchuniathon</note>: *Porphyry* recognizes the word as Phoenician and reports that it means "truth-lover" <note>original: "philalethen," from the Greek φιλαλήθης</note> according to *Theodoret* in the *Second book on the Treatment of Greek Affections*. Similar things are taught by the most learned *Aldrete* <note>Bernardo José de Aldrete (1560–1641), a Spanish humanist and linguist</note>. You, however, in order to find an excuse to harass the man, stoop with forbidden cleverness to a Hebrew explanation [similar indeed, but not the same]. But let it be; suppose the name is recognized as Hebrew. Must the name of *Issachar* also be believed to be false <margin>& San-</margin> because *Jacob* assigned him a "donkey crouching between the borders" <note>A reference to Genesis 49:14, where the patriarch Jacob compares his son Issachar to a strong donkey</note>? Did no illustrious *Asinii* <note>a Roman family name derived from "asinus," meaning donkey</note>, no *Scrofæ* <note>meaning "sow"</note>, no *Verres* <note>meaning "boar"</note> live in <margin>chunia-</margin> Rome because these titles were derived from ignoble beasts? You will object that *Porphyry* reports certain things about *Sanchuniathon* that are less consistent with historical truth. Is *Philo* to pay the penalty if <margin>thon, &</margin> *Porphyry* erred in the details? Are there then no Jews in the nature of things simply because *Justin* erred in one way and *Tacitus* in another while pursuing their history <note>A reference to the Roman historians Justin and Tacitus, who often recorded inaccurate or biased accounts of Jewish history</note>? For if *Philo* and *Porphyry* had forged the writings of *Sanchuniathon*, without a doubt either *Eusebius* or *Theodoret* would have noticed it and marked those counterfeit goods with deserved <margin>Mane-</margin> censure. But the Church Fathers closest in age to *Philo* and *Porphyry* put no sickle to this harvest; on the contrary, they willingly admitted these <margin>thon.</margin> writings as genuine and of the purest coinage. And what fruit of a lie would there be for *Philo*? What for *Porphyry*? Nor should *Porphyry* be believed to have reached such a peak of stupidity as to think he could conquer Christianity by citing those words of *Sanchuniathon*. What of the fact that he confesses to having seen the complete writing of *Sanchuniathon*...

<meta>This page continues the defense of the antiquity and authenticity of ancient authors, specifically Hermes Trismegistus and Manetho, against the skepticism of Johannes Heinrich Ursinus. # ON THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.

...somewhere *Kircher* <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath who wrote extensively on Egyptian hieroglyphs</note> mentions. But were the books of *Manetho* <note>An Egyptian priest and historian from the 3rd century BCE</note> unknown to *Herodotus*? Who would stake their reputation on such a claim? Perhaps he saw them, but held them in contempt. Or suppose he did not see them. Should we then deny that they existed? Travelers often pass by many things with a yawn, even those that are distinguished and rare. Few of us who have traveled through France have recognized *Bernard Palissy* <note>Bernhardum Pallissium (c. 1510–1589), a famous French potter and natural philosopher known for his study of salts and minerals</note>, that most inquisitive writer; few in Britain know *Fortescue* <note>Sir John Fortescue (c. 1394–1479), an English jurist</note>; few in Portugal know *Luís de Camões* <note>Ludovicum Camoësium (c. 1524–1580), Portugal's national poet</note>; few in Spain know *Alvaro Alonso Barba* <note>Didacum Alonsum Barbam (1569–1662), a priest and metallurgist whose work on silver mining was vital to early chemistry</note>; few in Italy know *Ferrante Imperato* <note>Ferrantem Imperatum (1550–1625), a Neapolitan apothecary and naturalist</note>; and yet, all these authors have long been famous for their writings. But, you say, *that Book of Mercury praised by Philo offers up fables.* We do not exempt *Mercury* <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, associated with the god Mercury/Hermes</note>, though he was a most wise man, from the human lot so far as to think he could not have erred in his writings. What if, perhaps, as time went on, several "borrowed rags" were sewn onto his brilliant purple robes by the hands of counterfeiters? For even the Sacred Scriptures are not without their apocrypha. Therefore, the weapons of *Ursinus* are too blunt to be able to cast either *Manetho* or *Sanchuniathon* out from the ranks of the ancients. But we must return to *Hermes*.

<margin>How many books did Hermes write?</margin>
*Jamblichus* <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 CE)</note>, says *Ursinus*, told a "massive lie" <note>original: "trabali... mendacio," literally a lie as big as a structural beam</note> when he said that *Mercury* wrote *36,529* books. How easy it is for *Ursinus* to accuse the Ancients of lying! More politely, the illustrious *Bochart* <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667), a French scholar of biblical geography and animals</note> interprets "so many books" as "so many verses"; just as *Pliny* says of *Zoroaster* in *Book 30, Chapter 1*. Or, if you prefer, instead of so many books, one might recognize so many "strips of papyrus" <note>original: "philyras," referring to the thin layers of the papyrus plant used for writing</note>, because *Hermes* wrote in Egypt, where the plant of the Nile took the place of paper. But how many books is *Ezra* <note>Esdras</note> said to have written in a very short time? How could the world not contain the books to be written about the deeds of the Savior <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 21:25</note>? How is it that a whole library does not contain...

# DE ORTU ET <note>Title continues: ...PROGRESSU CHEMIAE (On the Rise and Progress of Chemistry)</note>

...surprised *Livia*? <note>original: "pit Liviam?" This is the end of a word like *latuit* (escaped) or *fugit*, likely referring to Livia Drusilla, the Roman Empress, as an example of an historical figure who might have been unaware of certain texts.</note> Authors are to be explained, *Ursinus* <note>Johannes Heinrich Ursinus (1608–1667), a German scholar who argued that Hermetic texts were late forgeries</note>, not immediately pierced with daggers <note>original: "obelis configendi"; the obelus was a mark used by ancient critics to denote suspected or fake passages</note>. Therefore, see that you do not find in your own eye that beam which you have sought in this "beam-like" lie, as you call it <note>A reference to the biblical "mote and the beam" (Matthew 7:3), suggesting Ursinus is blind to his own errors while attacking the antiquity of the text</note>. What of the fact that nothing is easier than to explain *Iamblichus* by *Iamblichus* himself? For he says clearly elsewhere in *On the Mysteries of the Egyptians*: *The Egyptian writers, believing that all things were discovered by Mercury, dedicated their books to Mercury.* <note>The Roman Mercury was identified with the Greek Hermes and the Egyptian Thoth</note> And elsewhere: *For the books which are circulated under the title of Mercury contain Mercurial opinions, even if they often speak in the style of Greek philosophers; for they were translated from the Egyptian language into Greek by men not unlearned in philosophy.*

Clearly, *Iamblichus* recognizes that not all books that boast the title of *Hermes* were written by *Hermes* himself; rather, some are commentators and interpreters of the opinions, sayings, and writings of *Hermes*. The books that today present themselves to the world under the name of *Hermes* are few, and subject to the attacks of many. The *Poimandres of Hermes* <note>The "Shepherd of Men," a foundational text of the Hermetic Corpus</note>, the *Sacred Discourse*, the *Key*, the *Discourse to Asclepius*, and the *Minerva*, along with a few others, were recalled, as it were, from the underworld by that philosopher of great genius, *Francesco Patrizi* <note>Franciscus Patricius (1529–1597), who edited the Hermetica to prove they were older than Moses</note>; they were defended by *François de Foix-Candalle* <note>Flussates Candalla (1512–1594), a bishop who translated the Hermetic works</note> and others, who nevertheless succumb to the burden because it is clearly shown by *Vossius* and *Casaubon* <note>Isaac Casaubon (1559–1614) used linguistic analysis to prove the Hermetica were written in the early centuries CE, not ancient Egypt</note> that those writings are from a more recent workshop, being sort of semi-Platonic and semi-Christian.

We, along with *Iamblichus*, shall consider that Hermetic ideas are contained therein, which—having been derived in some way from the fountains of *Hermes*—is no wonder that they agree with Platonic and Christian philosophy. *Plato* drew from those sources, being not unmindful of *Thoth* <note>original: "Toythi"</note>, that is, the Master *Hermes*, in his *Phaedrus*. The Fathers of the Christian Church read and praised the Hermetic writings. *Eusebius* mentions them in his *Chronicle*, as does *Cyril* [who calls the discourse of Hermes to Asclepius...

# THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

...ing <note>Original: "tes ]". In *Tertullian*, Chapter II *On the Soul* and *Against the Valentinians*, Chapter XV, *Hermes* is hailed as the *Master of the Natural Philosophers*. Because of this, the *Physical Writings of Hermes* are also praised by *Zosimus* <note>Zosimus of Panopolis (c. 300 AD), one of the earliest known alchemists, who frequently cited Hermetic wisdom</note>. Granted, the spirit of the first or second Christian century may have mixed in some elements; that does not mean the entirety of the works are forgeries. We should hold a more sacred view of the intentions of those early candidates of the Church. *Jacob Golius* <note>A famous Dutch orientalist (1596–1667) who collected many Arabic manuscripts</note>, in addition to the *Arabic Writings of Geber* <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the "father of chemistry"</note>, brought back with him from the East *Hermes on Poisons and Antidotes* and the *Astrology of Hermes*, writings greatly esteemed by the Arabs. In the library of *Thomas Erpenius* there existed an Arabic booklet titled *Hermes on Precious Stones*; it is believable that *Albert the Great* <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar famous for his works on nature and minerals</note> made use of this long ago in his learned work *On Minerals*—which was also praised by *Conring* <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a German physician and polymath</note>—where he very frequently appeals to the authority of *Hermes*.

<margin>Which writings of Hermes are considered unauthentic?</margin>
But the *Medical Astrology* <term>Iatromathematica; the ancient practice of applying astrological theory to medicine</term> attributed to *Hermes* and addressed to *Ammon the Egyptian* was given to the public long ago by *David Hoeschel* in the year 1587 at Augsburg in a Greek-Latin edition; however, it had already been published earlier in 1532 at Nuremberg from the press of the *Sosii*. But what should we judge of those seven chapters of *Hermes* included in the *Theatrum Chemicum* <note>The "Chemical Theater," a massive and influential compendium of alchemical texts first published in the early 17th century</note>? The text itself reveals that they belong to some "younger" *Hermes*. For although the method of teaching breathes a certain recondite air, and is sufficiently Egyptian, it cannot be considered the genuine offspring of the most ancient *Hermes*. This is because the author mentions the ancient Philosophers and cites others; because he dwells much on the battle of the four elements; because he calls upon Greek letters (which did not exist in the age of *Thoth* <note>original: "Thaautico"; referring to Thoth, the Egyptian god of wisdom identified with Hermes Trismegistus</note>); and because the whole work "smells" of Christianity. Regarding the other works just mentioned, let every man’s judgment be free as to whether they should be considered spurious or genuine. Genuine

# ON THE ORIGIN AND...

...many doubt they exist, although if merely doubting were enough to dismiss a work, we would find few of the writings of *Hippocrates*, *Homer*, or *Aristotle* today—a fact that *Laërtius* <note>Diogenes Laërtius, a 3rd-century biographer of Greek philosophers.</note> alone would testify to. In those dense shadows of Antiquity, where it is easy to guess but difficult to know for certain, we will not pledge our absolute faith; however, we are more inclined to believe they are not the genuine works of *Hermes*, but rather, as *Iamblichus* <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who argued that Egyptian priests attributed their own discoveries to Hermes to honor him.</note> suggests, they contain "Hermetic opinions"—that is, certain sayings of Hermes that have been interpolated and revised in various ways.

<margin>Kircher’s judgment on the Emerald Tablet.</margin>
Furthermore, what should be thought of the *Emerald Tablet of Hermes*? *Kircher* <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath and one of the first to attempt to decipher Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note> in his *Egyptian Oedipus* investigates the matter as follows: *“Regarding the Emerald Tablet,”* he says, *“there is a profound silence among all ancient authors; who that Hermes was, when he lived, where this tablet was found, by what finder, and in what language it was written, remains unknown. Is it possible that such a famous monument of Hermes was hidden from the most inquisitive of the Greek Philosophers, who traveled to Egypt for the very purpose of study? Only Albertus Magnus <note>A 13th-century German friar and philosopher.</note> in his book on Chemical Secrets (which is, however, counted among the forged books of Albert) says that Alexander the Great, during his travels, found the tomb of Hermes—the Father of all Philosophers—filled with all sorts of treasures; not metallic ones, but golden writings on a tablet of Zatadi <note>A term of likely Arabic origin referring to a type of green stone or emerald.</note>, which others call the Emerald Tablet. But where did Albert get this? On whose authority? Bernhardus Canesius <note>Likely referring to Bernard of Trevisan, a legendary 15th-century alchemist often associated with the discovery of the tablet.</note> the Alchemist was the first, as far as I know, who presented it to the world, claiming that the 'whole work' and the 'treasure of the whole world' are contained within it. I would add that almost everything found on that tablet has been extracted here and there from Hermes’s Pymander and Asclepius <note>The "Divine Pymander" and "Asclepius" are the core dialogues of the Corpus Hermeticum.</note>, and from Iamblichus and Proclus, etc.”*

<margin>Kriegsmann’s judgment.</margin>
However, *Wilhelm Christian Kriegsmann* <note>A 17th-century scholar who wrote a specialized treatise on the Emerald Tablet.</note> judges the matter entirely differently in a unique little work. *“To say nothing,”* he says, *“of the tradition regarding this tablet—which says it was taken from the hands of Hermes’s corpse in a cave near Hebron by a woman named Zara <note>The name "Zara" is a variant of "Sarah."</note> several centuries after the Great Flood—the story is in no way poorly founded, if we speak of Abraham’s wife...”*

# PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

...should be understood as *Sarah* <note>Referring to the legend that the Emerald Tablet was found in the hands of the corpse of Hermes in a cave near Hebron, sometimes identified with the Tomb of the Patriarchs where Sarah, wife of Abraham, is buried</note>; since the times of the deceased *Hermes*—that is, *Canaan, the grandson of Noah*, and *Sarah*—coincide; and the location (the city of *Hebron*, built by *Heth*, the son of *Canaan* or *Hermes*, where *Abraham* had established his residence) is most suitable for the matter; moreover, the custom of the *Phoenicians*, the descendants of *Hermes*, of hiding all dead bodies buried in caverns, beautifully agrees with this.

<margin>Kircher's</margin>
Let everyone follow whatever opinion they wish here; but first, if you have time, observe with me the fragility of *Kircher's* arguments.

<margin>fragile arguments.</margin>
How can *Kircher* make us certain about that silence of Antiquity? Has he held in his hands all the authors who have perished? Perhaps a single *Manetho* <note>An Egyptian priest and historian (3rd century BCE).</note>, a single *Sanchuniathon* <note>A Phoenician author whose works are mostly lost.</note>, if they existed in their entirety, would convict the *Jesuit* <note>Athanasius Kircher was a Jesuit priest.</note> of excessive audacity. It is necessary that *Albertus Magnus*—however much he might be a substitute <note>The author suggests the work attributed to Albertus Magnus might be pseudonymous or compiled from earlier sources.</note>—drew what he handed down from someone else, and who knows if it was not from an ancient writer? "But," [Kircher argues,] "knowledge of it would not have escaped the *Greek* travelers."

Why not? Foreigners were not admitted into the shared knowledge of the priestly secrets, as has already been argued before. Which of the *Greeks* inspected those *forty-two* books of *Hermes*, so familiar to the *Egyptian* priests? Yet that they existed in *Egypt* is attested by that brilliant witness, *Clement of Alexandria* <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who described the processions of Egyptian priests carrying the books of Hermes.</note>. Which of the *Greeks* learned to imitate *Egyptian* mummies? Who learned the hatching of eggs <note>original: "pullitiem," referring to the Egyptian secret of artificial incubation in ovens.</note>? Who learned the "strings" of *Memnon* <note>Referring to the Colossi of Memnon, which were famous in antiquity for producing a musical sound at sunrise.</note>?

Or, if modern examples are preferred, what *Danish* traveler in *Muscovy* <note>An archaic name for Russia.</note> has ever forced from the inhabitants, or elicited until now, the method of tempering *Russian* leather? What newcomer has been able to persuade the *Parisian Gobelins* <note>The famous royal tapestry factory in Paris, known for its secret dyeing techniques.</note> to reveal that noble dyeing of silk and cloth until now? Indeed, the silence of the craftsmen has made that pleasant scent of *Roman* gloves <note>Perfumed gloves were a high-status luxury item in the early modern period, with Rome being a center for their production.</note> inimitable even today to foreigners who investigate it. The same applies to the *Emerald Tablet*, that hidden treasure of those *Chemists* who have eyes to see...

# [ON THE] ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]

...it is not improbable that it happened to the treasure.

<margin>The Isiaca Tablet found again.</margin>
And to strike this string more precisely: how few of the ancients mention the hieroglyphic **Isiaca Tablet** <note>Also known as the Bembine Tablet, a famous bronze tablet with Egyptian-style motifs that fascinated Renaissance scholars</note>? That wonderful treasure from the deepest antiquity, which **Kircher** <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath who attempted to decipher hieroglyphs</note> recently wrote had likewise perished in Italy and lamented its loss; yet I myself saw it in Italy this past year. I saw that very ancient, authentic tablet, older than **Cambyses** <note>A Persian King (r. 530–522 BCE) who conquered Egypt</note>, in the Court of the **Duke of Savoy** at **Turin**, courteously shown to me by that great light of Italy, the **Lord Marquess of Pianezza**, first minister of state to the Duke of Savoy. If **Herwart** or **Pignorius** <note>Earlier scholars who studied the tablet</note>, overcome by the sublimity of the matter, have not yet examined it with perfect precision, this in no way detracts from the antiquity of this most elegant tablet. Kircher’s own words, cited from his *Oedipus Aegyptiacus*, deserve a place here:

> In the year 1630, he says, in the wretched pillaging of the city of **Mantua**, the **Isiaca** (or **Bembine**) **tablet** so disappeared that although it was sought with the greatest zeal to discover what finally became of it, to this day no one has been able to find out. It is indisputably concluded that this tablet, which **Herwart** published, was made by the Egyptians, and in Egypt, before **Cambyses'** invasion of Egypt.

Evidently, just as the **Isiaca Tablet** has until now escaped the diligence of the seeking **Kircher**, so much more could the **Emerald Tablet** escape the Greeks, who were either not looking for it or were unaware that it should be sought. It is more credible that the **Emerald Tablet**—if indeed it belongs to **Hermes**, as is estimated with good reason—was snatched and carried elsewhere during those wartime collisions in Egypt, when the **Phoenicians**, then **Cambyses** the son of **Cyrus**, and later **Cornelius Gallus** <note>The first Roman prefect of Egypt under Augustus</note> in the age of **Augustus** were harassing Egypt and overturning **Diospolis** <note>The Greek name for Thebes</note> and other secret sanctuaries of the Priests. Perhaps, with the plunderer perishing, it was crushed in the mud or a marsh, which is known to have happened to that most celebrated **Danish Golden Horn** <note>The Golden Horns of Gallehus, famous archaeological discoveries in Denmark (found in 1639 and 1734)</note>.

...it is certain, however much Kircher and others hostile to Chemistry might easily show by the same trick of refutation that the famous golden horn <note>The Golden Horns of Gallehus, ancient Danish artifacts found in the 17th century</note> found in Jutland <note>original: "Cimbria"</note> never existed, because no clear record of it appears in the *histories* of the Danes.

<margin>An ancient Obelisk found in Rome.</margin>
Two years ago in Rome, I saw an extraordinary Egyptian obelisk, marked on all sides with hieroglyphic signs, unexpectedly brought into the light and dug out from the foundation <note>original: "suggrundario"</note> of the Temple of Minerva after the darkness of many centuries. Is that obelisk to be judged a forgery by Kircher, simply because it was not mentioned in the writings of any of the most ancient authors? That a temple to Isis once stood on that site had not only been proved previously by Nardini <note>Famiano Nardini (1580–1661), a prominent archaeologist of Rome</note>, but the discovery of half-statues of calves made of touchstone <note>original: "lapide lydio," a dark stone used to test precious metals</note> excavated from the same foundation also convinced observers, representing the bull Apis <note>The sacred bull of ancient Egyptian religion</note> in these images.

<margin>The Emerald Tablet is not inappropriately attributed to Hermes.</margin>
Furthermore, no small amount of credibility is added to the *Emerald Tablet* <note>The Tabula Smaragdina, a foundational text of alchemy attributed to Hermes Trismegistus</note> by the fact that Iamblichus and Proclus the Lycian <note>Prominent Neoplatonist philosophers of late antiquity</note> previously informed us that there once existed in Egypt various columns or tablets of Hermes, inscribed with the teachings of Mercury <note>The Roman equivalent of Hermes</note>. What prevents one of these from remaining safe, as if from a shipwreck? Thus, Hermes may be judged no less fortunate than Duilius <note>Gaius Duilius, the Roman admiral who won the first naval victory against Carthage in 260 BCE</note>, the first to defeat the Carthaginians on the waves, whose rostral column <note>A column decorated with the prows of captured enemy ships</note> still triumphs on the Capitoline after an interval of nearly twenty centuries from its first construction, while a thousand other monuments have perished.

<margin>Kircher is inconsistent.</margin>
Kircher continues: "*In this tablet a certain universal production of natural things to their most perfect state is described, and according to a certain analogy, it can be applied most excellently not only to terrestrial and metallic things, but also to vegetables, animals, and celestial bodies.*" Therefore, the sense of the tablet is nothing other than a theory of a most excellent essence, which they call the **Elixir of life**, **potable gold**, and the **quintessence**. But in that Egyptian Alchemy...

# [ON THE] ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]

...[the Isi]aca <note>referring to the Bembine Tablet or *Tabula Isiaca*, an Egyptian-style bronze tablet</note> Tablet, which Kircher pursues in his *Oedipus* <note>*Oedipus Aegyptiacus*, Kircher’s 1652 work attempting to decipher hieroglyphs</note>, he shows himself to be a strange blind-fighter <note>original: "Andabata," a gladiator who fought wearing a helmet without eye-holes; here used to mean someone arguing blindly</note>. I would address this elsewhere with more words if the matter required it; here, he attributes no other meaning to the tablet than the theory of **potable gold** <note>original: "auri potabilis," a medicinal "liquid gold" believed to be a universal cure and a goal of alchemy</note>, and yet, driven by some dizzying whim, he adds at the same point that it can also be very well applied to vegetables, animals, and celestial bodies. Granting that it can be applied to all things, provided it is most appropriately applied to the **Chemical Mastery** <note>The *Magisterium*, or the successful completion of the Great Work of alchemy</note>, this is something no one experiences more clearly than one who, properly initiated into the sacred rites of the Chemists, handles with his own hands what Hermes intended.

But what stops us from observing Kircher wavering here! *The Egyptians*, he says, *possessed the theory of the **Philosopher’s Stone**, but not the practice.* Soon after: *The Egyptians extracted gold from almost all materials, especially from the silt of the Nile and from stones of every kind.* Likewise: *The sands of the Nile, impregnated with gold from the Ethiopian mountains, carried gold-bearing sand with them. Then, at Thebes, Ethiopia, and Arabia, there were huge stone mines fertile with gold; from that very hard earth, cooked with much fire, gold was produced, as Diodorus Siculus <note>A Greek historian of the 1st century BCE</note> describes at length in Book III, chapter 2.* 

Kircher again: *However, I would not deny that the Egyptians were skilled in the art by which gold and silver were made from the **fixation** <note>the process of making a volatile substance, like mercury, solid and resistant to fire</note> of mercury mixed with other metals, but I speak of this specifically in my *Subterranean World* <note>original: "Mundo subterraneo," Kircher’s 1665 work on geology and alchemy</note>.* I want it, I don't want it; I don't want it, I want it: what has been said is unsaid. *They had the theory,* he says, *not the practice.*

<margin>From where did the immense wealth of the Egyptians come?</margin>
But that theory in practical disciplines is useless, Kircher, unless it is established by practice. And no one can show they had a true theory except through their practice. If they did not have the practice, from where came those infinite riches of the Egyptian Kings, celebrated even in proverbs? Nor were those riches collected through a long wandering of centuries, but immediately great just a few centuries after the Flood, immediately visible in so many monuments, pyramids, and obelisks...

# [ON THE] PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

...[as you] know, displayed in mausoleums built at incredible expense. From this, it is clear that the treasures of the Egyptians were great even in the age of Moses, as the Apostle certainly testifies in Hebrews 11:26: <note>original Greek: "Μωσῆς μείζονα πλοῦτον ἡγησάμενος τῶν ἐν Αἰγύπτῳ θησαυρῶν τὸν ὀνειδισμὸν τοῦ χριστοῦ"</note> *Moses, considering the reproach of Christ to be greater wealth than the treasures in Egypt.* The Apostle's purpose here is evident; he intends to show that the immense treasures of the Egyptians at that time were seen and known by Moses, yet the shame borne for the name of Christ—to be ended at last by a happy exodus—was valued more highly in his holy heart.

For the mines of the Egyptians, of which Diodorus <note>Diodorus Siculus, a 1st-century BCE Greek historian</note> speaks, were both rare and labor-intensive to work, yielding modest results. They are not to be compared to the Macedonian mines of Philip, nor to Hannibal’s Bebelo <note>a famous silver mine in the Pyrenees mentioned by Pliny</note> in the Pyrenees, nor to the **Potosi** mines of the *Spaniards* <note>the major silver mining center in modern-day Bolivia, which fueled the Spanish Empire’s economy</note>. Meanwhile, the gasping poverty of Philip of Macedon was also touched upon by *Justinus* <note>Marcus Junianus Justinus, a Roman historian</note>; and at times, a lack of resources left Hannibal unable to raise new recruits. Furthermore, a shrewd traveler recently demonstrated that the King of the Spaniards today makes more money from the sale of **stamped paper** <note>official government documents requiring a royal seal/tax</note> secured with the Royal seal than from both the East and West Indies. 

The wealth of the Egyptian Kings, however, was so beyond limit or measure that they were always occupied in spending it with ambitious extravagance, yet they always remained rich. Hence those most sumptuous pyramids, criticized by *Pliny* <note>Pliny the Elder, who viewed the pyramids as a vain and foolish display of wealth</note> for their excessive display of Egyptian riches; and *Strabo* writes in Book XVII: *Near Thebes or Diospolis, there are inscriptions on obelisks that declare the riches and power of those Kings.* But for more on the immense wealth of the Egyptians, *Kircher* <note>Athanasius Kircher, a polymath who wrote extensively on Egyptian "mysteries"</note> and others should be consulted. To judge the lion by its claw <note>a Latin proverb, "ex ungue leonem," meaning to judge the whole by a small part</note>, consider the building of the largest pyramid: according to the testimony of *Herodotus* and *Diodorus*, three hundred and sixty thousand men built it over a period of twenty years...

# ON THE ORIGIN AND...
<note>original: "DE ORTU ET"; likely part of a chapter title such as "On the Origin and Progress of Chemistry"</note>

To summarize what has been said: I say that common salt is the seed of the earth, the seasoning of nature, the base and support of all bodies, and the genuine and nearest element of the earth. Just as it is colored by various minerals, it is likewise converted into salts differing in species—such as <term>nitre</term> <note>saltpeter, used in gunpowder and fertilizers</note>, <term>alum</term> <note>a mineral salt used in dyeing and tanning</note>, and <term>vitriol</term> <note>sulfate minerals, often called "green" or "blue" vitriol</note>. Despite this, it always holds the "cradle of its origin" by an inviolable right; once the color is removed, it remains the same as it was before. Again, elsewhere [he says]:

> *Under common salt, as if under a wrapping, there lies hidden a certain wondrous power of salt-sulfur-mercury, the creator of all things. The sulfurous power grants heat, the mercurial power grants the radical moisture, and finally, the salt grants subsistence. In this is the fire of life, the nearest principle of all things.* <note>Kircher is invoking the "Tria Prima" (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury), the three primary elements of Paracelsian alchemy</note>

A little later:

> *Purify salt as much as you wish; you will always find in it a true earth. Even if it seems devoid of all salt, expose it to a clear sky and the sun for several days, and you will find it pregnant with new salt.*

What wonders indeed! <note>original: "Mira de lente!"; a Latin idiom literally meaning "Marvels about a lentil," used ironically to describe making a great fuss over a small matter</note> The things he offers regarding nitre

<margin>
Glauberianism
</margin>
smack of the old Glauberianism <note>The theories of Johann Rudolf Glauber (1604–1670), a famous chemist who identified "Glauber's salt"</note>, not the more recent kind. Indeed, as long as Glauber draws his breath, he—as a perpetual 

<margin>
Kircher's [view].

reformer—changes his philosophy year after year, so much so that neither Proteus nor Vertumnus <note>mythological Roman and Greek gods capable of changing their shapes at will</note> has anything over this disciple in which to greatly glory. But how differently would Kircher philosophize regarding salt if he had more frequently consulted either the sharp insights of Descartes, or the testimonies of the ancient Chemists, or finally, the experiments of true fires—despising the letters of flatterers?

<margin>
Whether common salt turns into vitriol, alum, or nitre?
</margin>
In the beginning, Kircher wavers as to which salt it is safer to grant those immense praises; at one moment he is devoted to common salt, at another more inclined toward nitre. Nor does it help to have said that common salt, just as it is variously colored by minerals, is converted now into nitre, now alum, now vitriol, and yet returns again into common salt.

<meta>This page continues the critique of Athanasius Kircher’s alchemical theories as presented in his work Mundus Subterraneus. The author is refuting Kircher's claim that all salts can be reduced to or originate from common salt, arguing instead from the perspective of laboratory observation.</meta>

### THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.

...to return. Our hands have eyes, *Kircher*; they believe what they see. The workshops of *Vulcan* <note>original: "Vulcaniæ officinæ"; a poetic term for chemical laboratories using furnaces and fire</note> demonstrate nothing of the sort. <term>Nitre</term> <note>saltpeter</note> tortured by flames degenerates into an acidic liquor; if it is scorched by adding live coals, it turns into a <term>lixivial salt</term> <note>original: "salem lixivivum"; an alkaline salt, such as potash, produced by leaching ashes</note>, which is entirely different from the nature of common salt. If it is boiled long enough with sulfur in a closed vessel, while the bellows finally strain to the limit of their power, it turns into a stone; but into common salt, it has never yet been turned by any artifice. I suspect that *Kircher* was deceived by this: that the <term>spirit of nitre</term> <note>nitric acid</note> poured onto salt eventually reforms its crystals into the appearance of "revived" nitre. However, this edible salt merely mimics nitre; it is not truly it. For the nitre that results from this should not be attributed to the salt, but to the spirit of nitre—that is, the thinned particles of nitre joining together again with the help of the salt so that they might take on their original form.

Nor indeed will nitre, or <term>alum</term> <note>a mineral salt used in dyeing</note>, or <term>vitriol</term> <note>sulfate minerals</note>, provided they are properly purified, ever degenerate into common edible salt. But we are wasting words on a matter already conceded: we grant this much to *Kircher*, that common salt may pass into nitre, alum, and vitriol when it has changed its particles through the movement of subterranean fires. <margin>*Nothing is immutable in common salt.*</margin> And thus, broken into smaller portions, it converts its previously salty taste into an acid—if, however, there are mixed into the composition of nitre, scraps of lixivial salt; for alum, scraps of lead; and for vitriol (that is, <term>chalcanthum</term> <note>blue vitriol or copper sulfate</note>), thin shavings of iron or copper. However these things may be, the claim that something immutable lies hidden in the center of salt (whether common or nitre) which coagulates and fixes all things—this is what we deny in the strongest possible terms. This is because a skilled *Chemist* can easily drive both of these salts—and indeed the whole of them, leaving behind very little dregs—upward into the heights <note>i.e., through distillation or sublimation</note>, and resolve them into a volatile spirit...

# ON THE ORIGIN AND

...let us reduce what has been said into a summary: I say that common salt is the seed of the earth, the seasoning of nature, the base and support of all bodies, the genuine and immediate element of the earth; as it is tinted by various minerals, so too it is converted into another salt differing in species, such as <term>nitre</term> <note>saltpeter</note>, <term>alum</term> <note>a mineral salt used in dyeing and medicine</note>, or <term>vitriol</term> <note>sulfate minerals</note>; notwithstanding this, it always holds the cradle of its origin by an inviolable right, and once the color is removed, it is the same as it was before. Again elsewhere: *Under common salt, as if under a covering, there lies hidden a certain wondrous saline-sulphureous-mercurial force, the fashioner of all things, to which the sulphureous force bestows heat, the mercurial force bestows radical moisture, and finally the salt bestows subsistence. In this is the fire of life, the immediate principle of all things.* A little later: *Purify salt as much as you wish, you will always find in it a true earth, which, even if it seems destitute of all salt, if you expose it to a clear sky and the sun for several days, you will find it pregnant with new salt.* Wondrous trifles! <note>original: "Mira de lente!"; likely an idiomatic expression meaning "Much ado about nothing" or "Strange tales of small things"</note> What he offers concerning nitre tastes of the old <margin>Glauberianism</margin> <term>Glauberianism</term> <note>The theories of Johann Rudolf Glauber (1604–1670), a famous chemist known for "Glauber's Salt"</note>, not the more recent: <margin>Kircher's [view].</margin> for indeed, as long as Glauber draws common breath, so long does he, as a perpetual reformer, change his philosophy year after year in one way or another, so that neither <term>Proteus</term> nor <term>Vertumnus</term> <note>Greek and Roman gods capable of changing their shapes at will</note> has anything more to boast about than this disciple. But how much differently would Kircher philosophize about salt, if he had more frequently consulted either the insights of <term>Descartes</term> <note>René Descartes, whose mechanical philosophy provided a different explanation for the behavior of particles</note>, or the testimonies of the ancient chemists, or finally the experiments of truthful fires, while despising the letters of flatterers? <margin>Whether common salt is turned into vitriol, alum, or nitre?</margin> At the start, Kircher wavers as to which salt it is safer to assign those immense praises, now devoted to common salt, now more inclined toward nitre. Nor would it help to have said: *that common salt, as it is variously tinted by minerals, is converted now into nitre, now alum, now vitriol, and yet*

<meta>To return.</meta>

<summary>The author summarizes and critiques Athanasius Kircher’s theory that common salt is the "seed of the earth" and the base for all other salts. He mocks Kircher for following the ever-changing theories of Glauber and suggests that experimental chemistry and Cartesian philosophy would offer better insights.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Athanasius Kircher’s theories on the nature of salts. The author is refuting Kircher's claim that all mineral salts like nitre, alum, and vitriol are merely "masked" versions of common table salt that can be reduced back to their original form.</meta>

->## THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY##<-

...to return. Our hands have eyes, *Kircher*, and they believe what they see. The workshops of Vulcan <note>original: "Vulcaniæ officinæ"; a poetic reference to the laboratories of chemists and smiths who work with fire</note> demonstrate no such thing. <term>Nitre</term> <note>saltpeter</note> twisted by flames degenerates into an acidic liquid; if it is scorched by the injection of live coals, it turns into a lixivium salt <note>an alkaline salt, like potash</note>, which is most foreign to the nature of common salt; if it is thoroughly cooked with sulfur in a closed vessel, while the bellows finally increase the extreme forces, it turns into stone; but by no artifice has it yet turned into common salt. From this, I divine that *Kircher* was deceived, because the <term>spirit of nitre</term> <note>nitric acid</note> poured over salt finally reforms its crystals into the appearance of revived nitre; but this edible salt merely counterfeits nitre, it is not it. For that nitre which results from the process is not to be assigned to the salt, but to the spirit of nitre—that is, the thinned particles of nitre which, with the help of the salt, join themselves together again to take on their former shape.

Neither indeed will nitre, or alum, or vitriol, provided they are properly purified, ever degenerate into common edible salt. But we waste words on a matter already conceded: we grant this much to *Kircher*, that common salt may pass away into nitre, alum, and vitriol when its particles have been changed by the motion of subterranean fires, and thus what was previously a salty taste is converted into an acid through being broken into smaller portions; provided, however, that there are mixed into the composition of nitre the scraps of lixivium salt; to alum, the scraps of lead; to vitriol—that is, <margin>***Nothing is immutable in common salt.***</margin> <term>chalcantum</term> <note>blue vitriol or copper sulfate</note>—the thin cuttings of iron or copper. However these things may stand, we deny in the most explicit terms that there lies hidden in the center of salt—whether common or nitre—something immutable that coagulates and fixes all things. This is because a skilled chemist can, without difficulty, drive both of these salts—and indeed the whole of them, with very few dregs remaining—into the sublime, and reduce them into a volatile spirit...

L

->## ON THE ORIGIN AND... ##<-

...it might form; then, because it is easy to reduce both these salts into a tasteless earth, void of any power except for a slight astringency <note>original: "tenuiter stipticis"; referring to a contracting or drying effect on the tongue</note>, not only by following the path shown by *Blaise de Vigenère* <note>A 16th-century French diplomat and alchemist</note> in his curious treatise *On Fire and Salt*, but also by experience, which is obvious and easy, and often attempted by me. This is taught to be possible by the Spirit of God in *Matthew 5*: <note>original Greek: "Ἐὰν δὲ τὸ ἅλας μωρανθῇ, ἐν τίνι ἀλισθήσεται, εἰς οὐδὲν ἰσχύει ἔτι, εἰ μὴ βληθῆναι ἔξω, καὶ καταπατεῖσθαι ὑπὸ τῶν ἀνθρώπων"</note> *But if the salt has lost its flavor, with what shall it be seasoned? It is no longer good for anything, except to be thrown out and trampled underfoot by men.* And in *Mark 9*: <note>original Greek: "καλὸν τὸ ἅλας, ἐὰν δὲ τὸ ἅλας ἄναλον γένηται, ἐν τίνι αὐτὸ ἀρτύσετε;"</note> *Salt is good, but if the salt becomes unsalty, with what will you season it?* 

<margin>Salt is turned into tasteless earth.</margin>

Indeed, even the purest salt, when a suitable and repeated fire blunts its sharp points [namely, so that it may be often ignited, often dissolved, and often coagulated], strikes the tongue no more than any **potter’s clay**, but it dies away into another white, tasteless earth, which not even *Kircher* himself could distinguish from the rest. *Kircher* saw this through a lattice <note>a metaphor meaning he saw it only partially or indistinctly</note>, but he did not follow it through enough to the final goal. He says, *"Purify salt as much as you wish, you will always find true earth in it, which, though it may seem devoid of all salt, expose it to clear sky and sun for some days, and you will find it pregnant with new salt."* If *Kircher* had advanced that method of his for purging salt to its ultimate limit, he would have noted without a doubt that the entire body of salt is finally changed into a tasteless earth, and indeed—if you remove the astringent force—one that is almost ineffective. He ought to have scorched that earth further, which seemed to him to be devoid of all salt, and boiled it in water until it not only *seemed* to be, but *was*, stripped of every salty taste; only then would he have observed that this earth absorbs no more new salt from the air than any other body reduced to <term>lime</term> <note>original: "calcem"; the crumbly residue left after a substance has been thoroughly burned or "calcined"</note>. For since it can be a doubt to no one who spends their life among experiments that the air which we breathe daily,

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Athanasius Kircher’s theories on salt. The previous page ended with the observation that Kircher had only a partial understanding of how salt can be reduced to a tasteless earth. This page expands on why common salt cannot be the "Salt of the Philosophers" used in alchemy and begins a historical transition of chemical knowledge from Egypt to Greece.</meta>

->## THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY ##<-

<margin>
Does tasteless salt take on the form of salt again from the air?
</margin>
...day, it is also swollen with salty vapors, which can even be shown to the eye. It should not seem strange to anyone that this "corpse" of salt, after admitting the impressions of the tiny particles flitting in the air, once again presents something salty to the tongue, since almost all bodies that have endured the force of fire—and are thus more open—suffer those same "shades" <note>original: "manes"; here referring to the ghostly remnants or subtle influences of the original substance</note>. But against that special claim of Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath who wrote extensively on magnetism, medicine, and geology</note> regarding salt, the primary point is this: that the solvent and the thing to be dissolved, the fixative and the thing to be fixed, ought to be of the same species. This is a condition that does not apply to metals and common salt. Therefore, that which Kircher perhaps intended—to force Mercury into a stable mass by means of this salty particle, a mass that would resist the violence of fires—will only happen **when Germany drinks the Tigris** <note>A Latin idiom meaning something is impossible, as the Tigris river is in Mesopotamia</note>. 
<margin>
Why common salt is not the Salt of the Philosophers?
</margin>
The ancient Masters of Chemistry boast of a certain other coagulating salt, as different from edible salt as the sky is from the earth. But let us return from this detour to the main path.

<margin>
Chemistry migrates to the Greeks, but only wrapped in mists.
</margin>
After wandering through a long circuit of centuries among the priests of Memphis—specifically in the temple of Vulcan <note>The Roman name for the Greek god Hephaestus, associated with fire and metallurgy</note>, an account we owe to Zosimus and Herodotus—that chemical "Eleusis" <note>A reference to the Eleusinian Mysteries, symbolizing secret, sacred knowledge</note> walked about in secret. As travelers who went down to Egypt for the sake of study plucked scraps of it from here and there, it finally began to migrate into Hellas [Greece], with Orpheus, Homer, Democritus, and other more curious Greeks acting as midwives. However, at the start, it was quite cloudy and more like a shadow than Juno <note>A reference to the myth of Ixion, who was deceived by a cloud shaped like the goddess Juno; meaning the knowledge was a deceptive imitation</note>, because Egypt kept the Greeks away as **"always children"** <note>original Greek: "ἀεὶ παῖδας"</note>, and as the profane rabble, barred from their secrets, while wrapping everything in the darkness of fables. From this comes that saying of Porphyry (following the Egyptians) found in Eusebius's *Preparation for the Gospel, book 3, chapter 7*: **"I will speak to those for whom it is lawful; close the doors, you profane ones"** <note>original Greek: "Φθέγξομαι οἷς θέμις ἐστὶ, θύρας δ᾽ ἐπίθεσθε βέβηλοι"</note>. And shortly after in *book 4, chapter 8*: **"It is fitting for me to hide these things as secrets more secret than the unspeakable, for not even the gods [reveal themselves] openly..."** <note>original Greek: "ταῦτα μοι ὡς ἀρῥήτων ἀρρητότερα κρύπτειν... οὐδὲ γὰρ οἱ θεοὶ φανερῶς"</note>

<meta>This page continues the author’s transition from a critique of Athanasius Kircher to a historical survey of how chemical knowledge was transmitted. Having just mentioned the "ancient Masters of Chemistry," the author now explains why the Greeks, who learned from the Egyptians, communicated their findings in riddles and allegories.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
# ON THE ORIGIN AND #

...they did not speak openly about them, but through enigmas <note>original Greek: "περὶ αὐτῶν ἐθέσπισαν, ἀλλὰ δι’ αἰνιγμάτων"</note>. Hence those frequent complaints among the Greek authors: *Plato* in the works of *Strabo*, *Pythagoras* in *Clement of Alexandria*, and others in various sources. Indeed, it is well established from *Herodotus*, *Diodorus*, and *Strabo* that the jealousy of the Egyptians progressed to such a degree that, before the reign of *Psammetichus* <note>The Pharaoh (reigned 664–610 BCE) who first allowed Greeks to settle and trade in Egypt</note>, no Greek man was permitted to set foot on Egyptian soil. Therefore, it is no wonder if the Greeks—having with great difficulty wrenched these curiosities from the hands of the Egyptians—did not immediately thrust them upon everyone indiscriminately upon returning to their homeland, but were content to reveal the truth through the "tortuous fables" of their maxims. *Pythagoras* certainly looked elsewhere with his numbers and his doctrine of "metempsychosis" <note>The transmigration of souls; the author suggests Pythagoras used this as a metaphor for chemical transformations</note> than what is commonly noted; hence *Eusebius* in *Preparation for the Gospel, book 11*: "The followers of Pythagoras hid everything" <note>original Greek: "οἱ περὶ Πυθαγόραν ἀπεκρύψαντο πάντα"</note>.

<margin>
Orpheus the Chemist.
</margin>
The first of the Greek writers, *Orpheus* the Argonaut, when he had joined the other Heroes in the task of carrying off the Golden Fleece from Colchis, did not hesitate to encompass that most celebrated journey in verses, veiling everything else in the shroud of a story rather than a literal account. This is easily shown by the fragments of his writing touched upon and praised by *Clement of Alexandria* and others. As for the *Argonautica* currently in common circulation—whether the whole thing is truly owed to Orpheus or to someone more recent—I do not wish to digress here, being content to add that remark of *Josephus* in *Against Apion, book 1*: *For there is no writing at all among the Greeks whose reliability is certain that is older than the poetry of Homer.*

<margin>
What was the Golden Fleece of the Argonauts?
</margin>
However, that the "Golden Fleece" was something other than what it seemed to *Strabo* (who was not initiated into these sacred mysteries), *Suidas* <note>A 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia-dictionary</note> alone will testify regarding the "Golden-fleeced skin" <note>original Greek: "χρυσόμαλλον δέρας"</note>: *This was not as it is poetically presented, but it was a book written on skins, containing how gold must be made through chemistry [alchemy].* *Eustathius* <note>A 12th-century scholar and archbishop known for his commentaries on Greek epics</note> tends toward the same conclusion in his notes on *Dionysius the Voyager*.

<margin>
Conring opposes this view
