...the African, and *Apollonius Rhodius* in his *Argonautica*, which was first observed by *Pico della Mirandola* <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494) was a famous Renaissance philosopher who looked for hidden truths in ancient myths</note>. Since Conring sees these things as so evident that he cannot twist them elsewhere with all his eloquence, he nevertheless found a way to cast a hateful shadow over them: "It is certainly established," he says, "that no one interpreted the fable of the Golden Fleece as <term>chemistry <note>original Greek: χημεία (chēmeia)</note></term> until after the era of Constantine the Great; throughout all the preceding time, how could this have been ignored if chemical efforts had been in any way, even in the slightest, in use?" But from where does such boldness come, my dear Conring? Did you personally inspect all the scrolls in the Library of Alexandria before they burned? Thus, my listeners, "Divine Chemistry" often mourns under biased judges who, unwilling to understand the dispute, pronounce judgment with a dictatorial voice; she is never condemned except before a foreign tribunal, in a court that is not her own.

<margin>
An objection is raised against him
</margin>
"How could it have been ignored," Conring continues, "if chemical efforts had been in use?" What a weak weapon! How could the method for preparing Egyptian mummies have been ignored for so many centuries, Conring? Or the Memphitic method for hatching chicks? <note>The ancient Egyptians were famous for using "incubators" or heated ovens to hatch eggs, a process that fascinated and baffled European travelers for centuries</note> Or the way Memnon’s strings were tuned? <note>The Colossi of Memnon in Thebes were famous in antiquity for producing a musical sound at dawn, often compared to a breaking harp string</note> Or the secret of the inextinguishable lamps? This was so despite the most curious Greeks traveling throughout Egypt. How can the tanning of Russian leather, the making of Turkish blades, Gobelin purple <note>The "Gobelins" refers to the famous French royal tapestry and dye works</note>, Roman gloves, Chinese porcelain, or Japanese lacquer remain a mystery even today, while merchants enter every sea and land? But how blunt is that other spear of Conring’s? "No writing of this art," he says, "can be shown today that predates the era of Constantine the Great, and that is a great argument for its novelty." In the beginning, novelty is no crime for any art when utility commends it; but even if someone were to grant that no writing older than Constantine the Great can be shown today, do you not see, Conring, that for the glory

# ON THE ORIGIN AND PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY #

[Page] 86

...it pertains to the glory of the art that at the same time both the light of the Gospel was lit in the Roman Emperor <note>Referring to Constantine the Great (r. 306–337 AD), the first Roman emperor to convert to Christianity</note>, and the light of Chemistry through public writings in the Roman world. Although that reasoning is of the weakest strength: *that no chemical writing exists older than the age of Constantine the Great*, therefore *Chemistry was born just yesterday* <note>original: "nudius tertius natam esse," literally "born the day before yesterday," a Latin idiom for something very recent</note>. What does Pliny say to this in *Book 30, Chapter 1*? He certainly shows that arts also endure without writings, however much more difficult that may be: *Before Osthanes*, he says, *no records of the Magi existed; it is a marvel that the art was preserved for so long an age, neither by famous nor by continuous successions.* <note>Osthanes was a legendary Persian magus and alchemist; Pliny the Elder argues that his writings were the first to record a much older oral tradition of magic and alchemy</note> And why should it not endure, when the whole craft can be handed down in very few words, as Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a famous Polish alchemist who claimed the "Great Work" was simple if one understood the "hidden chemistry" of nature</note> teaches, and thus easily propagated by hand from father to sons? Finally, most learned Conring, you do not look at the

<margin>
The practice of Chemists.
</margin>

practice of the chemists; it was not their purpose to encompass the art in transparent writing. Who has described the art of the Memphitic <term>Pastophori <note>Egyptian priests of a lower rank who carried sacred objects in processions; they were believed to be keepers of medical and chemical secrets</note></term>? Yet you yourself acknowledge that they devoted their efforts to chemistry. Among the common people, the Egyptian Priests spread this idea: *Gold and silver are the sickly colors of a sterile and corrupted soil* <note>original: "Aurum & argentum sterilis ac vitiati soli morboſos eſſe colores"</note>, as Eusebius relates from Plutarch in his *Preparation for the Gospel, Book 3, Chapter 8*; yet they themselves perceived something entirely different in their minds. What Homer, Hesiod, Pindar, Apollonius of Tyana, and others handed down concerning the Art is not understood by even one in a hundred, so much are they wrapped in fables and metaphors; meanwhile, they do not escape the attention of clear-sighted chemists. Concerning the rest, something has been tasted here and there; as for what concerns Apollonius, I am not one who would agree with Philostratus in everything, nor would I wish to take anything away from Eusebius or Tortoletus as they lash out at that man of Tyana; but clearly I observe that great men are sometimes so led by partisan zeal that while they follow their own inclinations, they even [lose sight of] virtue...

By what right, and by what injury, do they drag a hated person into blame? <margin>Apollonius is not to be rebuked in all things.</margin> The unfortunate *Philostratus* <note>Flavius Philostratus (c. 170–250 AD), who wrote the biography *Life of Apollonius of Tyana*</note> had written that *Apollonius* <note>Apollonius of Tyana, a 1st-century philosopher and miracle-worker often compared to Jesus</note> asked the *Brahmans* <note>Indian priests and scholars</note> whether golden water was found among the Indians. It pertains to the glory of the art that at the same time the light of the Gospel was kindled in the Roman Emperor <note>Referring to the conversion of Constantine the Great</note>, the light of Chemistry was also kindled through public writings in the Roman world. Although that reasoning is of the weakest strength: *that no chemical writing exists older than the age of Constantine the Great, therefore Chemistry was born just the day before yesterday.* <note>original Latin: "nudius tertius," an idiom for "very recently" or "only three days ago."</note> What of this? Pliny, in *Book 30, Chapter 1*, certainly shows that arts also endure without writings, though with greater difficulty: *Before Osthanes,* <note>A legendary Persian magus and early alchemist</note> he says, *no records of the Magi existed; it is a wonder that the art was preserved through such a long age, with successions that were neither famous nor continuous.* And why should it not endure, since the whole craft can be passed down in very few words, as Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist known for his "Twelve Treatises"</note> teaches, and thus easily propagated by hand from father to sons.

<margin>The Purpose of Alchemists.</margin>
Finally, most learned Conring <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a prominent German scholar who skeptically analyzed the history of alchemy</note>, you do not consider the purpose of the alchemists; it was not their intention to encompass the art in transparent writing. Who described the art of the Memphite Pastophori? <note>Egyptian priests who carried sacred images and were often associated with secret knowledge and medicine</note> Yet you yourself acknowledge that they devoted their efforts to chemistry. The Egyptian priests spread this among the common people: *Gold and silver are the sickly colors of a sterile and corrupted soil,* as Eusebius relates from Plutarch in *Preparation for the Gospel, Book 3, Chapter 8*; yet they themselves perceived entirely different things in their minds. What Homer, Hesiod, Pindar, Apollonius of Tyana, and others have handed down concerning the Art is not understood by even one in a hundred, so enwrapped are these things in fables and tropes; meanwhile, they do not escape the attention of the keen-eyed alchemists. Regarding the others, some things have been touched upon here and there; as for Apollonius, I am not one who would either agree with Philostratus <note>Biographer of Apollonius</note> in all things, nor would I wish to take anything away from Eusebius or Tortoletus <note>Bartholomew Tortoletus, a critic of Apollonius</note> as they lash out at that man of Tyana; but I certainly observe that great men are sometimes so led by partisan zeal that while they indulge their own temperament, they even [ignore] virtues...

[virtu]es <note>Completing the word "virtutes" from the previous page</note> [which wise men nevertheless revere even in an enemy] by what right, or by what injury, they drag a hated person into blame. <margin>Apollonius is not to be scolded in all things.</margin> The unfortunate Philostratus <note>Flavius Philostratus (c. 170–250 AD), author of the *Life of Apollonius of Tyana*</note> had written that Apollonius asked the Brahmins <note>Hindu priests and sages</note> whether "golden water" was found among the Indians. Here, indeed, Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), an early Christian historian who wrote a treatise against Apollonius</note> replies mockingly: *O wonderful! O question full of wisdom!* But I do not quite understand in what way the wisdom of the Tyanean <note>Apollonius of Tyana</note> failed him here; for there were then, and are still today, gold-bearing waters of the Ganges among the Indians.

Why, even the experiments of Ercker <note>Lazarus Ercker (c. 1530–1594), a Bohemian metallurgist</note>, Agricola <note>Georgius Agricola (1494–1555), known as the "father of mineralogy"</note>, and others of this kind, show precisely how much gold and other metals are carried in the streams flowing from the rocks. Unless, perhaps, one chooses to believe the philosopher's sagacity referred to the "best water" <note>original Greek: ὕδωρ ἄριστον (*hydōr ariston*)</note> of Pindar, or that wonderful "liquid silver" <note>original Greek: ὑδράργυρον (*hydrargyron*), meaning mercury or quicksilver</note> to which all metals owe their birth. If we look more closely at that blue-colored well of astounding power, which Philostratus mentions Apollonius saw in India, we will easily recognize the characteristics of mercury in it—to say nothing of many other things. 

What if, from a certain chapter of the *Proverbs of Solomon*, carefully compared with Maimonides <note>Moses Maimonides (1138–1204), the Jewish philosopher</note> and Cornutus's *On the Nature of the Gods* <note>Lucius Annaeus Cornutus, a Stoic philosopher of the 1st century AD</note>, an attentive person could very easily extract the method of "killing" Mercury into a most brilliant red powder <note>The chemical process of calcination to create mercuric oxide, often used in alchemical transformations</note>—which in those places no mortal before me, I believe, has either sought or found.

<margin>Why did the Chemists write obscurely?</margin> 
The Chemists and those learned in Chemistry wrote before, but as Zosimus <note>Zosimus of Panopolis (c. 300 AD), a foundational alchemist</note> elegantly teaches, they wrote "ambiguously" and "indirectly" <note>original Greek: λοξῶς (*loxōs*)</note>, lest they profane the majesty of Nature’s secrets by explaining everything clearly. The fables of the ancients show anything but emptiness in their inner depths, as Plutarch <note>Plutarch (c. 46–120 AD), a Greek philosopher and biographer</note> showed at length when speaking most wisely on the physical theology of the Greeks. But he does not cease to bite at Suidas <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note>...

*Conringius* <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a German physician and scholar who doubted the ancient roots of alchemy</note>, because he had opened his mind to a view of the "golden fleece" from some ancient writer, was more favorable toward *Chemistry*. For elsewhere, the unfortunate *Suidas* <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia formerly attributed to an author named Suidas</note> had written: <note>original Greek: χημεία ἡ τοῦ ἀργύρου καὶ χρυσοῦ κατασκευή...</note> *Chemistry is the preparation of silver and gold; searching out the books on this subject, Diocletian burned them, on account of the rebellion stirred up by the Egyptians against Diocletian.* To these people he behaved cruelly and murderously; for, having searched out the books written by the ancients concerning the chemistry of gold and silver, he burned them, so that wealth might no longer accrue to the Egyptians from such an art, nor should they, trusting in an abundance of money, henceforth rise up against the Romans. *Conringius*, in order to cast

<margin>
Suidas is
vindicated
again.
</margin>
suspicion on everything, says: *Since Suidas alone relates this, who would dare safely believe him alone? He lived, as the learned judge, only five hundred years ago, and thus a full eight hundred years after Diocletian; such men are not usually fit witnesses.* But this, *Conringius*, is a manifest injustice. Even if *Suidas* lived only five centuries ago, what he wrote he did not spin out of himself like a spider, but he acknowledges that he borrowed from older authors: *Eudemus the Rhetorician, Helladius* (a contemporary of *Theodosius the Younger*), *Eugenius of Augustopolis, Zosimus of Gaza, Caecilius the Sicilian, Longinus Cassius, Lupercus of Beirut, Justinus Julius the Sophist, Pacatus, Pamphilus, Pollio of Alexandria*—all ancient writers close to the time of *Diocletian*. Thus, the testimony of *Suidas*, resting upon ancient authorities, cannot be so easily dismissed.

Yet even if *Suidas* alone said it: to a man of prudent judgment, one *Nestor* <note>The wise elder and counselor of the Greeks in the Iliad</note> should be worth more than ten *Ajaxes* <note>The powerful but often headstrong warriors of the Iliad</note>. Indeed, *Divine Chemistry* has cause to complain rightly of the tyranny of great men of letters, by whom, as if by masters...

...indeed, but in this arena of debate, it has often and not seldom been unjustly beaten by unskilled censors. Hence *Salmasius* <note>Claude Saumaise (1588–1653), a renowned French classical scholar</note> harps on nothing but "pseudo-Democrituses" and "pseudo-Osthaneses" <note>Referring to alchemical texts falsely attributed to the Greek philosopher Democritus or the legendary Persian magus Osthanes</note>. *Scaliger* <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a leading scholar of the Renaissance</note>, when he does not grasp a verse of the poet Manilius <note>Marcus Manilius, 1st-century Roman author of the *Astronomica*</note>: *“And to double the material with a certain skill of hand,”* <note>original Latin: *Materiamq; manu certâ duplicarier arte*</note> immediately taking courage from the accusation, cries out that the line was inserted by some Alchemist. Under this pretext, he preferred to be seen as a laughingstock by the people of Bourges, by Barthius, or even by gold-leaf beaters <term>bracteatores</term> <note>Craftsmen who beat gold into thin foil; the author uses this to suggest Scaliger's criticism is beneath a true scholar</note>, rather than miss an opportunity for inveighing against Chemists. Otherwise, their own rashness and the more mature judgment of posterity—who observe everything more closely—avenges those sufficiently ill-advised critics.

But *Conring* <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), the German physician who doubted chemistry's ancient pedigree</note> insists: *“To whom would it seem likely that these books were burned, and not rather preserved for the benefit of the State?”* It appears that *Conring* was either rarely present in wars, or certainly has not seen what a Prince, exasperated by the new schemes of his citizens, might sometimes dare, command, and plot. Enraged Emperors command many things with their eyes shut, of which, as their anger cools, they are led to repentance; they grant many things to the biased judgments of their courtiers.

<margin>
Diocletian did not act imprudently in burning certain chemical writings.
</margin>
Let us grant that it was deliberated in the Court of Diocletian concerning the surest way of pacifying the Egyptians; some may have answered that their chemical books should be taken away, and those architects of rebellion carried off to Rome, so that they might increase Roman wealth. More prudent men might have countered that there were more spurious books among them, leading one into error, than genuine ones; and if all were carried off indiscriminately to Rome, the people of Mars <note>The Romans, who claimed descent from the god of war</note> would perhaps cling more quickly to those vain books than to the legitimate ones, and thus that masculine virtue of warring—ingrained in the Romans from their very stock—would vanish into the smoke of the "ash-blowers" <term>ciniflones</term> <note>Originally a term for slaves who heated hair-curling irons; here used as a derogatory name for alchemists who spend their lives over bellows and furnaces</note>. What is more likely than that the mandate of the far-sighted Diocletian followed, that this material of rebellion should rather be burned, lest military minds finally...

[Page] 90

<margin>
To the Romans, Mars was more savory than Mercury.
</margin>
...lest they be excessively weakened by investigating such ambiguities? It was well known to the learned Emperor <note>Diocletian</note> that it was not the Roman way to descend to the lowly concerns of the arts, but rather to breathe the spirit of Mars and bloodshed, and to distinguish themselves in the arts of ruling. This follows the words of Anchises in Virgil:

> *Others will forge breathing bronzes more softly,*
> *I believe they will even draw living faces from marble;*
> *They will plead cases better, and describe the paths of heaven*
> *With a measuring rod, and tell of the rising stars;*
> *But you, Roman, remember to rule the nations with your power—*
> *These shall be your arts.* <note>original: *Excudent alii...* from Virgil's *Aeneid*, Book 6</note>

Egypt had been conquered many centuries before Diocletian; yet in all that time, which of the Romans learned to imitate Egyptian mummies, or understood hieroglyphics, or finished pyramids and obelisks, or mastered the countless other inventions of the Egyptians? The Poet spoke well regarding the Greeks after they were brought under the yoke:

<margin>
The Romans were negligent in the more ingenious arts.
</margin>
> *Greece, a race eloquent but poorly brave,*
> *Had not yet handed over the conquered arts to the victors.*

Why say more? We recognize the Romans' negligence in imitating the more subtle crafts even from the fact that Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, known as Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), author of *Natural History*</note> complained that the art of casting bronze for equestrian statues—once extremely well known throughout the city—had perished by his own age. And lest we think the descendants of Romulus <term>Romulidae</term> <note>A poetic name for the Roman people, as descendants of their founder Romulus</note> have only recently declined in this regard, just two years ago that more refined art of Mosaics <term>Musivaria ars</term> <note>The technique of creating images from small pieces of colored glass or stone</note> was lost again in Rome, carried to the grave with the ingenious Calandra <note>Giovanni Battista Calandra (1573–1644), a celebrated mosaic artist who worked on St. Peter's Basilica</note>. Petronius says most truly: *In ancient times,* he says, *when naked virtue still pleased, the liberal arts flourished, and there was a great competition among men to ensure that nothing which might benefit future generations should remain hidden for long. Democritus <note>Greek philosopher (c. 460–370 BC) often associated in later legends with alchemy and natural secrets</note> squeezed out the juices of every herb, and lest the hidden power of stones and shrubs remain unknown...*

The fables of the Ancients have long since been explained in terms of Chemistry.

...spent his life among experiments. But we do not even dare to learn the arts already prepared; instead, acting as accusers of Antiquity, we only teach and learn its faults. There is no time now to examine other "Sileni of Alcibiades" <note>A reference to Plato's *Symposium*, describing objects that appear ugly or plain on the outside but contain divine treasures within; the author is suggesting that ancient myths have hidden "golden" meanings</note>, that is, the many Greek fables of hidden meanings: the apples of the Hesperides [concerning which <term>Eustathius</term><note>Eustathius of Thessalonica (c. 1115–1195), a Byzantine scholar and archbishop known for his extensive commentaries on Homer</note> in his commentary on the *Iliad* gives a chemical interpretation], the Hydra defeated by Hercules, the mixtures of the souls of Mars and Venus, and others, to call them to account. *Maier* <note>Michael Maier (1568–1622), a German physician and famous alchemical author</note>, *Faber of Castelnaudary* <note>Pierre-Jean Fabre (1588–1658), a French physician who wrote extensively on the "Chymical" secrets of the universe</note>, and *Kesler* have rolled this stone, and they fly along <note>an idiom for pursuing a difficult or repetitive task with vigor</note>, while I divide the Greeks into two classes and measure out to each their own praises.

<margin>
The most ancient Greeks heard of Chemistry in Egypt, but scarcely understood it.
</margin>
Therefore, the first Greeks of several centuries, though they were very frequent visitors to Egypt, seem only to have sipped the chemical art, not drunk it deeply, if you except a very few; and so, what they brought back with them to Greece, however true and breathing of "inner Chemistry," was nevertheless closer to oracles, which they themselves—through the jealousy of the [Egyptian] Priests—did not properly understand. They were similar to the *Platonic Sibyls* <note>Inspired prophetesses described by Plato who spoke truths while in a divine trance</note>, and to those who, when driven by the gadfly of prophesying, utter the truest things, but which they do not grasp or follow once they have been restored to themselves. But how much progress either *Democritus*, *Homer*, *Pythagoras*, *Pindarus*, or finally the first *Orpheus* made in chemical practice, we shall not dispute here, content to look for the manifest traces of Chemistry in their writings, though perhaps the Authors themselves sometimes did not sufficiently understand what they had heard from the Egyptians.

<margin>
Were Hippocrates and Galen Chemists?
</margin>
*Hippocrates* certainly, in his book *On Diet*, also knew the principle of the art when he wrote: *Like clings to like, while discordant things rebel* <note>original: *Concors concordi adhæret, discordia rebellant*</note>; yet, what I wonder at like one stunned is that neither he nor *Galen* <note>The two most foundational figures of Western medicine; the author is surprised that such brilliant men did not utilize chemical distillation</note> followed the use of this sentiment to such an extent that it cannot be proved by any plausible argument that they understood even the simple process of distilla-

# ON THE ORIGIN AND #

[Page] 92

...[distil]lation of rose water, or vinegar, or any herb had become known. However they might have known other **Masteries of the Chemists** <note>original: 'Chemicorum Magisteria'; refers to advanced chemical preparations or "magisteries" used in alchemy to transform matter</note>, and described them with a **coarser skill** <note>original: 'crassiori Minerva'; a Latin idiom meaning a rough or unrefined understanding, literally "with a thicker Minerva" (the goddess of wisdom)</note>, there is no evidence they knew modern distilled waters. Plato knew of **chemical filtration** performed through wool, which he most clearly expressed in his *Symposium*. **Calcinations** <term>calcination</term><note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile substances or oxidize it, common in early chemistry to create powders</note> were known to **Hippocrates** in his *book on hemorrhoids* and elsewhere; recommending roasted flower of copper, burnt Egyptian alum, roasted chalcite <note>a copper-bearing mineral</note>, and scorched silver foam <note>likely litharge or lead oxide, a byproduct of silver refining</note>. The same man, in his noble little work *On Breaths*, came so close to the mysteries of distillations that it seems he could only have been pulled back from it by some God. *Whatever that fiery power touches,* he says, *becomes liquid, and from it spirit is made, which when it rushes to the pores of the body, becomes sweat; for the spirit, having been condensed, is turned into water, and penetrating the pores, breaks out, in exactly the same way as vapor raised from boiling water—if it finds some obstacle against which it must strike—it is thickened and densified, and drops distill from those bodies against which the vapor itself was struck.* Without doubt the great **Coan** <note>Hippocrates, so named because he was from the island of Kos</note> saw these things happening on the lids of pots and in the gymnasia of bath-keepers, and yet, what is marvelous, he did not learn to fit a "nose" <note>the "rostrum" or spout of a still (alembic) that allows collected condensation to flow into a separate vessel</note> to his lid. Unless perhaps one likes to think that Hippocrates indeed knew these things, but refused to publish them out of a justifiable secrecy, according to that passage in the *Law*: *Sacred things are shown to sacred men, but it is not lawful for the profane to see them until they have been initiated into the rites of knowledge.* <note>original Greek: τὰ δὲ ἱερὰ ἐόντα πρήγματα ἱεροῖσιν ἀνθρώποισι δείκνυται, βεβήλοις δ' οὐ θέμις, πρὶν ἢ τελεσθῶσιν ὀργίοισιν ἐπιστήμης</note> For as for what another most sagacious man thinks—that the **spirit of wine** <term>spirit of wine</term><note>an early term for ethanol or concentrated alcohol, often produced through distillation</note> was known to **Hippocrates**—it is confirmed in his book *On Generation*, where the **Phoenix of Physicians** <note>A laudatory title for Hippocrates, implying he is unique and supreme in his field</note> says: *Wine,* he says, *thrown into flames, causes the flame to leap out and increase for a short time, then cease*; this is what I would wish could be clearly [wrested] from there...

...[con]ceived, but the explanation remains ambiguous; for indeed any wine, or even any liquid poured into a flame, produces that which is better known to no one than to those in blacksmiths' workshops.

<margin>Was Chemistry understood by Aristotle?</margin>

That which **Aristotle** writes in *Meteorology, Book IV, Chapter IX*: *sweet wine emits a flame* <note>original Greek: *ανίησι φλόγα* (aniēsi phloga)</note>, has little to do with the matter, since the text proves he is speaking of **must** <term>must</term><note>freshly pressed grape juice that contains the skins, seeds, and stems; the first stage of winemaking</note>; for must, while fermentation is carried out in the cellars, sometimes catches fire in such a way that it unexpectedly suffocates the wine-merchants with its fiery vapor. However, we conclude that **spirit of wine** <term>spirit of wine</term><note>an early term for ethanol or high-proof distilled alcohol</note> was not present in Aristotle's mind from the fact that he clearly adds that this wine does not inebriate—something that even the common street-singers <note>original: 'Ambubajarum collegia'; literally "colleges of flute-players," a Roman idiom for low-class entertainers or crowds</note> would shout does not apply to distilled wine liquor. For the great **Sennert** <note>Daniel Sennert (1572–1637), a famous German physician and chemist who attempted to harmonize alchemy with Aristotelian philosophy</note> was greatly mistaken when he said: *It is common today even for the Saxons to set fire to heated wine, seasoned with sugar and spices, and thus prepare a delicate drink;* for, most celebrated Sennert, your Saxons never set fire to the wine itself, but to the **spirit of wine**, because neither your Pannonian and Tokay wines, nor Spanish, nor even those noble Greek wines, allow themselves to be inflamed—being still overwhelmed by the coldness of the water within them—unless distillations have first been performed. **Aristotle** (*Meteorology, Book II, Chapter I*) and **Galen** (*On the Use of Parts, Book VII, Chapter XIII*) also came very close to the secrets of distillation, but they were not permitted to reach the goal. However, **Galen** teaches that he achieved what is called **distillation by descent** <note>a method where the heat is applied from above and the distilled liquid flows downward into a receiver</note> <margin>Galen knew certain chemical matters.</margin> in his *Book on Easy Preparations*. Galen knew of **sublimation** <term>sublimation</term><note>the chemical process where a solid turns directly into a gas without becoming a liquid; used by early chemists to purify substances like sulfur or arsenic</note> in *Book IX of On Simple Medicines*, having perhaps been taught by **Dioscorides**, who relates the same in *Books II and V of On Medical Matter*. **Dioscorides** shows that he knew of **calcinations** <term>calcination</term><note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile substances or oxidize it</note> in *Book I of On Medical Matter*, as does Galen in *Book VI of On the Composition of Medicaments*. The **Bain-marie** <term>Bain-marie</term><note>literally "Mary's Bath" (Balneum Mariae); a water bath used to heat substances gently and evenly, traditionally attributed to "Mary the Jewess," an early alchemist</note> is likewise quite adequately [conveniently]...

...[is] described by **Galen** in Book IV of *On Protecting Health*, chapter 8, except that he substitutes oil in place of water. For in the works of **Mesue**, **Aegineta**, and **Albucasis**, the proofs of Chemistry reveal themselves more clearly. <note>Johannes Mesue (an influential Arab physician), Paul of Aegina, and Albucasis (the great Andalusian surgeon) represent a bridge between classical medicine and later chemical pharmacology.</note> **Galen** saw **lye salts** <term>lye salts</term><note>alkaline salts, such as potash, obtained by soaking wood ash in water and evaporating the liquid</note> only as if through a lattice <note>original Latin: *per transennam*; a common idiom for a brief or superficial glimpse</note>, but hardly ever in a solid form. In *On the Properties of Simple Medicines*, chapter 41, he pursues the matter thus: *Lye is produced by washing ash. The Latins call the remains of burnt bodies "ash," whether they are stony in nature, or remnants of trees or animals. Therefore, lye is made according to the nature of that which is washed out; that which has a very sharp "empyreuma" <note>an ancient term for the "smell of fire" or the fiery essence remaining in charred matter</note> or fiery nature is itself very sharp; that which is not so is more moderate. Therefore, it is mixed with those properties called "septic" <note>In this context, "septic" refers to corrosive or digestive substances that can dissolve or break down flesh</note>, since it possesses a caustic heat; but because of the thinness of its substance, it burns without pain.* The nature

of lye salts is expressed well enough here by **Galen**, where he teaches that they are caustic but burn without pain; however, he does not seem to have grasped the method of reducing them into grains and salty clumps. ### ON THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

...the common folk have carried their experiments further than **Galen**. 
<margin>Conringius attacks lye salts in vain.</margin>
But what does **Conringius** <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a German physician and scholar who often criticized the chemical medicines of the Paracelsians.</note> say to this? *There is no reason,* he says, *to praise the Paracelsians much for the discovery of these salts; for why would you give those to a sick man when far more healthy things with the same powers are available? Certainly, it is well known that substances which are similar to lime and possess a caustic power should not be taken rashly by mouth.* Here **Conringius** strives against the current; for why should those things not be taken by mouth which, over the long lapse of years, the people of **Umbria** <note>A region in central Italy.</note> and the **Rhine** have used as daily seasonings for food without any loss of health? Or those which today the inhabitants of the **Ocean** coast everywhere create from seaweed <note>original Latin: *alga*; used here to describe the production of soda ash or potash from kelp.</note> burnt to ash and use without harm? Which even today Physicians successfully administer to check the worsening of fevers? Truly, these salts are not related to quicklime; nor, as **Galen** correctly teaches, are they caustic except without pain; nor is anything more powerful than they are in disarming that hostile acidity of the internal organs. In short, let **Conringius** look upon a thousand successful experiments attempted with the salt of **wormwood**, **centaury**, **blessed thistle**, **broom**, **juniper**, and others, and undoubtedly, as a more circumspect judge, he will now soften his criticism. And so that he might believe more effectively, we add that passage of **Pliny** from Book 36, chapter 27, borrowed from **Marcus Varro**: *Lix,* he says, *is the ash of the hearth; for from it, a drink of lye-ash <term>lye-ash</term><note>Latin: *cinis lixivus*; a solution made from ashes, essentially a crude form of potassium carbonate.</note> provides a cure; as one may see with gladiators, who, when they have finished their bouts, find relief in this drink.*

<margin>Was Dioscorides a Chemist?</margin>
However, there is a just suspicion that **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), the Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*.</note> caught the scent of something that **Galen** did not penetrate, both because in Book V of *On Medical Matters*, chapter 110, he expressly mentioned the **alembic** <term>alembic</term><note>A chemical apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for distillation.</note>, that most well-known instrument for distillations, which was also touched upon by **Hesychius**. **Dioscorides** says: *the cup of the scorched vessel* <note>original Greek: περικαύστου ἄμβικα (perikaustou ambika)</note>: *smearing it round with clay.* For other words, such as *Almanac*, *Almagest*, and *Alchemy*, teach us that the particle *Al* is a "makeweight" <note>An addition or prefix.</note> of the **Arabs**.

# ON THE ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]

...originating from Arabic idioms; then finally, because ***Dioscorides*** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician and botanist whose work was the primary text on pharmacology for 1,500 years.</note> was an attendant to that most extravagant of women, ***Cleopatra***. According to ***Pliny***, she suddenly dissolved that famous pearl <term>unio<gloss>A large, high-quality pearl, so named because each was unique.</gloss></term> by plunging it into a draught of vinegar in the sight of ***Antony***, and then swallowed it. We do not observe such powers in Roman or Greek vinegar today, and being certain of the truth of the history, we interpret that liquid as having been tempered by the skill of a Chemist. <margin>The Chemical Cleopatra.</margin> For the works of the *Chemical Cleopatra* <note>A pseudonym used by early alchemical authors; the author suggests the historical queen may have possessed alchemical secrets.</note> still exist, even if she perhaps only provided the inspiration for this dissolution, while ***Dioscorides*** provided the hand; as it is customary for the more menial tasks of the great arts to be delegated to servants. <margin>Distillations of today unknown to Galen.</margin> Moreover, that ***Galen*** was blind to the secrets of distillations is proven by what he mournfully proposes in *Book I of On the Power of Simple Medicines*:

> *I am not far,* he says, *from being willing to undergo all dangers, if some machine or art might be found to separate the contrary parts in vinegar, just as one can in milk.*

But if he had understood the machine of distillation, he would have easily and in a few hours elicited from vinegar the phlegm, spirit, oil, and earth—the four distinct elements.

<margin>Chemistry migrates from the Egyptians to the Greeks and the more curious Romans.</margin>
Thus, while the ***Egyptians*** most greedily guarded their arts, only a few ***Greeks*** in the centuries preceding the time of the Savior penetrated the inner chambers of Chemistry. However, from the time when ***Egypt***—after being thoroughly traversed by ***Cornelius Gallus***, ***Julius Caesar***, ***Antony***, and ***Augustus***—was reduced to the form of a province, and the inner sanctuaries of the Priests were more violently shaken open, the secrets of Chemistry gradually migrated to the ***Greeks*** and the more curious of the Romans (though these were few). Hence today, entire shelves are stuffed with the Chemical writings of the ***Greeks***, partly translated into Latin and published, and partly still lying hidden in the ***Vatican***, ***Venetian***, ***Royal Parisian***, ***Escorial***, ***Cologne***, and ***Munich*** libraries. Indeed, he recounted that he himself had seen men in Greece who had advanced so far in knowledge through the writings of *Zosimus*, *Olympiodorus*, and *Archelaus* that they actually demonstrated the truth of the art <note>The "truth of the art" usually refers to successful transmutation or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> before his very eyes. Stirred by this, unless the fates of his advanced age interfere, he will soon grant to the public the *Physics and Mystics of Democritus*, *Olympiodorus*, and *Heliodorus* (dedicated to the great Emperor *Theodosius*), as well as *Theophrastus*, *Hierotheus*, *Archelaus*, and several anonymous works brought forth from the Vatican Library. For the rest, the illustrious *Zosimus of Panopolis* <note>Zosimus of Panopolis (c. 300 AD) is one of the most significant historical figures in the transition from Egyptian metallurgy to Greek alchemy.</note> leaves all of these far behind him; he is followed, though at an unequal distance, by *Anepigraphus*, *Michael Psellus*, *Pelagius*, *Isis*, *Blemmidas*, *Nicephorus*, and others who have been studied in the Royal Library of Paris, formerly by *Labbe* <note>Philippe Labbe (1607–1667), a French Jesuit scholar and bibliographer.</note> and recently by me, and for the most part transcribed. For as far as the Romans are concerned, they were late to set a price on the conquered arts; hence, if anything was to be fashioned into statues, it was left to the hands of the Greeks...

### ON THE ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY] 98

<margin>Caligula’s hope turned to hopelessness.</margin>
...things wonderful for your use, noble in architecture, illustrious in paintings, and ingenious in medicine, though they themselves were mostly intent on the arts of ruling. Only Gaius Caligula, among the first Caesars as far as we know, longed for the service of chemistry to obtain wealth that would soon be squandered. Regarding him, Pliny says in *Book XXXIII, Chapter IV*: *“Hope had invited the Prince Gaius, who was most greedy for gold; for which reason he ordered a great weight of <term>orpiment</term> <note>original: "auripigmentum," a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral. Its name literally means "gold pigment" because of its color and the ancient belief that it contained gold.</note> to be smelted: and he clearly produced excellent gold, but of such small weight that he suffered a loss.” Thus Pliny.* 

Evidently, Caligula had undoubtedly learned from the writings of the Chemists that arsenic was required for the completion of the Philosophical Work <note>The "Philosophical Work" refers to the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note>, for the Greek writers groan about nothing more frequently. Hence, since he knew that Syrian orpiment was the "mother" of arsenic, he vainly consumed his greedy hopes in smelting it—but did so foolishly. 

Indeed, it is certain that the riddle of the ancient Sibyl—<note>original Greek: "'Εννέα γράμματ' ἔχω, τετρασύλλαβός εἰμι, νόει μέ"</note> *“I have nine letters, I am four syllables, consider me”*—is fitted to no one more suitably than to arsenic, the offspring of orpiment. This remains true despite whatever objections might be raised by <term>Jean Dorat</term> <note>Latinized as Jo. Auratus (1508–1588), a French poet and scholar known for interpreting riddles.</note> or <term>Athanasius Kircher</term> <note>A famous 17th-century Jesuit polymath and scholar of "hidden" sciences.</note>. The matter itself demonstrates that by "arsenic" (or *arrhenicum*), the Sibyl and the Greek authors understood nothing other than that "masculine sulfur" by which Mercury is forced into obedience <note>In alchemical theory, "masculine sulfur" was the active principle that "fixed" or solidified the volatile "feminine" Mercury.</note>. But let us look at that verse of the Sibyl:

<margin>The Sibyl on Arsenic.</margin>
> I have nine letters, I am four syllables, consider me;
> The first three syllables have two letters each,
> The remaining syllables have the rest; and five of them are consonants,
> Of the whole number, the hundreds are twice eight [1600],
> And three times three tens [90] with seven [7] <note>This refers to the practice of gematria, where letters represent numbers. The total numerical value described here is 1697. While scholars debate the meaning, the author argues it spells out the Greek word for Arsenic.</note>; 
> Once you know who I am, you shall not be uninitiated into the wisdom I possess.

The first, as far as I know, of the previous century to scent that the Sibyl hid a mystery here was <term>Aimery de Ranconet</term> <note>Latinized as Emarus Ranconetus (1498–1559), a French magistrate and scholar.</note>, President of Paris, who...

...to introduce it to Chemistry, and he fitted it with most happy skill to the word <term>Arsenikon</term> <note>original Greek: ’Αρσενικὸν, meaning arsenic</note>, as is found in Cardano <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), an Italian polymath</note> in his book *On the Variety of Things, Chapter 10*. <margin>
Why there is no hope in common arsenic.
</margin>
Furthermore, both reason and experienced hands <note>literally "eyes-in-the-hands," referring to practical laboratory experience</note> persuade us that the material of the Great Work <note>The "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) is the alchemical process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note> is not to be sought in common arsenic. For although it may superficially tint copper and imbue it with a more noble color, it has a hateful fleetingness; when it comes to the punishment of the flames, it does not wait—as Geber <note>An influential 8th-century Arabic alchemist</note> rightly notes—for the battle of the fire, but rushes away and defiles the nearby air with a poisonous stench. If by the ingenious torture of simple water [which I remember happened to me when weighing all other options], it is strengthened by long custom to endure the fires constantly, it afterwards does not enter the bodies of the metal, but floats upon the molten mass like a little ashen dust. With <term>orpiment</term> <note>yellow arsenic sulfide</note> and <term>realgar</term> <note>original: "sandaracha," here referring to the red mineral form of arsenic, not the tree resin</note>, the result is almost the same: orpiment is driven upward by its own nature by the force of fire; and realgar <gloss>Rauschgelb</gloss> [does not do so] unless there are mixed in either sands or some ash...

# ON THE ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]

...whether metallic or vegetable. That which ascends from both—if indeed it adheres to the top cover or the <term>alembic</term> <note>A distillation apparatus consisting of two retorts connected by a tube; the "head" or "cap" of the still</note> like a spongy little powder—is a terrible poison. If, by a subtle discovery, it has been accustomed to fires, it will no longer allow itself to be swallowed up by the Moon.

<margin>Which arsenic was approved by the Sibyl?</margin>
Therefore, although almost all the Greek chemical writers—Zosimus, Olympiodorus, Anepigraphus, and others—are extensively occupied with praises of Arsenic, one must nonetheless think that they were looking at the power of the word, not at common usage. Since <term>arsenikon</term> <note>original Greek: ἀρσενικὸν</note> or <term>arrhenikon</term> <note>original Greek: ἀρρενικὸν</note> signifies something "masculine" in the Greek idiom, the ancient Sibyl here showed through a cloud a certain sulfur tireless against all fires; for the school of Chemists composes even the more noble metals from sulfur as the "male" and Mercury as the "female" principle. 

Thus it may be clear from this how foresightedly <term>Hermolaus Barbarus</term> <note>An influential 15th-century Italian scholar and "restorer" of classical texts who sought to reconcile ancient science with modern observation</note>, the restorer of more refined literature, once wrote: *Arsenic or Arrhenicum is sometimes called "man-tamer" <note>original: androdamanta</note> by the Democritean chemists, sometimes "Scythian comarin," and sometimes these very names are applied to the sulfur of the Philosophers' Stone.* But hurrying on to another leaf of the Sibyl, leaving aside the matters concerning the common Sibylline poems which Schmid, Blondel, and others laboriously debate, let us fix our eyes for a moment on the oracles of the Cumean Sibyl touched upon by Virgil.

<margin>The Cave of the Sibyl, or the Sibyl's?</margin>

...yielding <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page: "...sulfur as a yielding [or masculine principle]"</note> [for neither does that statement of *Petronius of Trogir* <note>Petronius Arbiter, a Roman courtier; 'Tragurianus' refers to the discovery of a manuscript of his work *Satyricon* in Trogir/Trau</note> contradict it: *For I myself saw with my own eyes the Sibyl at Cumae hanging in a flask; and when those boys said to her, "Sibyl, what do you want?", she would answer, "I want to die"* <note>original: "apothan in helo," a Latinized corruption of the Greek "apothanein thelo"</note>]; yet whether she poured forth those oracles at ancient *Cumae*, or in the mountains neighboring that city, I shall not interrupt her declaiming, provided I am permitted to weigh these words of hers from *Virgil’s Aeneid, Book VI*. Perhaps these were not even sufficiently understood by *Virgil* himself when he sang them, having borrowed them from another:

<margin>Virgil preaches Chemically.</margin>
> *Hear what must be performed first. Hidden in a shady tree*
> *is a bough, golden in both its leaves and its pliant stem;*
> *it is declared sacred to the underworld Juno <note>Proserpina, the queen of the underworld</note>: the whole grove*
> *covers it, and shadows enclose it in dark valleys.*
> *But it is not granted to go beneath the hidden places of the earth*
> *before someone has plucked the gold-haired growth from the tree.*
> *Fair Proserpina has established that this be brought to her as her own*
> *gift: when the first is torn away, a second golden one does not*
> *fail, and a branch flourishes with the same metal.*
> *Therefore, search deep with your eyes, and when it is rightly found,*
> *pluck it with your hand; for if the fates call you, it will follow*
> *willingly and easily: otherwise, you will not be able to conquer it*

It is the opinion of various men—among whom are *Robertus Vallensis* <note>The first historian of alchemy (16th century)</note>, *Glauber* <note>Johann Rudolf Glauber, a famous 17th-century chemist known for "Glauber's Salt"</note>, and others—that the *Cumaean* prophetess poured forth these lines regarding the material of the Chemical mastery <note>The *magisterium*, or the process of creating the Philosopher’s Stone</note>. Nor can it be denied that a secret meaning lies hidden under that cloak of a fable, perhaps unknown even to *Virgil* himself, who borrowed these things from another source. Whoever calls into more exquisite counsel the *Glaura* of *Augurellus* <note>Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli, author of the alchemical poem *Chrysopoeia*</note> and the Arcadian youth of *Palingenius* <note>Marcellus Palingenius, author of the *Zodiacus Vitae*</note> will perhaps interpret this more correctly than *Maro* <note>Virgil's cognomen</note> himself. At the mention of that golden bough, there occurs to me not only that passage from the learned *Joseph Acosta’s Natural History*...

# OF THE ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]

<margin>The Philosophical Tree germinating in acidic waters.</margin>
*Book IV, Chapter I.* <note>The author appears to be quoting from a Spanish source here, likely Alvaro Alonso Barba’s *Arte de los metales* (1640), a famous treatise on metallurgy.</note> Metals are like plants hidden in the bowels of the earth, and they have a certain resemblance in the way they are produced, for one also sees their branches, and a trunk from which they emerge, and in some way it seems that minerals grow in the manner of plants; <note>original Spanish: "Los metales son como plantas encubiertas en las entrannas de la terra, y tienen alguna semejanza en el modo de produzir se, pues se veen tambien sus ramos, y como tronco de donde salen, y en alguna manera parece, qve crecen los minerales al modo de plantas"</note> but that metallic germination which we observe occurring in acidic liquors, when Mercury <term>Mercury (quicksilver)</term> encounters the atoms of metals, especially comes to mind. This is true even though Bartoletus <note>Fabrizio Bartoletti (1576–1630), an Italian physician and chemist.</note> in his *Hermetic Encyclopedia*, Petrus Ribola, Athanasius Kircher, and others have described the method of operation at great length; they remain rather indifferent to the causes of such an unexpected and swift production of a "thicket" appearing almost out of nothing. Although many metallic germinations still lie hidden in the "well of Democritus," <note>A metaphor for the depth and difficulty of attaining true knowledge.</note> one most elegant variety, not unworthy of a curious leisure, was born among the metallurgical labors of **Lazarus Ercker** <note>A famous German mineralogist and author of *Beschreibung allerfürnemisten mineralischen Ertzt und Bergwerksarten* (1574).</note> and is fully described in his *Metallic Works*. I also well remember one generated for me in Paris from small bits of Silver <note>original: "Lunæ minutiis"</note> cooked in a unique balsam of sulfur.

<margin>
A wondrous
but true
story of
the metallic

[The Duke of] Enghien <note>The "Duke of Enghien" likely refers to Louis II de Bourbon, Prince of Condé (1621–1686), a famous French general who lived in Brussels during his exile in the 1650s.</note> left Brussels for the sake of his studies, accompanied by a great retinue of wise men. Among them was Saint-Simon, originally from Heidelberg, who through long habit had become French, and is today secretary to the said Prince. He is a man who, if any other, is German in spirit and loyalty, and entirely averse to trifles. Therefore, a certain man from Liège <note>Liège was a major center for mining and metallurgy in the 17th century.</note>, of unknown face and name and modest in his dress, approached this Saint-Simon while he was living in Brussels. As often happens for the sake of pleasure, Saint-Simon was quite diligently investigating the secrets of Nature during his leisure time. The stranger said: *"I hear, sir, that you sometimes stoop to chemical concerns and delight your mind with this noble leisure; but you depend too much on masters who are ignorant of secret things, and therefore you have seen nothing wonderful so far. Look! So that you may see with your own eyes that not everything claimed by chemical study is vain, I entrust to your keeping this tiny powder—as you see, it is small and hardly equal to a single grain of barley in weight. I do so on the condition that you return it to me whole when I come back in six hours. In the meantime, to satisfy your curiosity, proceed as follows: take three or four pounds of river water, or any other spring water, and after catching it in a glass, sprinkle this tiny powder into it. Then, having repeated the task, shake the vessel from time to time so that the water, by mixing with the powder, may wear away some small part of it and incorporate it into itself. After a few hours have passed, pour off the liquid into another vessel, dry the settling powder, and return it to me in good faith."*

Saint-Simon obeyed and showed himself compliant with what was said, though uncertain of the outcome. The man from Liège returned toward evening, received his powder back, and turning to Saint-Simon, said: *"Now that I am leaving, test the strength of your water, and having poured in several ounces of common Mercury <term>Mercury (quicksilver)</term>, expect a useful germination."* <note>In alchemy, "germination" refers to the rapid, plant-like growth of metallic crystals in a solution.</note> And saying a few more words back and forth, he departed. Saint-Simon, most eager to carry out the task successfully, soon [took] ounces of Mercury...

### DE ORTU ET <note>original: "On the Origin and..." (part of the title "On the Origin and Progress of Chemistry")</note>

<margin>
The Ancients, when they most show themselves to be dealing in trifles, are philosophizing most subtly.
</margin>

Meanwhile, to return at last to those *Virgilian* golden boughs <note>A reference to the Golden Bough in the *Aeneid*, which the author interprets as an alchemical symbol.</note>, it will be enough to observe here that the Ancients, when they prepared to philosophize most wisely about natural things, commonly showed themselves to be offering only fables or ridiculous banquet entertainments. This is abundantly clear from that banquet of the Phaeacians in *Homer's Odyssey, Book 8*, where the harper recounts the union of Mars and Venus with the most chemical artifice <note>original: χημικωτάτῳ (*chemikōtatō*), meaning "in a most chemical manner."</note>, as if he were doing something else entirely. However, it is not my plan to wander away from *Virgil* now; for he not only introduces *Silenus*—barely awakened from a drunken stupor in *Eclogue VI*—speaking most wisely about the Nature of things:

## AELIA LAELIA CRISPIS.

> *Neither Man, nor Woman, nor Hermaphrodite;*
> *Neither Girl, nor Youth, nor Old Woman;*
> *Neither chaste, nor a whore, nor modest,*
> *but all of these;*
> *Taken away neither by hunger, nor by the sword, nor by poison,*
> *but by all of these;*
> *She lies neither in heaven, nor in the waters, nor in the earth,*
> *but everywhere.*
> *Lucius Agatho Priscius,*
> *Neither Husband, nor Lover, nor Kinsman;*
> *Neither grieving, nor rejoicing, nor weeping,*
> *Knows and knows not for whom he has placed this,*
> *neither a mass, nor a pyramid, nor a tomb,*
> *but all of these.*
> *This is a tomb having no corpse inside;*
> *This is a corpse having no tomb outside;*
> *But the corpse and its tomb are the same.*

<margin>Explained in various ways.</margin>
Others interpret this most ancient riddle <note>original: "gryphum." While a gryphus is literally a griffin, in this context it refers to a complex enigma or riddle.</note> in different ways: Marius Michael Angelus as rainwater; Agathias as Niobe turned into stone; Richardus as the rational soul; Joannes Turrius as the <term>First Matter</term> <note>original: "materia prima," the formless base of all substance in alchemy.</note>; Gevartius as Love; Zaccharias Pontinus as three deceased persons joined together; and Nicolaus Barnaudus as the <term>Philosophical Stone</term> <note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note>.

### ON THE ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]

...said. <note>The text begins mid-sentence, likely completing a thought from the previous page.</note> Kircher, in his *Subterranean World* <note>original: "Mundo Subterraneo," a 1665 work by the Jesuit polymath Athanasius Kircher.</note>, approaches the matter in another way, and more explicitly. *He says, "There never were lamps that burned perpetually; rather, a flame was seen from stagnant air enclosed for many centuries, which was suddenly ignited either by the first breath of those entering, or by the access of air entering from outside through a certain antiperistatic power.* <note>Antiperistasis was a historical theory where a quality—like heat or light—is intensified when surrounded by its opposite.</note> *In this way, small flames are often seen in cemeteries. An Arab author said it well: There was in Egypt, he says, a field whose trenches were full of liquid bitumen; from here, philosophers constructed channels, and from these places to subterranean crypts, in which they placed a lamp; the lamp had a wick made of linen that cannot be burned;* <note>original: "lino quod uri nequit," likely referring to asbestos, which was known as "mountain flax" or "asbestos linen."</note> *in this way, the lamp, once lit, burned forever because of the perpetual flow of bitumen."*

I myself confess that I have not gained enough [knowledge]—neither from the work of *Fortunio Liceti* <note>Fortunio Liceti (1577–1657), who wrote a famous treatise "De Reconditis Antiquorum Lucernis" (On the Hidden Lamps of the Ancients).</note> *On the Lamps of the Ancients*, nor from the histories extant in the authors *Hermolaus Barbarus, Francesco Maturanzio, Bernardino Scardeone*, and others, nor from what I have heard in *France, Italy,* and *Germany*—to conclude by any effort of the mind that anything truly burned in tombs for so long. However, I readily agree with *Kircher* that something bright and sparkling has occasionally appeared there and then vanished once air was admitted. I would think that the lamp of *Olybius* was of a type of very subtle oils, and that it gave back a splendor to the lamp because it reflected the rays of light falling upon it into the eyes of those standing by, through the impact of its smooth and highly polished particles. 

For, to pass over *Manfredo Settala* <note>Manfredo Settala (1600–1680), a famous Milanese collector and scientist.</note>—a most excellent man who solemnly affirmed to me in *Milan* that he had found in ancient lachrymatory vials <note>Small glass vessels found in Roman tombs, once thought to hold the tears of mourners.</note> a type of unknown oil that gilded the stones it touched—I certainly learned in *Prague* not long ago that in a chemist's workshop, from <term>saltpeter|nitrum</term> alone, boiled with long patience, a body emerged like a <term>carbuncle|A legendary red gemstone believed to glow in the dark.</term>, both glowing in the dark and [shining...]

<meta>This page continues the author's skepticism regarding "perpetual lamps." After discussing Athanasius Kircher's theories on bitumen and stagnant air on the previous page, the author now provides a first-hand account of his own archaeological investigations in Rome and uses a chemical observation about ancient glass to challenge a common anatomical belief about the human eye.</meta>

# THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

...preserving its brilliance for some time, even in a cold vessel. While I leave the credibility of this statement as an open question, I know that two years ago in Rome, on the *Clivus Scauri* <note>A Roman street running up the Caelian Hill, famous for its ancient ruins.</note>, an entire house was unearthed. It had been buried for ten centuries or more beneath the roots of artichokes <note>original: "scolymi," likely referring to the golden thistle or wild artichoke.</note> and other herbs in the garden of a certain citizen. Amidst the ruins of those most beautiful buildings of Corinthian workmanship, I diligently searched for ancient lamps of this kind among the rubble, but in vain.

<margin>
Why does glass turn into thin plates through age?
</margin>

However, I did find many lachrymatory vessels <note>term: "vasa lacrymalia," small glass bottles found in ancient Roman tombs, long thought by early antiquarians to hold the tears of mourners.</note> made of glass. Having been overcome by the long passage of time, these had separated into many thin layers <note>original: "philyras," a term for the inner bark of a tree or thin sheets of papyrus, used here to describe the flaking of weathered glass.</note>, which were most elegantly distinguished by iridescent, peacock-like colors. These layers were easily split into leaves—similar to Muscovy talc <note>term: "talco Moscovitico," also known as Muscovy glass or mica, a mineral that peels into thin, transparent sheets and was often used for windows.</note>, if you were to remove the colors. This was caused by salt—either innate to the glass or from the surrounding earth—which created these little plates because it pressed equally everywhere upon the neighboring parts. Now that these layers are separated, reason dictates that the salt, having spread into the nearby parts of the glass, exercised the same tyranny until the entire mass of glass (which was thick at the start, and is still thick in some places) gradually degenerated into removable scales.

From this, one can conclude that the anatomists are mistaken when they assert that the crystalline humor of the eye <note>term: "humor oculi crystallinus," what we now call the lens of the eye.</note> consists of layers. They are led to this belief simply because the lens allows itself to be separated into thin sheets after it has been boiled.

<margin>
The crystalline humor of the eyes does not consist of layers.
</margin>

For the same fate has befallen the eye as befell the lachrymatory glasses. Indeed, while the heat—which also spreads its particles equally—first works its way to a certain depth of that crystalline humor, it compels and thickens it more powerfully. Then, as new and repeated heat follows, it penetrates deeper into the crystalline body, causing a new condensation; but this new layer is easily separated from the previous one due to the differently arranged particles. Similar things often happen to the boiled white of an egg. But this is "beyond the olive trees" <note>original: "extra oleas," a Latin idiom meaning to wander off-topic or stray from the main point.</note>. More to the point is that caustic waters <note>term: "aqvas causticas," likely referring to nitric acid or other mineral acids, commonly called *aqua fortis* or "strong water" by alchemists.</note> (which they call "strong") are like...

<sig>O 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: lucer-</meta>

<summary>The author recounts searching for perpetual lamps in a Roman excavation and describes how ancient glass decomposes into iridescent layers. He uses this chemical observation to argue that the layered structure seen in a boiled eye lens is an artifact of the cooking process rather than its natural state.</summary>

### ON THE ORIGIN AND...

...perpetually burning lamps, not because they shine forever, but because they almost perpetually sear nearly anything they touch, in the manner of fire. Hence, if you pour spirit of turpentine <term>Spirit of turpentine: A volatile oil distilled from pine resin, used in early chemistry and medicine for its solvent and flammable properties.</term> over a similar water, it will gradually be tinged with a deep red color, just as if it had been cooked for a long time by fires; indeed, it sometimes ignites with a great noise, which I recall happened to me as well. 

<margin>
*Conring is headstrong again.* <note>original: "Iterum præceps Conringius."</note>
</margin>

That claim of *Julius Caesar Scaliger* <note>A famous Italian scholar and physician (1484–1558) known for his works on natural philosophy.</note>, which he attributes to the Chemists, has long since been exploded: *that Juniper coals possess such power that they keep fire uncorrupted for an entire year.* To this, *Conringius* <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a German polymath who was often skeptical of alchemical claims.</note> says liberally: *That is indeed so far from the truth that a juniper ember cannot survive even a single day*; a point which *Porta* <note>Giambattista della Porta (1535–1615), an Italian scholar and occultist.</note> also rightly noted. But in this matter, *Conringius* was not sufficiently cautious, not noticing that principle of the *Legal Scholars*: *A slight circumstance often changes the whole case*, about which I may speak elsewhere; just as regarding a certain herbaceous seed very common in our fields, which in these very days, after it had once glowed in the fire at my house, was removed and placed in a cold spot, yet I found it still glowing and searing for as many as fifty solid hours.

<margin>
*Perpetual heat is attempted in various ways,*
</margin>

Moreover, various men attempt to achieve perpetual heat in various ways: some by the rotation of wheels—now by water, now air, now by weights; others by mixing salts of a contrary kind; others by searching out and imitating the elements of natural hot springs—in which *Henricus Rochas* <note>Henri de Rochas, a 17th-century French physician who wrote on mineral waters and "demonstrations" of nature.</note> is industrious, in his *Physical Demonstrations*; others by investigating the continuous fuel of *Etna*, *Vesuvius*, and *Hecla* <note>Mount Hecla, a famous volcano in Iceland.</note>; 

<margin>
*and ingeniously by Boucaudius,*
</margin>

concerning all of which the most noble *Abbot Boucaudius*, most friendly to me, and an excellent candidate of more secret nature in the rest of his greatness of intellect, has devised something even more ingenious. He fixes a spear of about twenty feet into somewhat spongy earth, and once a deep hole has been formed, and from...

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of heat and chemical properties. ### THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

...safe from accidental falls, he pours in about ten pounds of **living Mercury** <term>Living Mercury: Quicksilver or elemental mercury, prized by alchemists for its extreme density and liquid state at room temperature.</term>, which, by its own weight and the yielding nature of the softer earth below, is gradually depressed; finally, by a descent creeping along for several months, it is moved toward the lowest parts of the earth, so that it eventually slips into the cradles of subterranean heat. Once this is done, a tireless fervor—as if in the thermal baths—exhales through the overlying hole, and refreshes the glass and other objects placed upon it with a moderate warmth. Thus, besides the glory of a subtle invention, the heat is perpetuated thereafter at no expense. 

However, he acknowledged along with me that an equally happy success of this artifice cannot be hoped for in all places, both because of the solidity of the earth below, and because of stones sometimes obstructing the path of the Mercury as it hastens toward **Tartarus** <note>original: "Tartara" — the deep abyss of the underworld in Greek mythology, used here to describe the deep interior of the earth.</note>, and because of springs of water in some way inhibiting the momentum of the quicksilver. 

Whatever the case may be, the genius of the invention gains light even from the fact that not only does *Acosta* in his *History of the Indies, book III, chapter XIX, report that in **Huancavelica**, Peru, from where the mineral of Mercury is extracted, there is a perennial spring of hot water;* <note>Jose de Acosta (1540–1600) was a Jesuit missionary who wrote extensively on the natural history of the Americas.</note> but also that there is a solemn complaint that the floodgates and embankments of millers [by which the water necessary for turning the mills is held back to suffice for the work] are sometimes perforated by Mercury injected by hostile hands, causing the loss of both water and mills. Furthermore, it is known to everyone and proven that deeper underground chambers and wells, when all other things are bound by the rigor of winter, belch tepid smoke into the air, with the interior bowels of the earth steadily supplying the material of heat.

...[instru]ment, so that it would be flexible, and that the entire workshop of its creator was destroyed, lest the value of copper, silver, and gold metals be diminished: and that rumor was for a long time more common than it was certain. 

Petronius of Trogir <note>original: "Petronius Tragurianus" — referring to the Roman author Petronius Arbiter, whose work was rediscovered in a manuscript at Trogir (Tragurium) in the 17th century.</note> follows up on the same matter, but a bit more expansively: "There was once a craftsman," he says, "who made a glass vial that would not break. He was admitted to Caesar <note>Tiberius Caesar, who reigned from 14–37 AD.</note> with his gift; then he had it handed back to Caesar and threw it onto the stone floor. Caesar was as terrified as he could possibly be; but the man picked the vial up from the ground: it was dented, just like a bronze vessel. Then he pulled a small hammer from his pocket and neatly corrected the vial at his leisure; in this way, he thought he held Jupiter by the balls <note>original: "coleum Jovis" — a vulgar Latin idiom from the Satyricon meaning to be extremely lucky or to have "conquered the world."</note>. At length, after Caesar said to him: 'Does anyone else know this recipe for making glass? Think carefully.' After the man denied it, Caesar ordered him to be beheaded; for if this were known, we would hold gold as cheap as mud." You recognize the senselessness of Tiberius, listeners, and you spit upon it, while at the same time you gaze upon the wonderful craftsmanship of those times.

But even if we suppose Petronius of Trogir mixes in some falsehoods, certainly **Cassius Dio** is not false, who writes the same things in Book 57 [of his *Roman History*]; nor is **John of Salisbury** false, who in the *Policraticus* <note>A 12th-century work of political philosophy and ethics.</note>, Book 4, Chapter 5, says so eloquently: *In Petronius, Trimalchio <note>The wealthy freedman character in Petronius’s Satyricon who tells this story at a banquet.</note> reports that there was a craftsman who made glass vessels of such toughness that they broke no more easily than those of gold or silver. When he had manufactured a vial of this kind from the purest glass, and one worthy (as he thought) of Caesar alone, he approached Caesar with his gift and was admitted. The appearance of the gift was praised, the hand of the craftsman commended, and the devotion of the giver accepted. But the craftsman, in order to turn the admiration of the onlookers into total shock and to more fully win the Emperor's favor, took back the vial he had requested from Caesar’s hand and threw it quite violently onto the floor, with such force that not even the most solid and unchanging bronze material would have remained unharmed. Caesar, however, was not yet more*

[Caesar] was as amazed as he was terrified. But the man lifted the vial from the ground; it was not broken, but dented, as if the substance of bronze had taken on the appearance of glass. Then, pulling a small hammer from his robe, he corrected the glass most skillfully, and repaired it with frequent blows just like a dented bronze vessel. Having done this, he thought he held "the heaven of Jupiter" <note>original: "caelum Jovis" — a play on the vulgar idiom "coleum Jovis" (Jupiter's balls) used on the previous page, suggesting the craftsman believed he had reached the height of divine favor.</note>, because he believed he had earned the friendship of Caesar and the admiration of all. But it happened otherwise. For Caesar asked whether anyone else knew this method of tempering glass. When the man denied it, the Emperor ordered him to be beheaded, saying: "Because if this craftsmanship became known, gold and silver would become as cheap as mud." Whether this report is true and faithful is uncertain, and different people feel differently about Caesar's deed. I, however, without prejudice to the understanding of wiser men, judge that the devotion of the powerful craftsman was poorly rewarded, and that the outlook for the human race was damaged to no purpose, since an excellent art was destroyed. Nor indeed do Pliny or Petronius show themselves to doubt the truth of the experiment here, but since neither was initiated into the sacred Chemical <note>Referring here to alchemy, the "sacred art" of material transformation.</note> rites, they did not grasp the sublimity of the matter; between the distrust of others and their own ignorance, they leave the truth of the history to be judged by the decision of those who understand. Thus, a Chemist might define the matter more certainly; one who is convinced not only by the writers of his art—Libavius, Goclenius, Quattramio, Valensis, <note>These are prominent 16th and 17th-century authors on alchemy and early chemistry: Andreas Libavius, Rudolf Goclenius, Evangelista Quattrami, and Robertus Vallensis.</note> and others—that glass can be rendered ductile by art, but also that crystals can be advanced to a hardness, brilliance, and <margin>Chemical Gems</margin> constancy equal to natural gems. <margin>It is believable that ductile glass once existed.</margin>
Furthermore, so that the stubbornness of those who deny this might not be considered invincible, they should look more closely with me at that ductile chemical mass called <term>horn of the Moon</term> <note>original: "cornu Lunæ" — a term for silver chloride. When melted, it cools into a waxy, translucent, and somewhat flexible substance, which the author uses as an analogy for flexible glass.</note>. This substance is nothing other than the texture of silver inverted by the contrary particles of acid spirits; yet, it shows itself most readily meltable even at a candle flame, and yielding. If it is poured out into thin strips, it becomes transparent, rivaling glass, and in some way obedient to the hammer. Hence, there have been those who recall making ductile vials by an easy experiment in imitation of this. There is also another not insignificant piece of persuasion from my own laboratory: that a "ductile salt" is obtained without difficulty. [For what is common glass but a strong mixture of salt and sand?] 

<margin>Ductile salt.</margin>
When I had discovered this type of salt through experimentation at Leiden <note>original: "Lugduni Batavorum" — the Latin name for the city of Leiden in the Netherlands, a major center for 17th-century science.</note>, I showed it to the illustrious Mr. *Sylvius* <note>Franciscus Sylvius, a famous physician and chemist who pioneered the study of chemical reactions in the human body.</note>, both Professor and Chemist, and to *Steno* <note>Nicolas Steno, a pioneer in both anatomy and geology.</note>, *Marcgraf* <note>Georg Marcgraf, a noted naturalist and astronomer.</note>, and other noble minds. I believe it had never before been noticed, either by chance sight or by reading. 

This ductile salt is prepared—to be frank—from <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: "sale armoniaco" — ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy for cleaning metals and other reactions.</note>, often dissolved in a large glass vessel and slowly condensed into icicle-like forms. From this process, crystals eventually emerge with a length corresponding to the width of the vessel. I have noted some of these stretched to a length of six finger-breadths, appearing outwardly like the double-edged blades of swords. When I bent them gently, they obeyed the movement of my hand most submissively, and they returned themselves to their former straightness at my will, even after many bendings. 

<margin>Flexible glass is possible.</margin>
They showed this same resilience for a whole month, as often as I pleased, until they finally dissolved into water upon the arrival of humid air. Otherwise, a modest amount of industry could render even cold glass so flexible that it might satisfy the accounts in Pliny's history...

<margin>Metallic glass has a different spirit than common glass.</margin>
For in metallic glasses, that slightly oily tenacity is so far from being removed by the sharpness of the fire that they are very easily restored again into the body of the original metal. This is demonstrated by a thousand experiments with that common <term>glass of Saturn</term> <note>original: "Saturni vitrum" — lead glass.
