<margin>
Can glass be brought to such a state that it may endure the hammer?
</margin>
...or like the links of chains, as Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar and bishop who wrote extensively on the "alchemy" of minerals and metals.</note> says: indeed, in metals, a tiny amount of "earthy" matter is mixed with a tiny amount of "watery" matter, and vice versa—the greatest part of one with the greatest of the other—and one is not separated from the other because of the homogeneity of their parts [observe here how the Chemist notes that **like approaches like** <note>original Greek: ὅμοιον ὁμοίῳ πελάζειν — a classical philosophical principle suggesting that similar substances naturally gravitate toward one another.</note>] and the union of the elements, which is the opposite of what happens in stones. How learnedly and with almost Cartesian <note>Referring to René Descartes, who argued that the properties of matter depend on the shape and motion of its microscopic particles.</note> subtlety has the Chemist, wise in mind, expressed these things, however rough his language may be? From this, it is concluded, **Reader**, that he who reduces either common glass or—perhaps more appropriately—metallic glass to such a thinness of homogeneous parts, will also ensure that it follows the behavior of metals, and shows a submissive and flexible nature with a <term>ductile</term> <note>The physical property of being able to be drawn out into a thin wire or hammered thin without snapping.</note> tenacity. *Descartes, in Part IV of his Principles of Philosophy, says: Glass is very fragile because the surfaces along which its particles touch each other are quite tiny and small.* Let the industry of the curious, therefore, wake up to this: that the particles of glass might be reduced to such smallness that they touch each other at many more points, and then "ductile glass" will not be a difficult thing to achieve. Here, C. M. <note>Christopher Merret (1614–1695), an English physician and scientist who translated Antonio Neri’s seminal work on glassmaking into English and added his own critical "Observations."</note> strongly and learnedly opposes this in his *Observations on Antonio Neri’s Art of Glass*.

<margin>
An English writer opposes the idea of ductile glass.
</margin>
*Add hereunto,* he says, *that the nature of malleability consists in a close and throughout adhesion of parts, and a capacity to the change of figure in the minutest parts. Both which are inconsistent with the nature of Glass. For the materials of Glass, Sand, and Salts, have such figures as seem incapable of such adhesion in every part one to another. For all salts have their determinate figure which they keep too, in their greatest solutions and actions of the fire upon them, as many instances might evince, and that figure is various according to the salts. Salt-peter, and all alkalizate-salts are pointed, and by their pungency, and caustickness* <note>Merret’s argument is that because the "salts" (chemical fluxes) used to make glass have fixed, sharp, geometric shapes, they can never "stick" together smoothly enough to bend or be hammered like the smoother particles of metal.</note>

<meta>This page continues the debate over whether glass can be made malleable (flexible enough to be hammered). The previous page established the views of those who believed glass could be treated like a metal if its microscopic parts were refined. Here, the author quotes a skeptic—likely the English physician Christopher Merret—who argues that the rigid, needle-like shapes of the salt and sand used to make glass prevent it from ever being flexible.</meta>

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>ON THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.</header>

*...thickness seem to be made up of infinite sharp-pointed needles. And as for sand, the figure thereof is various, nay, infinite, as it appears in Microscopes. Now how can any man imagine that such variety of figures in sand can so comply with the determinate figures of salt, as to touch one another in the smallest points <note>original: "in minimis" — a technical term in early particle physics referring to the smallest possible units of matter</note>, which is necessary to make it malleable?* etc. That is: add to these things that the nature of malleability (to express it more clearly than elegantly) consists in the tightest cohesion of parts, and in a readiness to follow along and change shape into the smallest parts; neither of which suits the character of glass. For the materials from which glass is made—sand and salts—lack shapes that embrace each other so firmly. All salts show specific and determined shapes, which they retain most stubbornly even in the greatest solutions and the fiercest embraces of flames, which would be easy to show with many examples. These very shapes are varied, just as the nature of the salts themselves is varied. Saltpeter <note>original: "Nitrum"</note> and all alkaline salts <note>original: "salia lixiva" — salts derived from leaching ashes with water</note> end in points, and because of that power of stinging and burning, they seem to be formed from infinite cohorts of very sharp needles. As for sand, its shape is varied—indeed, of infinite variety—as even microscopes have shown. Who now could convince himself that this variety of shapes in sand can be so joined with the determined shapes of salts that their parts touch in the smallest points, which is required for <term>malleability</term> <note>The ability of a material to be hammered or rolled into thin sheets without breaking.</note> (that is, a ductile obedience under the hammer)? Although it appears that hardly anything more impressive could be said than these things, hardly anything less solid is discovered.

<margin>
But the same argument is countered.
</margin>

We grant, not unwillingly, both diverse shapes to salts and to sands; but that these remain unharmed after that bitterness of flames—this is what we desire to be demonstrated by the learned Englishman <note>A reference to Christopher Merret (1614–1695), a member of the Royal Society who famously doubted the possibility of flexible glass in his notes on the chemistry of glassmaking.</note>. ...they cling together like the hooks of a chain, as **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval philosopher who wrote extensively on alchemy and minerals.</note> says. In metals, however, the smallest amount of "earthy" matter is mixed with the smallest amount of "watery" matter, and vice-versa; the greatest amount of one is mixed with the greatest of the other. One cannot be separated from the other because the parts are of the same nature <note>original: "homogeneitatis partium"</note> and the elements are so tightly joined, which is the opposite of what we see in stones. How learnedly and with almost Cartesian subtlety did the wise-minded Chemist <note>The author is likely referring to a specific alchemical writer, possibly Geber or a similar authority.</note> express these things, even if his language was somewhat rough? From this it is concluded, **Listen**, that whoever can reduce either common glass or—perhaps more appropriately—"metallic glass" to such a thinness of uniform parts will bring it about that the glass follows the behavior of metals, displaying a yielding and flexible nature with ductile tenacity.

<margin>Can glass be reduced to such a point that it might bear the hammer?</margin>
**Descartes, *Principles of Philosophy*, part IV.** *Glass is very fragile,* he says, *because the surfaces where its particles touch each other are remarkably small and tiny.* Therefore, let the industry of curious minds awaken to this task: if the particles of glass can be reduced to such small pieces that they touch each other at many more points, then creating ductile glass will not be difficult to achieve. However, "C. M." <note>Christopher Merret (1614–1695), an English physician and scientist who translated Antonio Neri’s work on glassmaking.</note> vehemently and learnedly opposes this in his *Observations on The Art of Glass by Antonio Neri*.

<margin>An English writer opposes the idea of ductile glass.</margin>

For all salts have their determinate figure which they keep too, in their greatest solutions and actions of the fire upon them, as many instances might evince, and that figure is various according to the salts. Now how can any man imagine that such variety of figures in sand can so comply with the determinate figures of salt, as to touch one another in minimis <note>Latin: "in the smallest parts"</note>, which is necessary to make it malleable? etc.* That is: Add to this, that the nature of malleability (to express it more clearly than elegantly) consists in the tightest cohesion of parts, and a readiness to change shape into the smallest parts; neither of which fits the character of glass. All salts show peculiar and determinate shapes, which they retain most stubbornly even in the greatest solutions and in the fiercest embraces of flames, which would be easy to show with many examples. These very shapes are various, just as the nature <note>original: "ingenium"</note> of the salts themselves is various. As for sand, its shape is various, indeed of an infinite variety, as even microscopes have demonstrated. Who now could persuade themselves that such a variety of shapes in sand could be joined with the determinate shapes of salts in such a way that their parts touch each other in the smallest degree—which is nonetheless required for malleability (that is, a ductile obedience under the hammer).

While it appears that nothing more brilliant than these words could have been said, one finds they are hardly solid. But the same argument <margin>is met with a rebuttal.</margin> is countered. # ON THE ORIGIN AND
<note>original: DE ORTU ET</note>

<page-num>118</page-num>

This spirit <note>original: genius; referring to the transformative power or "character" of the furnace fire</note>, especially if it has lasted for a long time, overturns everything: it blunts what is sharp, sharpens what is blunt, stretches the curved, and curves what is straight; in short, it mixes, displaces, and disturbs the whole. Who then will guarantee that the sand or the salts retain their own shapes within the glass? Shapes *are* left behind, but they are accidental—only those which that final surge of fire permitted. For while the mass is hot, the shapes vary at every single moment, changing hour by hour as the flame assaults them. We recognize that glass in common use contains **pores**, partly because a flame of two or three days cannot so blunt all the angles of the sand and salt and reduce them to a smooth uniformity that it doesn't form other twists and turns even within the solid body of the sand and salt; and partly because the fire, as it finally expires, escapes into the air only through channels—however tiny—which are clearly left open in some way.

<margin>Glass is full of pores.</margin>
Hence, Mr. Hudde <note>Johannes Hudde (1628–1704) was a Dutch mathematician and Amsterdam official known for his work on optics and lenses</note> of Amsterdam—the darling of **Cartesian Philosophy** <note>The mechanical philosophy of René Descartes, which explained natural phenomena through the shape and motion of particles</note>—two years ago used his breath to stretch glass into very thin, yet still whole and solid little spheres. When he filled these empty spheres with common water, he noticed that watery droplets gradually seeped through the very substance of the glass, and the vessel was thus slowly emptied; because of the thinness of the glass, the straighter pores were unable to block the passage of the liquid. The Commentator <note>Likely referring to a specific author the text is critiquing</note> writes here that salts do not lose their shapes in the heat of flames, regarding which we must judge as follows: some salts, if they are subjected to fire alone, maintain the consistency of their shapes for a long time, but if they are mixed with other materials, they lose them quickly. Moreover, if they are cooked alone for a longer time and then dissolved, coagulated, and burned again—repeating the dissolving and burning more frequently—they finally lose all their **sharp points** <note>original: aculeos</note> and are converted into "tasteless earth" <note>A chemical term for a substance that has lost its reactive properties or "spirit"</note>, as has been shown by a thousand experiments...

<summary>The author argues that the intense heat of glassmaking destroys the original geometric shapes of sand and salt, creating microscopic pores. He cites Johannes Hudde's experiment where water seeped through thin glass to prove that glass is porous rather than perfectly solid.</summary>

Indeed, I remember that more than once I reduced **Venetian** glass to a powder <note>original: *alkohol*; in this period, the term could refer to a very fine powder as well as distilled spirits</note>, and then, having poured on hot distilled water, I attempted to extract the salt from it by boiling; but I obtained only a tiny amount, and it did not correspond to even a hundredth part of the glass’s body. Furthermore—and this is worth noting—its angles did not match the lye salt <note>original: *sale lixivo*; salt extracted from wood ashes used in glassmaking</note> that had entered the mixture before.

<margin>Are there salts in glass?</margin>

There are glasses, I do not deny, which are under-cooked and contain an abundance of salt, so that they exhale a kind of salty sweat when rainy weather is imminent; but those very same glasses, if they are cooked longer, will neither sweat nor leave any sign of salt, as the assault of the flames overturns its particles. 

Hence the most noble **Magalotti** <note>Lorenzo Magalotti (1637–1712) was a diplomat and secretary of the Accademia del Cimento</note>, when he very kindly showed me not long ago in the Medici laboratory in Florence various salts of herbs—extracted from barks, roots, woods, and seeds by the force of fire, which catch the eye with a wonderful variety of shapes—added that, based on his own experiments and those of **Mr. Redi** <note>Francesco Redi (1626–1697), the renowned physician to the Grand Duke of Tuscany</note>, all of those salts, if they were tortured in the long-lasting execution-chamber of fire, would eventually turn into cubes. If he had fatigued these cubes further still by the **arts of Vulcan** <note>A poetic reference to the intense heat of the furnace, named for the Roman god of fire and the forge</note>, they would undoubtedly have all eventually turned into a white, tasteless earth. 

I will say nothing now of how the Commentator arms himself here against common glass, since it seems more likely that the famous **flexible glass** <note>A legendary lost invention from Roman times, *vitrum flexile*, said to be unbreakable</note> was actually metallic. ### ON THE ORIGIN AND
<page-num>120</page-num>

To honor here the sharp mind of the great **Descartes** <note>original: *Carteſii*; Rene Descartes, the French philosopher who proposed that all physical phenomena could be explained by the shape and motion of particles</note>, who although he did not see flexible salt, advanced so far by subtle meditation that he predicted it could exist in his *Principles of Philosophy, part IV*. Namely, he says, *the particles whose matter is a little more solid, such as those of salt, being intercepted and crushed in these passages, are made plain and flexible from being round and rigid: no differently than a round rod of glowing iron can be flattened into a long plate by frequent blows of hammers.* Let these principles be applied to the rigid particles of glass, and the case is decided. In a word: if glass is worked by fire for a long time, so that the particles—disarmed of their angles and hooks—join together more faithfully and are imbued with a certain tenacious glue of the Chemical art, I see no reason that compels us to noisily oppose the testimonies of so many Ancient writers with such bold denial.

They were the greatest "gluttons" <note>meaning they were voracious consumers and producers of knowledge</note> of Chemistry after the times of the Egyptians and Greeks. Among them, **Geber** <note>Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the father of Arabic chemistry</note> became famous in the first century after Muhammad; he was a clear, solid, and wise writer, whose Latin version of his *Works* is available everywhere, while the Arabic version is kept in the Museum of the excellent **Jacob Golius** in **Leiden** <note>original: *Lugduni Batavorum*</note>. **Leo Africanus** <note>A famous Moorish diplomat and traveler</note> contends that this Geber was actually Greek, just as perhaps the Greek...

# THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.

<margin>The Arabs learned Chemistry from the Greeks.</margin>
...recognize him as belonging to that nation, but writing in Arabic. For let no one mistakenly think the Arabs preceded the Greeks in learning. It is gleaned from the eight hundred years of Arabic writers in Hottinger’s *Historical Miscellany* <note>original: *Analect. Hist.*; referring to the work of Johann Heinrich Hottinger, a 17th-century Swiss scholar of Oriental languages</note> that no theologian, philosopher, or historian is older than Islam and the Quran. Finally, it was through the work and expense of Al-Mansur <note>The second Abbasid Caliph, known for founding the House of Wisdom in Baghdad</note> and Al-Ma'mun <note>An Abbasid Caliph who greatly promoted the translation of Greek scientific texts</note> that almost all the ancient writers of the Chaldeans, Egyptians, and even the Greeks and Romans were translated into the Arabic language; these translations now fill vast libraries throughout Africa and the entire Orient. The Arab historian Abul-Faraj <note>Also known as Bar Hebraeus, a 13th-century scholar</note> confirms nearly all of this in his *History of the Dynasties*. And to this diligence of the Arabs, we today owe many writings of the ancient authors which had already perished in Europe, but were restored from their versions. **Jacobus Golius** <note>A Dutch mathematician and Orientalist (1596–1667) who traveled the Levant to collect manuscripts</note> brought various works of **Galen** <note>A prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note> back with him from the East, rendered in Arabic—even those which are missing today from the European Greek text of Galen. Following that same kind of study was the greatest master of the Arabic school, **Ibn Sina**, or **Avicenna** <margin>Ibn Sina the Chemist.</margin>, in the eleventh Christian century. The incorruptible witness **Albertus Magnus** <note>A 13th-century German friar and bishop who was a major influence on medieval science</note> testifies that he wrote on Alchemy in his *Book III on Metals, Chapter IX*, and his writing is right before our eyes. Concerning **Avenzoar** and **Averroes** <note>Two influential Andalusian-Muslim thinkers</note>, the matter is not so clear as to whether they treated chemical matters or only those of Galen. However, many chemical writings of the Arabs are still in the public light today, such as those of **Senior Zadith son of Hamuel**, **Khalid**, **Abubali**, **Adresi**, **Alphidius**, **Al-Kindi**, **Bubachar**, **Zambertus**, **Abulcasis**, and others. Even to this day, no art is more cultivated among the more ingenious Saracens, Arabs, and Africans than Chemistry. This was acknowledged by **Leo Africanus** <note>A Berber Andalusi diplomat and author of *Description of Africa*</note> about the inhabitants of Fez <note>A city in modern-day Morocco</note>, when he converted from Mohammedanism to the Christian faith some one hundred and fifty years ago...

# ON THE ORIGIN AND...

<margin>
Africans even today are Chemists.

...[observed], then also three years ago the most learned **Thomas Parry**, an Englishman from Oxford, confessed in my presence that he had noted the same thing in Tangier, Africa. It is credible that Chinese Chemistry owed its origins to these very Africans and Arabs who frequently traveled to the region of China for trade; about which **Pantoja**, **Trigault**, and most recently **Martini** and **Kircher** <note>These were prominent Jesuit scholars and missionaries—Diego de Pantoja, Nicolas Trigault, Martino Martini, and Athanasius Kircher—who provided 17th-century Europe with its first detailed reports on Chinese science and culture</note> have written. 

<margin>
From where was Chemistry spread into China?
</margin>
For no one doubts that it was derived from these same people into Spain, and finally into many other regions of a more refined Europe; for who doubts that the Saracens <note>A term used in the Middle Ages and Renaissance for Arab Muslims</note> flooding into Spain brought their arts with them? It is well known that these arts were so effectively established on the shores of the Mediterranean Sea that even today in Montpellier <note>A city in southern France famous for its medieval medical school, which heavily integrated Arabic pharmaceutical knowledge</note> there live pharmacists descended from their ultimate lineage. 

<margin>
From where into Spain, etc.
</margin>
Inspired and taught by these authors, **Arnaldus de Villa Nova** from France, **Ramon Lull** from Majorca, **Albertus Magnus** from Germany, **Roger Bacon** from Britain, **Rupescissa** <note>Jean de Roquetaillade, a 14th-century Franciscan alchemist</note>, and other chemists of previous centuries imbued the western parts of Europe with new techniques of distillation <term>distillation: the process of purifying a liquid by heating it into vapor then condensing it back into liquid, essential for making "waters" and spirits</term>, which were recommended for medical and metallurgical purposes. 

For the Greeks had until then lived on their own resources <note>original: *proprio adhuc succo victitaverant*; literally "had lived on their own juice," meaning they relied on their own ancient traditions</note>, and they did not yet allow the art drawn from the Egyptians to die out among them, however much they were oppressed by Islamic servitude. 

Furthermore, when that twelfth Christian century <note>The author refers broadly to the intellectual awakening of the High Middle Ages (12th-13th centuries) when Greek and Arabic texts were translated into Latin</note> ennobled Europe with several great lights—Italy indeed with **Pietro d'Abano** and **Thomas Aquinas**, France with **Arnaldus de Villa Nova**, Spain (or its subject Majorca) with **Ramon Lull**, Britain with **Roger Bacon** and **John Duns Scotus**, Germany with **Albertus Magnus**, and Denmark with **Saxo Grammaticus**—it would not be beside the point to linger a little while in more accurately weighing certain of these great geniuses of Nature who cultivated this curious art, so that we may see from whose fountains Chemistry flowed into all Euro-

## THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.
Q 2

<margin>Roger Bacon, English Chemist. When did he live?</margin>
Propagated far and wide through Europe, [Chemistry] still manfully defends its majesty, even if the envious and the idle bark at it in vain. The greatest of these by birth, **Roger Bacon** <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292), also known as Doctor Mirabilis, was a Franciscan friar and early proponent of the empirical method</note>, a monk of sublime intellect, flourished around the year of Christ 1205. For in that famous *Letter on the Secret Works of Art and Nature, and on the Vanity of Magic* <note>original: "Epistolâ illâ luculentâ de Operibus Secretis Artis & Naturæ, atq; Nullitate Magiæ"</note> written to **William of Paris** [who, as is established from the works of **Petavius** <note>Dionysius Petavius (1583–1652) was a Jesuit scholar famous for his work on chronology; his citation here suggests this text was written in the mid-to-late 17th century</note>, lived around the year of Christ 1226], it is written thus: "After six hundred and two years of the Arabs had passed," he says, "you asked me about certain secrets." Now, the six hundred and second year of the Arabs, or of the **Hegira** <note>The migration of Muhammad from Mecca to Medina, marking the start of the Islamic calendar</note>, falls in the year of Christ 1205, provided it is observed that the first year of the Hegira was the year of Christ 622, and also that the Arabic year consists of 354 days, and in every thirty years, eleven days are added from the remaining fractions.

<margin>The writings of Roger.</margin>
The more curious still study three writings of this **Roger Bacon**: two on Chemistry, and one on *The Secret Works of Art and Nature, and on the Vanity of Magic*. In these, especially the last, he shows a sufficiently profound knowledge of a most vast intellect; he proves himself to be a mechanic, physicist, physician, mathematician, and chemist, admirable beyond the grasp of those times. To him, more recent authors owe many things which they now boastfully shout are their own inventions. But let us hear him speaking: <margin>The admirable mechanics of Roger.</margin> "By the fashioning of art alone," <note>original: "Per figurationem solius artis"</note> he says, "instruments of navigation can be made without rowers, so that the largest river or sea-going ships may be moved under the direction of a single man with greater speed than if they were full of sailors." [This was recently attempted in Belgium <note>Likely a reference to the "land-yachts" or advanced naval engineering in the Dutch Republic/Spanish Netherlands in the early 1600s</note>]. "Chariots can also be made so that they move without an animal with inestimable force" (was he referring to wind-propelled carriages? <note>term: wind-propelled carriages (currus veliferos); these were experimental vehicles, like those built by Simon Stevin in 1600, which used sails to travel across land</note>).

*Aristotle, in the chapter on the vacuum,* <note>original: "Aristoteles cap. de vacuo"; likely referring to the *Physics*, though the optical applications are Bacon's own expansion</note> *and thus for every state and opposing army, the greatest terrors can be created, so that they might waver either because of a multitude of appearing stars or because of men gathered above them, especially if the following lesson is held together with that first one; for transparent objects* <note>original: "perspicua"; referring here to lenses</note> *can be so shaped that things placed very far away appear very close, and vice versa, so that from an incredible distance we might read the smallest letters and count things however small.* 
<margin>Telescopes known to Roger.</margin>
*(For these uses some have not long ago begun to develop telescopes, others polyhedral glasses* <note>lenses with many facets, like prisms</note>*, others a wondrous kind of mirrors.)*
*Bodies can also be so shaped that the largest appear smallest, and vice versa; so that the high appear low, and vice versa; so that hidden things appear manifest; so that what is done in opposing states and armies can be detected by enemies. (How ignorantly, then, are microscopes, macroscopes,* <note>instruments for magnifying large objects</note> *camera obscuras, and polemoscopes* <note>a "war-scope" or early periscope for looking over fortifications</note> *believed to be inventions of our own times, when it appears all these were known to this Roger?)* *Transparent objects can even be so shaped that a man entering a house would truly see gold and silver and precious stones, and whatever a man might wish, yet whoever might hasten to the place of the vision would find nothing. (These things, now very well known, were shown to me in reality by **Servière** <note>Nicolas Grollier de Servière (1596–1689), a French engineer famous for his cabinet of curiosities</note> in Lyon, France; by the most refined **Settala** <note>Manfredo Settala (1600–1680), a Milanese scientist and collector</note> in Milan; and by **Kircher** <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), the famous Jesuit polymath and inventor</note> in Rome.) Therefore, we do not need to use magical illusions, since the power of philosophy teaches us to perform what is sufficient. But concerning more sublime powers, it must be fashioned that rays are led and gathered through various refractions and reflections at any distance we wish, so that whatever object is there may be burned.*
<margin>Roger’s burning mirrors.</margin>
*(This surpasses both Archimedes and the burning mirrors* <note>original: "specula caustica"</note> *of **Villette** <note>François Villette (1621–1698), who constructed famous large burning mirrors for Louis XIV</note>.)*

Aristotle, in his chapter on the vacuum <note>Referencing Aristotle’s *Physics*, though Bacon often interpreted ancient texts to support his own experimental findings</note>, [shows that] in this way, the greatest terrors can be inflicted upon an opposing city or army. This is done so that they might be scattered, either by a multitude of appearing stars or by [the appearance of] men gathered above them—especially if the following instruction is combined with the first. (For these purposes, some have recently begun to develop telescopes, others polyhedral glasses <note>lenses with many facets that multiply an image</note>, and others a wondrous kind of mirrors).

Bodies can also be so shaped that the largest things appear smallest, and vice versa; that high things appear lowest, and vice versa; and that hidden things appear manifest, so that those things which occur in opposing cities and armies can be detected by enemies. (How ignorantly, therefore, are microscopes, macroscopes, *camera obscuras*, and polemoscopes <note>original: "polemoscopia"; an early form of the periscope or "trench telescope" used to look over obstacles</note> believed to be inventions of our own times, when it appears all these things were known to this Roger?)

(These things, now very well known, were shown to me in reality by Servier <note>Nicolas Grollier de Servière (1596–1689), a soldier and inventor famous for his "cabinet of curiosities" in Lyon</note> at Lyon in France, by the most refined Settala <note>Manfredo Settala (1600–1680), a Milanese scientist and collector of mechanical wonders</note> at Milan, and by Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), the celebrated Jesuit polymath and inventor of the magic lantern</note> at Rome).

It is not necessary, therefore, for us to use magical illusions, since the power of philosophy <note>meaning "natural philosophy" or science</note> teaches how to bring about what is sufficient. Scholars decipher the jumble as "carbonum pulvere," meaning "charcoal powder."</note> and of sulfur, and thus you will make thunder and flashes, if you know the artifice.”*

*I shall pass over what he comments on regarding the "star-stone" <term>astroite: a star-shaped fossil or crystal traditionally thought to move when placed in vinegar</term> running through vinegar, the squaring of the circle, and various chemical experiments. From this, it is clearly evident*

*that Roger’s mind entered more deeply into the secrets of Nature than was commonly believed by the foolish of his age. <meta>This page continues the defense of Roger Bacon's intellectual reputation. ...it is said that he is a Magician, if anyone raises his eyes a little higher and searches more deeply into the secrets of Nature. But what price for their evil labors do those detestable slanderers of the spirit bear? What do they deserve, *Audience*? 
<margin>
His name is vindicated by posterity.
</margin>
Not only did Naudé <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653), a French librarian and scholar who wrote a famous defense of great men suspected of magic</note> most effectively vindicate Roger, but Spondanus <note>Henri de Sponde (1568–1643), a French jurist and historian</note> also asserts the Philosopher’s cause in his *Annals* in this manner: *"In this year, 1278,"* he says, *"the doctrine of the Englishman Roger Bacon was condemned because of certain suspected novelties; for he was most well-versed in every kind of doctrine, faculty, and science, and miraculously subtle (whence he was commonly known as the 'Wonderful Doctor' <note>original: "Doctor mirabilis"</note>). While he investigated Mathematics and Natural Philosophy more inquisitively than was proper (or rather, than seemed proper to the authorities), he was commonly regarded as a sorcerer and a Magician. Pits <note>John Pits (1560–1616), an English Catholic biographer</note> mentions that these things happened to him out of the envy or ignorance of others, who condemned those things which either escaped their own diligence or exceeded their understanding."* Thus, Roger shared this common fate with Virgil, the Bishop of Salzburg, whom—according to Aventinus—the Roman Curia ordered to abstain from office and accused of heresy because, confirmed by the science of the stars, he asserted that the Antipodes <note>Antipodes: the concept that people could live on the opposite side of the Earth, which was once considered heretical as it challenged certain literal interpretations of scripture</note> existed. That similar things happened to Copernicus, Galileo, and Descartes is unknown to no one. 

But from where did Roger gain such profound erudition? Besides his genius, *Audience*, which spontaneously carried him toward 
<margin>
By which Masters Roger made progress.
</margin>
sublime things, he acknowledges everywhere in his writings that he made progress not only from reading *Aristotle, Pliny, Solinus, Ptolemy, Hippocrates, Gellius, Ovid, Isaac the Physician <note>Isaac Israeli the Elder, a 10th-century Jewish physician and philosopher</note>, Plato, Josephus,* and others, but (as it was our purpose to demonstrate) he also turned with a diligent hand the works of *Avicenna, Averroes, Albumazar, Geber, Abubali, and Al-Farabi,* the most subtle of the Arabian Philosophers. ...of *Roger Bacon*, called by the inhabitants *the house of Friar Bacon* <note>original: "the house of frayer Bacon"</note>. When *Edmund Dickinson* <note>An English physician and alchemist (1624–1707) who served King Charles II</note>, a distinguished physician, showed this to me from the opposite bank of the Thames as it laps against the city, he added that *Roger*, who was daily attacked by the insults of certain monks, had established his home on the farther bank. He did this partly so that he would not be subject to their jurisdiction by living on the nearer bank—and thus within the city—and partly to show that he did not flee the eyes of his adversaries, but would spend the rest of his life triumphing over the ignorance of that hated crowd.

<margin>Arnold of Villanova, Chemist.</margin>
*Arnold of Villanova*, a philosopher, physician, and excellent chemist, first looked upon this mortal light as an infant in the town of Villeneuve in Narbonese Gaul <note>A region of southern France; Arnold's exact birthplace is still debated by historians</note>, according to *Symphorien Champier*. He was the first of the Christians in these recent centuries to be raised to the highest peak of the art through the mastery of the Arabs [if one perhaps excludes *Roger*]. Regarding him, *Johannes Andreas*, a legal scholar of those times, writes in his *Additions to the Mirror of Law, title: on the crime of forgery*:

> *In our days,* he says, *we had Master Arnold of Villanova in the Roman Court as a supreme Physician and Theologian, about whom I wrote a legal opinion on the Observance of Fasting; he was also a great Alchemist, and he agreed to submit the small rods of gold that he made to every test.*

He sufficiently demonstrates here, in the *Mirror of Alchemy* <note>original: "Speculum Alchymiae"</note> and other writings, who his teachers were: *Geber*, *Daucis*, *Morienus*, *The Emerald Tablet of Hermes*, *Avicenna*, *Eximeus*, *The Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum," a famous early Latin alchemical text</note>, and *Al-Ghazali*. He became famous shortly before the year 1300 AD as the master of *Lully* in chemistry, as *Lully* himself often acknowledges. Concerning him, *Lully* writes in the *Last Testament, book III*:

> *Neither I, nor my Master Arnold, used any furnace other than the following one.*

He was greatly honored by Frederick, King of Sicily, and was sent by him to heal the Pope; but on the journey, from a

<margin>The history of Raymond Lull as recounted by Mutio the Spaniard.</margin>
In the history of *Raymond Lull*, we must trace the beginnings more deeply, as *Vincent Mutio* <note>Vicente Mut (1614–1687), a Spanish astronomer, historian, and soldier</note> of Spain recently disturbed the long-standing opinion of all Europe regarding Lull's chemical knowledge in his book titled: *History of the Kingdom of Majorca*. *Mutio’s* lengthy dissertation, reduced to a summary, essentially comes to this: The father of *Raymond Lull*, also called *Ramon Lull*, was of the most illustrious blood of Barcelona and a companion of King *James* <note>James I of Aragon, known as "the Conqueror"</note> in the conquest of Majorca; his mother was of the most noble house of the Catalan Counts of *Eril*. Born to these parents on the island of Majorca in the year of Christ 1235, *Raymond Lull* first devoted himself to literature, but soon being more inclined toward arms and romance, he diligently wore away his time composing verses for his mistress, *Eleonora*. Once, while he remained fixed in church staring at her too intently, she approached her lover in a girlish manner. Just when it was thought she would offer him delights, she quickly loosened her bodice and showed him her breasts—cadaverous and rotting—and her chest, which was gripped by a horrible <term>carcinoma</term> <note>A cancerous tumor or ulcer; in Lull's hagiography, this sight of physical decay is the catalyst for his religious conversion</note>. Whereupon *Raymond*, disturbed by this as if by an infernal image, stood fixed and was struck dumb,

...was struck dumb, and wasting away with grief, he for a long time found no solace anywhere except in the darkness. To him, in his dismay, the form of the Savior fixed to the cross finally revealed itself with such power that Raymond afterwards began to despise the pleasures of the world hidden behind vain splendor, never returning to his former wallowing in love. From that point on, although he was already over thirty years old, he diligently studied the rules of Grammar, learned the Arabic language from his servant, and in the Year of Christ 1275, wandered into the wilderness to spend his time in prayer, fasting, sacrifices, penance, and tears, but above all being daily more inflamed to propagate the Christian faith among the pagans. While he was occupied with this, the Savior appeared to him again in the form of a burning Seraph <note>A celestial being from the highest rank of the angelic hierarchy, often associated with divine love and fire</note> and commanded him to write a "General Art" regarding all the sciences <note>This refers to the *Ars Magna*, Lull's logical system intended to prove the truths of Christianity through reason</note>. *Lull* obeyed and completed the task. Meanwhile, James, King of Aragon, moved by *Lull’s* influence, built a magnificent monastery on the Island of Majorca and endowed it with rich revenues, entrusting it to thirteen Brothers of the Order of Minors <note>The Franciscans</note> so that, once they were trained there in the Arabic language, they might then prepare themselves to convert the pagan nations. 

<margin>Lull’s travels.</margin>
From that time, *Lull* traveled widely across the world on various journeys. He taught his "Art of Sciences" in Paris and traveled through Palestine, Cyprus, Egypt, Rome, Jerusalem, Armenia, Bohemia, and Britain. In Genoa, he wrote several books, and the Genoese nobility, having been invited, offered him the money necessary for a journey to the Holy Land. The censors of Paris approved *Lull’s* writings, though *Bzovius* <note>Abraham Bzovius (1567–1637), a Dominican historian who was often critical of Lull's mystical or "artistic" approach to theology</note> is more hostile toward him than is deserved. It is certainly true that all the fountains of the sciences were open to *Lull*, including even Chemistry; however, the "making of gold" <note>original Greek: χρυσοποίησις (*chrysopoiesis*), the alchemical process of transmuting base metals into gold</note> is falsely attributed to him, since both in his teaching

weapon; <note>The catchword from the previous page, completing the sentence regarding the intellectual tools at Lull's disposal.</note> for if, after the thirtieth year of his age, he learned the rules of Grammar and the Arabic language, and afterward lived fifty full years as a sober, holy traveler, devoted to converting people—of whom the vast majority were Hebrews—it is absurd to believe that he learned no Hebrew words in the meantime. This is especially likely since, through a common flaw in human nature, most people show off in familiar speech a knowledge of a language of which they have barely tasted the first principles. It was not only *Plautus* in Latin Comedy who mixed in that "hotchpotch" of Punic speech; <note>A reference to the Carthaginian language appearing in Plautus's play *Poenulus*.</note> nor was it only the Roman girls in *Juvenal* who used to cry out, "my life and soul," <note>original Greek: ζὼη καὶ ψυχὴ (*zōē kai psychē*)</note> upon meeting a suitor, though they had learned only this one phrase of the Greek tongue. Nor was it only *Julius Caesar* who, for the sake of display, described the bridges he crossed with his army in Gaul more accurately than the battles he won there as a victor—however much he owed the construction of those bridges to his architects, but the victory of the battles to his own valor. 

But let us look closer; let *Mutius* point out those many "Hebrew" things in *Lull*! He names *Agazoph*, and *Ubidrugal*, and *Recfage*, and *Adulphur*, and *Periminel*, and *Optesi*, and *Eschatesi*, and the like. Because Lull distorted these terms partly from Arabic and partly from Latin and Spanish into a sense known only to himself—to the point that he himself in his *Theories* <note>Referring to Lull's work *Theoria et Practica*</note> calls them the offspring of the "Oritan" language [or should it read "Mauritanian"], <note>original: *Oritanae [an legendum Mauritanae]*; referring to the Moorish or North African dialects Lull encountered in his travels.</note>—Mutius, a good man but quite unskilled in languages except for his own *Castilian*, convinced himself they were Hebrew. He acted just as the barbarism of earlier centuries did: upon seeing Hebrew, Arabic, or Persian characters, they would repeatedly cry, "It is Greek; it cannot be read." <note>A reference to the medieval legal proverb *Graecum est, non legitur*, used when a scribe encountered Greek text he could not decipher.</note> So today those in the interior...

Day teaches day, *it is said* <note>original: "Audit."</note>, and as age progresses and judgment matures, we correct many things which were delights in our youth, and vice versa. This was rightly observed by the great *Zwinger* <note>Theodor Zwinger (1533–1588), a Swiss physician known for his encyclopedic "Theatrum Humanae Vitae".</note>, who—even setting aside the testimony of *Lacinius* <note>Janus Lacinius, a 16th-century Calabrian monk and editor of alchemical texts.</note>—relates in that illustrious *Theater* of his that *Raymond* [Lull] had attempted to persuade *Arnold of Villanova* <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist; the text suggests Lull was initially a skeptic who argued against Arnold.</note> (who flourished around the year of Christ 1300, which was toward the very end of Lull’s old age) with many arguments that <unclear>well-ordered</unclear> <note>original: "ευνο-" (likely a Greek fragment for "favorably" or "well-ordered")</note> [knowledge] was less founded in the opinion of men than in the constant law of Nature. However, *Villanova*, finally weary of the dispute, showed *Raymond* the next day that genuine metamorphosis of metals into a more noble state right before his eyes; in the end, he easily drew the man over to his own opinion.

It is added that many people publicly rail against Chemistry, yet in their private studies love nothing more tenderly; I have happened to see some of these people myself during my travels. They think they must occasionally grant something to the customs and judgments of the age, however unfair they may often be. Nevertheless, *Divine Chemistry* <note>original: "Diva Chemia"</note> has not so deserved this that she should depend on the "saliva" of the common crowd, to be either acquitted or condemned by the judgment of the lazy populace. 

But these matters aside, *Lull* may have written in the final period of his old age that *one species is not transmuted into another*; yet no Chemist will deny that very point if it is understood with a sound intellect. *Arnold of Villanova*, the Master of *Lull*, explains the matter in his *Book on Perfection*:

<margin>
Are species changed into other forms?

*Master. Species*, he says, *of metals cannot be transmuted; and this is true, as they themselves assert, unless they are reduced to the first matter.* <note>The "First Matter" (Materia Prima) is the formless base of all substance; alchemists believed metals had to be "killed" or dissolved back to this state before they could be reborn as gold.</note> These words, however few, contain much in their hidden meaning. *Roger Bacon* <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292), a pioneering English philosopher and scientist.</note> says this: *Species,* he says, *are not transmuted, but the subjects of the species are [transmuted] most excellently and properly, for every sensible operation [concerns] individuals...*

...tion. Other Chemists in great number hold similar views. Appropriately,
*Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the Persian polymath whose works on medicine and alchemy were foundational in the West.</note> in Book II, Tract I, Chapter IV, on the Operation of Single Medicines—which is commonly thought to be the fourth book of Aristotle’s Meteorology—says:* <margin>Let the practitioners of Alchemy know that the species or forms of metals cannot truly be changed, unless perhaps they are reduced to their "first matter" and thus are transformed into something other than what they were before.</margin> Lull may have said that Chemical gold "counterfeits" gold and is not truly gold; we acknowledge this to be true regarding the adulterated frauds of the Pseudo-chemists, but regarding the handiwork of true Chemists, it is monstrously false, and has been refuted by the experiments of many craftsmen in person, even during this very year.

Let us descend at last to the primary argument of Mutius: *The venerable Martyr Lull,* he says, *did not offer any gold to Edward VI; for Lull was born in the year of Christ 1235, during the reign of Henry III in England, who was succeeded by Edward IV; then Edward V was substituted around the year 1305. The dates 1327–1367 (actually 1377) refer to Edward III, but the text calls him "Edward VI." This may reflect a non-standard counting system or a persistent error in alchemical historiography.</note> Furthermore, since it is established that Lull was stoned at Bugia <note>A city in modern-day Algeria where Lull was martyred while proselytizing.</note> in the year 1315, how could he have offered anything to that Edward, who began to rule a full eleven years after Lull’s death? VIII. In the year of Christ **1311**, and according to **Spondanus** <note>Henri de Sponde (1568–1643), a French jurist and historian who continued the ecclesiastical annals of Baronius.</note>, Pope **Clement [V]**, primarily at the urging of **Raymond Lull**, decreed in the year of Christ **1311** that wherever the Roman Curia happened to reside, and also in the Universities of **Paris** in France, **Oxford** in England, **Bologna** in Italy, and **Salamanca** in Spain, the **Hebrew, Arabic, and Chaldean** <note>In this context, "Chaldean" refers to Aramaic or Syriac, languages essential for biblical and missionary study.</note> languages should be publicly taught, and books in these same languages should be faithfully translated into Latin. (From this we may learn that Lull enjoyed a more intimate familiarity with the Hebrew language than **Mutius** had previously been persuaded of.) These facts, when properly compared, do not contradict one another; concerning only the date of **Lull's** death, **Wolfgang Justus**, in his *Medical Chronology*, brings forth something different from Mutius—namely, that Lull was still alive in the year **1321**, while **Louis the Bavarian** held the helm of the empire. But whether Wolfgang's calculation failed him, we shall not delay to consider, as we are about to demonstrate that Mutius was utterly hallucinating regarding the era of the **Edwards**.

<margin>Mutius erred most seriously in the history of the Edwards.</margin>
For what **Mutius** writes—that **Edward VI** began the first years of his reign in the year of Christ **1327**—is indeed most foreign to historical truth. The English historian of those times, **Polydore Vergil** <note>An Italian humanist and historian (c. 1470–1555) who wrote a famous history of England.</note>, says appropriately: *Henry VIII,* he says, *begat a son, Edward VI, by Jane Seymour, who now reigns as a mere youth, certainly born to rule.* <note>original: "Henricus Octavus, inquit, ex Joannâ Seymer genuit filium Edoardum Sextum, qui nunc regnat admodum adolescens, certè natus ad imperium."</note> He was born, however, in the year of Christ **1538**. It is truly remarkable that Mutius is so ignorant of chronology and yet so confident. **Camden** <note>William Camden (1551–1623), a renowned English antiquarian and historian.</note> believes that Lull’s craftsmanship—from which so many noble "rose nobles" <note>A famous English gold coin; legend claimed Lull produced the gold for them via alchemy in the Tower of London.</note> of gold were produced—belongs to the time of **Edward III**. That is indeed a more tolerable view if it is explained suitably; for otherwise, if inspected superficially, history would defend neither Camden nor **Selden** <note>John Selden (1584–1654), a polymath and jurist; the work mentioned, *Mare Clausum*, argues for English sovereignty over the seas.</note>, who, ascribing the matter to **Edward IV** in his *Of the Dominion of the Sea*, rises up thus:

The coin, he says, was frequently struck in gold by our Kings, and called the **Rose Noble** <note>The Rose Noble, or Ryal, was a prominent English gold coin featuring a rose and a ship; it was worth about ten shillings.</note>. On one side, it is distinguished by a ship floating on the sea, with the King—armed with a sword and shield—seated in that same ship as if upon a throne, so that it might display the symbol of the English maritime empire. Its first author was **King Edward III**, who protected his seas with a most numerous fleet of some eleven hundred ships. The type which we have added is, I believe, that of **Edward IV**. **Camden** <note>William Camden (1551–1623), a famous English antiquarian and historian.</note> calls the inscription on this coin the "symbol of the Alchemists" <note>original: "Chemistarum"</note>, who supplied **Edward III** with the material for that money. However, since it is established from the writings of **Polydore Virgil** <note>An Italian humanist and historian who wrote the *Anglica Historia*.</note> that **Edward II** extended his reign until the year of Christ **1327**—that is, until eleven years after the death of **Lull**—and since **Edward III** succeeded him, it is most evident that the wealth of Midas was offered to neither of these by Lull.

<margin>
To which Edward did Lull offer those six millions?
</margin>

What then? Does the credibility of history falter? Not in the least, Listeners. That **Edward I** is the Prince familiar to Lull; he is the one showered by the golden rain <note>An alchemical metaphor for the production of gold through transmutation.</note>. Everything agrees on this point: the ages of Lull and **Edward I** coincide <insert>[According to **Spondanus**, this Edward died in the year **1309**, and Lull in the year **1315**.]</insert>; the plan of Edward I to conquer the **Holy Land** <note>original: "Terra Sancta"</note> coincides, about which the aforementioned **Polydore** speaks at length; and the times of the other Princes—**Charles** and **Rupert**, the Kings of Sicily—coincide, for they were contemporaries and acquaintances of Edward I, and Lull also sent his writings to them. Hence it is that in the Lullian works we often read the name of **Edward**, but never with the addition of "the second," or "the third," or "the sixth"; for indeed, no one would call a man "the first" unless he was succeeded by "the second." He himself says most clearly in his book *On the Transmutation of the Soul*:

By doing so, she brought a cure to an evil that was otherwise incurable. To such an extent is a woman's tongue like a divine remedy, as *Speed* relates, when sincere love has anointed it. <margin>
What did Lull achieve in chemistry?
</margin>
Mutius <note>Girolamo Muzio (1496–1576), an Italian author who wrote on the history of alchemy.</note> maintains it as an unshakable fact: that **Raymond Lull** offered six million gold pieces, which he himself had created, to **Edward**, King of England, to promote the war against the infidels in the Holy Land. That statement by Robert Constantin in his *Nomenclature of Medical Writers* remains equally unshaken: "By investigating," he records, "I find that among the English, he truly performed what he professes in his books, and in the **Tower of London** <note>original: 'arce Londini'</note> by the King's command, he manufactured real gold. And a type of coin was shown to me, which they still call the **Rose Noble of Raymond** <note>original: 'Nobile Raymundi'</note>, made of pure and refined gold of the highest standard." It remains unshaken, as **Lull** himself says in his *Last Testament*: "I converted at one time fifty thousand pounds of mercury, lead, and tin into gold in London," from which **Camden** <note>William Camden (1551–1623), a famous English antiquarian and historian.</note> records those golden "Rose Nobles" of the English first originated. English antiquaries <note>original: 'curiosi'</note> most strongly confirm the same today; and **Edmund Dickinson** <note>An English physician and alchemist (1624–1707).</note> adds that **Lull** lived in Westminster Abbey as a welcome guest. Indeed, many years after his departure, when the small cell he had inhabited was being renovated, a great deal of gold-making powder <note>original: 'pulveris chrysopœi'; from the Greek words for 'gold' and 'to make.'</note> was still found in a small chest, to the great profit of the master builder who found it.

<margin>
Albertus Magnus.
</margin>
But having progressed further than expected because Mutius provoked us, we shall draw shorter lines in the history of **Albertus Magnus** <note>Albert the Great (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and polymath who became a legendary figure in the history of science and alchemy.</note>. The city of **Lauingen in Swabia** <note>original: 'Lavinga Sveviæ'</note> received him at his birth around the year 1200; **Regensburg** <note>original: 'Ratisbona'</note> appointed him Bishop; and sharp, untiring study made him a distinguished theologian, philosopher, and physician. Even today, the monuments of his genius remain in the public eye—learned, subtle, and vigorous to the point of causing envy. His books on minerals alone would reveal "the lion by its claw" <note>A Latin proverb meaning that one can judge the greatness of a whole work by a small sample.</note>; they were praised even by **Conring** <note>Hermann Conring (1606–1681), a skeptical German scholar and physician.</note>, who was otherwise a sparing praiser of works on minerals. *Books about **women**, or the foolish writings about the virtues of herbs, stones, and animals, are commonly circulated under his name; these are not the genuine works of **Albert**, as they are for the most part closer to the delusions of old wives <note>original: "anicularum deliramentis"</note> and unworthy of such a great Philosopher. **Gratarolo** <note>Guglielmo Gratarolo (1516–1568), an Italian physician who published many alchemical collections.</note> published a chemical work by **Albertus Magnus** about a hundred years ago, which is full of knowledge and hidden meanings; however, not a few people consider even that one to be spurious <note>False or misattributed.</note>, because it is not commonly listed among Albert’s other works. But since nothing is more common than for some of an author's writings to be published separately for specific reasons—which is why the more secret works of **Falloppio** <note>Gabriele Falloppio (1523–1562), a renowned Italian anatomist and physician.</note> or **Praevotius** <note>Jean Prévoit (1585–1631), a professor of medicine at Padua.</note>, and the more obscure writings of **Scaliger** <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a celebrated humanist scholar.</note> or **Grotius** <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645), the famous Dutch legal theorist and polymath.</note>, were not published alongside their other works—I do not see why they find such fault in that chemical booklet attributed to him. Indeed, you will not commonly find any chemical works among the writings of **Avicenna** <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose medical and philosophical works dominated European universities for centuries.</note> either; yet if we are to believe **Sorsanus** <note>Abu 'Ubayd al-Juzjani, the student and biographer of Avicenna.</note>, he was passionately devoted to the study of chemistry and passed it on to his successors.*

*Since that time, the study of chemistry in Europe has grown almost immeasurably, so much so that today it labors under a massive pile of writers, though it is less fruitful in actual experiments.*

<margin>4,000 Chemical Authors.</margin>
***Pierre Borel** <note>Petrus Borellus (1620–1671), a French physician and the author of the first major bibliography of chemistry, the 'Bibliotheca Chimica'.</note> collected up to 4,000 chemical authors, even though he omitted many in the meantime, overcome by the immensity of the subject. Although in that **Crowd of Philosophers**, and in those "Chemical Theaters" <note>A reference to the 'Theatrum Chemicum', the largest collection of alchemical texts ever printed, which began publication in 1602.</note> of magnificent apparatus, more smoke is found than solid knowledge, and often more nonsense than reason, one must nevertheless endure that weariness. We should consider that these sacred mysteries are constituted in such a way that from an infinite number of thorns, only a few roses can be plucked. Nor does the whole sea teem with fish—nets are often cast in vain; nor do all the woods hide the dens of wild beasts; and yet the [pursuit] of hounds...*

<margin>Danish Chemists.</margin>
From our own Denmark, though many could be cited, I wish to name two: the first, that noble genius **Tycho Brahe**, no less admirable in celestial Astronomy than he was diligent in that subterranean version <note>The author refers to alchemy as "subterranean astronomy," a common early modern metaphor for the correspondence between the stars and the metals of the earth.</note>; the second, **Peder Sørensen of Ribe** <note>original: "Petrum Severini Ripensem" (1540–1602), a Danish physician and follower of Paracelsus.</note>, my fellow citizen, formerly a Professor at Copenhagen, and later Chief Physician to **Frederick II**, King of the Danes of glorious memory. He was beyond dispute an excellent Chemist, to whose genius **Paracelsianism** owes much of its splendor—which would otherwise have been quite empty. Germany venerates his pen, **Davisson** <note>William Davisson (1593–1669), a Scottish physician and chemist who became the first professor of chemistry at the Jardin des Plantes in Paris.</note> almost adores him, and **Francis Bacon** <note>original: "Verulamius Baco"</note> prefers him over the "Bombastic" followers <note>Referring to the followers of Paracelsus, whose full name was Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim. Bacon famously critiqued Paracelsus but respected Sørensen.</note>.

But some gloomy critic might object, saying it is strange 
<margin>Should Chemistry be rejected as harmful?</margin>
that so many heavenly minds from all nations spend their precious hours on an art so slippery and so rarely answering to one's hopes; that a study infamous for the shipwreck of so many family fortunes should be abandoned, and the ship steered toward safer waters. 

Nor am I one, listeners, who would advise the foolish, the rash, or the profane to rush into these sacred **Eleusinian mysteries** <note>The Eleusinian Mysteries were the most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece; here the author uses them as a metaphor for the deep secrets of alchemy.</note> with "unwashed hands" <note>original: "illotis manibus," a Latin idiom for approaching a task without proper preparation or reverence.</note>, as the saying goes—only to eventually atone for their rashness with bitter regret. While foresight is useful in other arts, in this one it is absolutely necessary. Nor am I one who has ever held out, or ever will hold out, the riches of **Croesus** <note>A king of Lydia famed for his legendary wealth; here used to represent the promise of making gold.</note> to the lovers of this Art, 
<margin>The true goal of Chemistry.</margin>
for I am certain that Divine Chemistry, especially **iatrochemistry** <note>original: "iatricæ"; the branch of chemistry focused on medicine and healing.</note> (which is my own profession), has a different purpose: namely, to investigate the secrets of Nature of every kind, to scrutinize the innermost parts of the Elements, and to bring forth the hidden properties of plants...

<summary>The author highlights the contributions of Danish scholars like Tycho Brahe and Peder Sørensen to chemistry before defending the discipline as a medical and scientific pursuit rather than a get-rich-quick scheme for gold-seekers.</summary>

<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Peder Sørensen, Pierre Des Noyers, iatrochemistry, Paracelsianism, Francis Bacon, William Davisson, medical chemistry, Denmark</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of the limits and proper goals of chemistry. Having listed great practitioners on the previous page, he now pivots to a philosophical warning: while chemistry is a divine tool for understanding nature and medicine, the pursuit of gold-making (transmutation) is a dangerous "sea" where few succeed and many lose their minds.</meta>

<header>ON THE ORIGIN AND [PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY]</header>
<page-num>146</page-num>

<margin>A wise moderation must be maintained in Chemical matters.</margin>
...into the light, so that he may recognize the textures of animals, go beneath the dark places of the earth, temper more pleasing and subtle medicines, and inspect and turn over all things, to admire, praise, and extol the Creator in His works. These shall be the perpetual works of our Chemistry; these are our **Pillars of Hercules** <note>In antiquity, the Pillars of Hercules marked the edge of the known world; the author uses them here to define the "safe" boundaries of chemical study.</note>, beyond which it is determined neither to stretch nor even to look forward. Let him who wishes love to be tossed by the dangerous **Acroceraunian rocks** <note>A coastal mountain range in Greece notorious for shipwrecks; a metaphor for the perils of alchemical obsession.</note>; **it is not for every man to visit Corinth** <note>original: "non omnium est adire Corinthum." A classical proverb meaning that not everyone has the luck or wealth to achieve a difficult goal—here, the "Great Work" of making gold.</note>. Let the **Elector of Cologne** <note>Likely Maximilian Henry of Bavaria (1621–1688), a prince-bishop known for his deep personal interest and investment in alchemy.</note>, **Helvetius** at The Hague <note>Johann Friedrich Schweitzer, known as Helvetius (1630–1709), who famously claimed to have received a small amount of the Philosopher's Stone from a stranger in 1666.</note>, and **Blondel** at Aachen <note>François Blondel (1613–1703), a physician who wrote on the medicinal waters of Aachen and chemical remedies.</note> see to it this year; they have seen certain "sons of the Gods" carried beyond those whirlpools of Charybdis. This was also witnessed by me once in Amsterdam, shown by a certain **Gottfried von Gerstorff**, a nobleman from Prague. However, because indulgent fortune barely picks out one from a thousand, and the fates forbid higher things, we must hold to safe harbors, nor should our sails be abandoned to the mockery of the winds without a pilot. Nevertheless, this never-exhausted science suggests enough secrets with a full hand so that one need not aspire to the **throne of Jove** <note>A metaphor for the ultimate power of transmutation or the pursuit of divine secrets beyond human station.</note>. 

Above all, faith should not be rashly placed in chemical books unless the authors' experience is known to a hair's breadth. Many, after spending immense labors in seeking in vain, prepare to show others a way most unknown to themselves by poorly stuffing books together. They do this with the intent of drawing many others into their own failure, lest they seem to have been insane alone. They are not unlike that Carthaginian, **Heliodorus**, buried at Cadiz, on whose sepulchral marble this was inscribed: 

**HELIODORUS. THE INSANE. CARTHAGINIAN. AT THE END. OF THE WORLD. BY THIS. TESTAMENTARY. SARCOPHAGUS. I ORDERED. MYSELF. TO BE BURIED. SO THAT. I MIGHT SEE. IF ANYONE. CRAZIER. THAN ME. WOULD COME. EVEN TO THESE.**

<meta>catchword: PLACES,</meta>

<summary>The author argues that chemistry should be used to study nature and medicine rather than chasing the "insanity" of gold-making. He warns that most alchemical books are written by failures who wish to lead others into the same ruin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's advice on how to study chemistry. Having previously warned against the "sea" of gold-making, he now evaluates specific authors and philosophical approaches, moving from the danger of "shipwreck" in confusing texts to the potential utility of modern Cartesian philosophy.</meta>

# THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.

->T 2<-

<meta>catchword: Cre-</meta>

<margin>Which books a student of Chemistry ought to read.</margin>
...PLACES, HE MIGHT PENETRATE. Indeed, it is considered a certain sweet and attractive thing <note>original: ἑλκυστικὸν (*helkystikon*). The author is likely alluding to the Roman poet Lucretius, who famously wrote that it is pleasant to watch a shipwreck from the shore—not because one delights in others' suffering, but because it is a relief to be free from such trouble oneself.</note> to see others struggling with oneself in a common shipwreck. One should judge in this way those overgrown volumes of **Maier** <note>Michael Maier (1568–1622), a German physician and counselor to Rudolf II, known for his complex alchemical allegories.</note>, **Fabre of Castelnaudary**, **Fioravanti**, **Penotus**, **Birelli**, and others. One should similarly judge the process in **Fernel’s** book *On the Hidden Causes of Things*, which he obtained as if it were a treasure from **Mollansius** who was mocking him—Fernel himself being ignorant of the more secret Chemistry, as **Blaise de Vigenère** explains in his work on *Philostratus*. Followers of metallic Chemistry <note>Alchemists specifically seeking the transmutation of metals.</note> should prudently abstain from such authors. Instead, they should cling closely and cautiously to the one **Zosimus**, the one **Villanova**, the one **Lullius**, and the one **Geber**, as to indubitable Masters. For the student of Medical Chemistry, no book is so much to be feared if it is read with judgment, since no writing has ever come forth so meager that it might not be of use in some part.

<margin>Descartes will be not without use to the Chemist.</margin>
Nor does that subtle **Philosophy of Descartes** <term>Cartesius: the Latinized name of René Descartes (1596–1650). While known as a mathematician and philosopher, his "mechanical philosophy" sought to explain chemical changes through the size, shape, and motion of tiny particles.</term> do nothing toward penetrating the mysteries of Chemistry; it bids one to take counsel concerning all things, even the smallest particles, acting as a whetstone for chemical wits and a path open to more sublime things. Although it must not be denied that, while **Descartes** was dominant in mathematical demonstrations, in metallic matters he was defeated by the diversity of phenomena <note>original: φαινομένων (*phainomenōn*), the observable appearances or "facts" of nature.</note> and he hesitated. He seriously panted for experiments by which his subtle genius might be directed in his speculation—this is quite contrary to the character of many modern Cartesians, for whom nothing today is more familiar than the claim that they see everything *a priori* <note>Reasoning from theoretical principles rather than from observation or experiment.</note> and have no need for experiments. Truly, the subtle Master spoke more modestly in the *Principles of Philosophy, part IV*: *“And thus,”* he says, *“we have here three things, which can be taken for the three common principles of the Chemists—salt, sulfur, and Mercury—by taking, namely, an acrid juice for salt, very soft little branches of oily matter for sulfur, and quicksilver itself for their Mercury.”*

<summary>The author critiques the confusing and voluminous literature of alchemy, recommending a return to classical masters for metallurgy while praising René Descartes' mechanical philosophy as a useful theoretical tool for chemists, provided it is supported by experimentation.</summary>

<keywords>bibliography, Michael Maier, Jean Fernel, Zosimos of Panopolis, Arnald of Villanova, Ramon Llull, Geber, René Descartes, mechanical philosophy, Tria Prima, salt-sulfur-mercury, experimentation vs theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of various chemical and philosophical systems. Following a discussion on which authors a student should trust, the author now transitions to evaluating René Descartes’ mechanical explanations for the formation of metals and the nature of chemical elements.</meta>

# OF THE ORIGIN AND

<header>OF THE ORIGIN AND</header>
<meta>catchword: cre-</meta>

<margin>Descartes' judgment on the Elements of Chemistry.</margin>
He believed <note>Referring to René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher who attempted to explain all physical phenomena through the movement of particles.</note> that all metals are able to reach us only because acrid juices <note>original: *acres succi*; these are acidic or corrosive fluids acting as solvents.</note> flowing through the passages of "Body C" <note>In Descartes' *Principles of Philosophy*, he used letters to designate various layers of the Earth; "Body C" represents the inner crust where minerals form.</note> detach certain of its particles from others. These particles, being then enveloped and clothed in an oily substance, are easily carried upward by quicksilver <note>original: *argentum vivum* (mercury).</note> that has been thinned by heat; depending on their various sizes and shapes, they constitute different metals. Perhaps I would have described these individually in this place if it had been possible, until now, to perform the various experiments required for a certain knowledge of them. Acknowledge here, Listeners <note>original: *Audit.* (Auditores). This indicates the text may have originated as a lecture.</note>, that a more exact knowledge of minerals is not an "ephemeral bud" <note>original: *germen ephemeron*; a metaphorical way of saying it is not something that grows overnight.</note> but requires the experiments of centuries. Descartes, without the toil of manual labor, explained the vortices of the heavens, the disordered movements of comets, the spots on the sun, and the wonders of the magnet; but here, he timidly suggests "it can be assumed" and "it can be believed." The modesty of the man is to be praised! For he did not dare to write "they must be assumed," nor "it ought to be believed," as he foresaw in his mind difficulties that were almost inescapable. 

For, to say nothing of other matters, how does the quicksilver of the common people deserve to be admitted as a "principle" <note>In chemistry, a "principle" or "element" is a fundamental building block of matter.</note> when it is lacking in plants and animals? We do not deny that it is sometimes found in the blood of men, but only those whose bodies, afflicted by the Venusian disease <note>original: *lue Venerea*; a historical term for syphilis.</note>, have been tormented by Mercurial ointment. We acknowledge it is sometimes found at the roots of grasses in the meadows of Poland, and in the Sangone Valley <note>original: *valle Sangii*.</note> near Turin <note>original: *Taurinorum Augustam*.</note> accompanying the roots of leopard's bane <note>original: *doronici*; a yellow-flowered plant of the genus *Doronicum*.</note>, but it was brought there from elsewhere; it is not innate to the plants. The "oily substance" of very soft branches <note>Descartes theorized that matter was composed of particles shaped like "branches" or "hooks" that latched together.</note> will with difficulty be demonstrated in gold, silver, or diamond. I would not deny that "fixed sulfur" is in them, but anyone will show with great difficulty that it consists of oily branches—as common sulfur is thought to consist. Finally, that all metals are likewise belched forth from the lower crust of the earth 

<summary>The author critiques René Descartes' mechanical theory of metallic formation, arguing that chemical knowledge requires centuries of empirical experimentation rather than just theoretical models. He specifically challenges the idea that mercury (quicksilver) and "oily" particles are the universal foundations of all substances, noting they are absent in most living things.</summary>

<keywords>Descartes, alchemy, mineralogy, quicksilver, mercury, syphilis treatment, Doronicum, chemical principles, Sangone Valley, sulfur</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion of the formation of metals, transitioning from René Descartes’ mechanical theories to the author's own observations about minerals found on the Earth's surface and a specific experimental account provided by Sir Kenelm Digby.</meta>

# THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

<header>THE PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.</header>

...hard to believe, both because iron ore, called "swamp ore" <note>original: *paludosa*; also known as bog iron, which forms in wetlands through chemical and biological oxidation.</note>, shows its origins in the very uppermost crust of the earth; and because few sands are found under the open sky that do not carry some trace of metal; and finally because globular pyrites, or "mammillary" marcasites <note>original: *marchasitae mammosae*; these are mineral formations, likely iron sulfide, that grow in rounded, breast-like shapes.</note> heavy with copper and iron, are often found in great abundance out in the open in fields—as in the Nivernais region of France—without a "mother-rock" <note>original: *matrice*; the surrounding rock in which a mineral is embedded.</note> or a mine. Regarding these, Paracelsus <note>The famous Swiss-German physician and alchemist (1493–1541) who revolutionized the use of chemicals in medicine.</note> wisely said:
> *The sky, he said, is their mine.* <note>original: "Cœlum illorum fodina eſt." This suggests that minerals can be formed by "celestial" or atmospheric influences rather than just deep underground.</note>

While I weigh these words more carefully, there comes to memory an experiment of the most illustrious Knight Kenelm Digby <note>Sir Kenelm Digby (1603–1665) was a prominent English courtier, diplomat, and natural philosopher known for his "Powder of Sympathy."</note>, Chancellor to the Queen of Britain, which he himself most kindly shared with me in London:

> *I was living,* he said, *in Paris, during the Cromwellian era <note>Referring to the mid-17th century when Oliver Cromwell ruled England and many Royalists, like Digby, lived in exile in France.</note> when my homeland was being lashed by civil strife. To escape those sad memories, I frequently devoted myself to chemical studies, when a certain curious person began to whisper wonderful things into my ears concerning the metallic virtues of the earth of Arcueil <note>A town just south of Paris, famous for its stone quarries and aqueducts.</note>. In short? I was finally seized by the urge and traveled to Arcueil [which is one milestone <note>About 4-5 kilometers.</note> from the city of Paris]. I ordered about twenty pounds of that reddish earth to be dug up and brought back to the city with me. The next day, I divided this earth into twelve equal parts and placed them in twelve large earthenware vessels. I committed them to the open air on the terrace of my house, with a wooden cover suspended loosely over the vessels to protect them from rain and accidental winds. I ordered the material to be sprinkled just a little bit with rainwater two or three times every day. Finally, when a month had passed, I boiled the clay taken from the first vessel in a larger amount of rainwater, intensifying the fire more strongly. I reduced the filtered water through slow evaporation into a thick residue, and at the bottom I clearly detected the beginnings of true vitriol <note>original: *vitrioli*; iron or copper sulfate crystals, highly prized for chemical and industrial uses.</note>—its color, taste, and crystal angles were evident. I continued the watering of the remaining vessels, and at the end of the second month, in the second vessel, I [continued] the work...*

<sig>T 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...the work [would be/continued]</meta>

<summary>The author argues that minerals can form at the Earth's surface from atmospheric influences, citing Paracelsus and a detailed experiment by Sir Kenelm Digby. Digby describes producing "vitriol" (mineral salts) from common Arcueil clay by exposing it to air and rainwater over several months.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the experimental account provided by Sir Kenelm Digby regarding the atmospheric formation of metals and transitions into the author's passionate defense of experimental science over abstract theory, citing the historical lineage of "the sacred art" of chemistry.</meta>

# THE ORIGIN AND PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY

<header>THE ORIGIN AND PROGRESS OF CHEMISTRY.</header>

> *...seeking back what was formed, I find **true sulfur** <note>original: "sulphur germanum"; in this context, "germanum" means authentic or genuine rather than German.</note>. In a word, in the third month **lead** offers itself to me; in the fourth, **tin**; in the fifth, **iron**; and in the sixth, **bronze** <note>original: "æs"; can refer to copper, bronze, or brass.</note>, in which were several thin scrapings of **silver**. A sudden storm arriving scattered the remaining six small vessels and prevented me from seeing the end of the experiments. When I later arrived in Paris, the venerable old gentleman, Monsieur de l'Oberie, and Monsieur Locques, the **Royal Spagyrist** <note>original: "Spagyricus Regius"; a "spagyrist" was an alchemist specializing in the separation and recombination of natural elements for medicinal use.</note>, testified that they had attempted the same things and found the same results, except that they varied slightly in the order of the extraction.*

You see, I think, my Listeners, what the air provides and what the influence of the heavens achieves, so that it is not so necessary to descend into those ingeniously fashioned "shells" <note>A critique of René Descartes’ theory of the Earth's formation, which imagined the planet composed of distinct, mechanical layers or crusts.</note> of the earth. But let us turn to experiments—which, even in the judgment of **Descartes** himself, are the most important part of this matter <note>original: "utramque hic paginam faciunt"; literally "make up both sides of the page," an idiom meaning they are of paramount importance.</note>—but only those performed not by just anyone, but by the talents of those with trained senses, by whom nothing is rashly believed and nothing is rashly rejected.

<margin>Experiments reign in Chemistry.</margin>
Therefore, the cultivators of Nature must be challenged to perform experiments more frequently than is commonly done. Without an apparatus of experiments, nothing should be easily admitted, because without them Physics, Medicine, Chemistry, and the other arts are mute and without glory. Therefore, Listeners, let experiments revive in the Academies and in the other arenas of the wise, so that noble Chemistry—that highest delight of Nature and of more elevated minds—may flourish again in our schools <note>original: "Lycéis"; referring to the Lyceum or institutions of higher learning.</note> to the benefit of all, especially those who heal. Let us not allow this **sacred art**—which was propagated by **Tubal-Cain** <note>A biblical figure (Genesis 4:22) traditionally regarded as the first blacksmith and metallurgist.</note> into the house of **Ham** <note>original: "Chami"; Noah's son. Alchemical tradition often linked the word "Chemistry" to "Khem" (Egypt), the land of Ham.</note>, proceeded from the descendants of Ham into **Egypt**, from Egypt into **Greece**, and then into **Rome** <note>original: "Latium".</note>, from there to the **Arabs** and the **Chinese**, and finally circling back through **Spain**, **France**, and indeed all of **Europe**—to grow numb through the laziness and lethargy of the age in our **North** <note>original: "Aquilone"; literally the North Wind, representing Northern Europe.</note> as well.

->**I HAVE SPOKEN,**<-
